# Decisions

Append-only. One dated line per decision a future session would otherwise have to re-derive.
Never edit or delete an entry: if a decision is reversed, add a new line saying so.

- 2026-08-29 Port target is mrab-regex at `1760a20647f1c2ddcc025128407fe6f7edb905a1` (release 2026.8.12), pinned as the `upstream/` submodule.
- 2026-08-29 SDK pinned to 10.0.400 with `rollForward: latestPatch` in `global.json`; `test.runner` set to Microsoft.Testing.Platform, which TUnit requires on the .NET 10 SDK.
- 2026-08-29 Analyzer set copied from an earlier internal monorepo (Meziantou, SonarAnalyzer, SharpSource, ErrorProne.NET) with warnings as errors, but **not** its `CommunityToolkit.Diagnostics` reference: the shipped package must carry zero runtime dependencies.
- 2026-08-29 Analyzer rules that a faithful port must violate (method length, complexity, nesting, magic numbers, commented-out upstream code, `NotImplementedException`) are relaxed in `.editorconfig` for `src/FuzzyRegex/Parsing` and `Engine` **only**. Correctness and security rules stay as errors everywhere. Rationale: upstream's structure must be preserved for syncs to stay mechanical, and autonomous slice sessions should spend allowance porting, not restructuring to satisfy a style rule.
- 2026-08-29 Test identity for the parity ratchet is `className + "." + testName` from the TRX report; the baseline stores the sorted **set** of passing ids rather than a count, so a slice's diff shows exactly which tests it enabled and a swap cannot hide behind an unchanged total.
- 2026-08-29 Feature area for `docs/STATUS.md` is derived from the test namespace (`Fuzzy.Text.RegularExpressions.Tests.Ported.<Area>`), and the capability a skipped test waits on is parsed from its `[Skip("needs:...")]` reason. Both conventions are enforced by a test (`PortedTestConventionsTests`), because a silent mis-parse would report fake progress.
- 2026-08-29 Budget gate: validated that **no local source reports live plan utilisation**. `~/.claude/stats-cache.json` lags by weeks and has no plan denominator; a `rateLimit` record appears only after a request has already been rejected (429); the claude.ai usage page needs an interactive browser, which an unattended `claude -p` driver has not got. The gate is therefore the deterministic slice caps (spec section 9's sanctioned fallback), plus rolling 24h/7d token windows computed from `~/.claude/projects/**/*.jsonl`, plus a back-off while a recorded rate-limit reset is still in the future.
- 2026-08-29 Budget token metric is the plain sum of all four token types, cache reads included, counted **globally** across every Claude Code session on the machine. Measured that day: about 2.9e9 tokens/day and 1.98e10/week.
- 2026-08-29 Token caps are a **circuit breaker, not a rationing mechanism**; the slice caps do the rationing. Setting the token caps at half of typical usage was tried and rejected: a dry run of the driver refused the very first slice, because an ordinary working day passes that mark by the afternoon. They now sit at roughly 2x the measured daily figure so they only trip on a runaway.
- 2026-08-29 `check-ratchet.ps1 -AcceptRemovals` is the only sanctioned way to retire a baselined test. A rename and a lost test look identical from outside, so removals go red by default; accepting them lists each one. Hand-editing `tests/parity-baseline.json` is forbidden because it would let real coverage loss through unnoticed.
- 2026-08-29 Skip reasons name a **capability** (`needs:lookbehind`), not a slice id, and Phase 1 opens with an API surface stub (slice S01). Both resolve contradictions in the design spec as originally written; the spec is amended inline and the changes are listed in its section 15.
- 2026-08-29 Per-slice cost is measured from `claude -p --output-format json`, which returns `usage`, `total_cost_usd`, `is_error` and `permission_denials` - more reliable than scraping session logs.
- 2026-08-29 The driver passes a scoped tool allowlist (Read/Write/Edit/Glob/Grep/TodoWrite/Skill/Task/Agent and Bash limited to dotnet, git, pwsh and python) with `--permission-mode acceptEdits`. No blanket permission bypass: an unattended session must not be able to run arbitrary commands on the machine.
- 2026-08-29 Python oracle runs `regex` **2026.7.19** from PyPI locally; the pinned submodule commit is tagged 2026.8.12, which upstream never published. **The gap is immaterial and this is measured, not assumed**: `git diff 0525aff..1760a20` (the 2026.7.19 tag to the pin) touches only `pyproject.toml`, `changelog.txt`, `.github/workflows/main.yml` and the `__version__` string in `regex/_main.py`. `src/_regex.c`, `src/_regex_unicode.c`, `regex/_regex_core.py` and `regex/tests/test_regex.py` are byte-identical, so matching behaviour is the same. Re-run that diff before trusting this at the next sync.
- 2026-08-29 The oracle in CI is **built from `upstream/`**, not installed from PyPI, so the ground truth is exactly the pinned commit; the Linux runners already have a compiler, so it costs nothing. `oracle.yml` asserts the built version equals the version in `upstream/pyproject.toml` - a silently mismatched oracle would report version drift as port defects. Verified the assertion has teeth by running it against the local 2026.7.19: it fails.
- 2026-08-29 **No C compiler installed on the Windows dev box**, deliberately. There is no MSVC at all, so a local from-source build means 3-7 GB of VS Build Tools, and for the current pin it would buy nothing but a different version string. Install it at the first upstream sync where the pinned commit genuinely differs from a published release - `sync-upstream` says how and when.
- 2026-08-29 Pester 6.1.0 installed for the PowerShell tooling tests (only the Windows-bundled Pester 3.4.0 was present, whose syntax is incompatible).
- 2026-08-29 `docs/STATUS.md` carries no timestamp and is written with LF endings, and `.gitattributes` sets `eol=lf` repo-wide. CI regenerates the board and fails on any diff; a wall-clock stamp made that check fail on every run, and a CRLF/LF flip rewrote every line on the Linux and macOS legs.
- 2026-08-29 `docs/plan/slice-log.jsonl` is gitignored. The budget gate counts slices from it, and the failed-slice rollback runs `git clean -fd`, which would have deleted it and reset the driver's daily cap to zero. Verified that `git clean -fd` (no `-x`) leaves ignored files alone.
- 2026-08-29 The driver launches `claude` through `ProcessStartInfo.ArgumentList`, not `Start-Process -ArgumentList`. The latter joins arguments with spaces and quotes nothing, so `Bash(dotnet *)` reached the CLI as two tokens and the scoped allowlist - the whole safety boundary for an unattended session - silently did not apply.
- 2026-08-29 A failed slice rolls back to the head recorded before the session, not to `HEAD`. A session can commit its work and move its slice file to `done/` and still leave the ratchet red; resetting to `HEAD` kept that commit and stranded the slice where it would never be retried.
- 2026-08-29 The editorconfig section for the port relaxations is `[src/FuzzyRegex/{Parsing,Engine}/**.cs]`. The obvious `**/*.cs` form requires an intervening directory, so it matched nothing directly inside `Parsing/` or `Engine/` - exactly where the ported engine lives - and every relaxation was inert. Verified with a probe file before and after.
- 2026-08-29 Phase 0 closed with a blind review that found eight defects, all confirmed and fixed. Five were in the slice driver and the ratchet, none would have been caught by the tests as they stood, and each fix that had testable logic gained a test. Worth repeating per slice as the spec requires.
- 2026-08-29 The root namespace is `Fuzzy.Text.RegularExpressions`, not `FuzzyRegex` (design spec amendment 8). A type cannot be named after its containing namespace and stay reachable: with namespace `FuzzyRegex` and entry type `FuzzyRegex`, a consumer writing `using FuzzyRegex;` gets `CS0118: 'FuzzyRegex' is a namespace but is used like a type` on `new FuzzyRegex(...)` and CS0234 on `FuzzyRegex.IsMatch(...)`. Measured with a throwaway project against the built library, not reasoned about; MA0049 flagged it and the suppression was rejected in favour of the fix. Assembly, NuGet id and every directory keep the name `FuzzyRegex`; test and benchmark namespaces moved too, because `FuzzyRegex.Tests.*` declared the colliding namespace itself.
- 2026-08-29 `FuzzyRegex.Match` keeps the .NET meaning (search anywhere, upstream's `search`); upstream's anchored `match` is `MatchAtStart` and `fullmatch` is `FullMatch`. Naming upstream's `match` `Match` would have silently given a .NET caller anchored behaviour - a trap no compiler catches. Static conveniences take `(input, pattern, options)`, `Regex`'s argument order rather than upstream's.
- 2026-08-29 S01's surface is wider than its slice file listed, by owner approval: the listed members could not express `fullmatch` (71 uses in the upstream suite), `partial` (91), `overlapped` (15), `pos`/`endpos` (22/14), `subn` (17) or `expand` (8), and S01's whole purpose is to be a compilation target for S02-S05. Counting the real usage first turned "is the stub wide enough?" from an opinion into a number.
- 2026-08-29 `FuzzyRegexOptions` uses upstream's `RegexFlag` bit values (`_regex_core.py:73-90`) rather than `RegexOptions`'s, so the parser port compares against upstream's tables directly. Nothing casts between the two enums, so the numbers need not agree with the built-in engine's.
- 2026-08-29 The pre-commit hook (slice S01b) formats and does not run tests; the suite belongs on pre-push and in CI. `run-slices.ps1` commits at the end of every slice moments after `check-ratchet.ps1` has run the whole suite, so a test-running pre-commit hook would run it twice per slice for no new information - and mid-slice TDD commits are *meant* to have failing tests. A local hook is also one `--no-verify` away from irrelevant, so it cannot be the gate; CI and the ratchet are.
- 2026-08-29 .gitignore negates `*.trx` for tools/tests/fixtures/. The Pester fixture sample.trx is an input that PortTools.Tests.ps1 reads, not test output, and the blanket `*.trx` rule meant it had never been committed - so the CI "Tooling tests (Pester)" step had been running against a file that does not exist on a fresh clone since Phase 0. `TestResults/` already covers the real test output on its own line. Found while committing S01, whose fix to that fixture was otherwise uncommittable.
- 2026-08-29 Review discipline is an evidence gate, not a pass count (spec amendment 9). The old wording, "one pass, no loops", conflated a critique loop (reviewer opines, code changes, reviewer opines again - genuinely harmful, stays banned) with repair against execution feedback (a red ratchet or an oracle divergence - ground truth, and two rounds capture 76-95% of achievable improvement across seven models with no model regressing, arXiv:2604.10508). It also read as one pass per *slice* rather than per *unreviewed change*, which is how S01 shipped ~200 lines of public API no reviewer had seen. Reviewers now hand over a reproduction rather than prose, and are not asked for explanations or proposed corrections, which measurably raise misjudgement (arXiv:2603.00539).
- 2026-08-29 Every review finding is reproduced before it is acted on. An adversarial gate of this shape killed ~79% of 171 candidate findings before advancement (arXiv:2604.19049), and removing execution-grounded validation from an agentic repair pipeline increased unnecessary repairs by 131.7% (arXiv:2604.10800). Automated code review is imprecise enough to need this: four techniques benchmarked in arXiv:2509.01494 scored between 2.79% and 9.22% precision. In S01 both reviews were reproduced finding by finding before any code changed.
- 2026-08-29 The differential oracle moves from Phase 6 to the start of Phase 3 (spec amendment 10). Scheduling the third correctness leg after the entire engine left Phases 3-5 resting on the ported suite and the ratchet alone, and those cannot carry it: in the closest published analogue only 72% of LLM-transpiled functions were semantically equivalent despite compiling and passing the existing tests (56.3-92.1% by codebase), with differential testing against the reference catching the rest at 85.7-88.2% precision and 100% recall (arXiv:2510.07604). Infrastructure already exists from Phase 0, so this changes when the harness is written, not what has to be built. The dialect-divergence caveat that normally weakens cross-engine differential testing (arXiv:2603.00311) does not apply: we compare against the exact upstream commit we are a port of, not across dialects.
- 2026-08-29 There is no `.csharpierrc`; `.editorconfig` is the only formatting configuration. CSharpier 1.3.0 reads `indent_style`, `indent_size` and `max_line_length` from `.editorconfig` directly, so a second config file would only be a place for the two to drift apart - the exact failure S01b exists to prevent. Measured, not assumed: a 112-character line wraps under CSharpier's default `printWidth` of 100 and survives untouched once `max_line_length = 120` is present. `endOfLine` is left at `auto`, which preserves what is there, and `.gitattributes` already pins that to LF repo-wide.
- 2026-08-29 CSharpier formats `src/FuzzyRegex/Parsing` and `Engine` like everything else - no ignore entry. The concern was that reflowing a hand-port would break the by-eye mapping onto `_regex_core.py` and `_regex.c`. Probed against the shapes those ports will actually take (dispatch dictionaries, opcode-name tables, a `case`-per-opcode switch, an if/elif chain, quoted upstream reference comments): CSharpier never reorders a statement, never touches a comment, and left the switch alone. It only rewraps, and for a table that means one entry per line, which makes an upstream entry appearing or disappearing a one-line diff rather than a three-row reflow. Where a specific grid must survive verbatim, `// csharpier-ignore` above it holds - verified on an aligned numeric table.
- 2026-08-29 There is no `.csharpierignore`. CSharpier already skips `obj`, `node_modules`, `.git` and `*.g.cs` by default and honours `.gitignore`, which covers `bin/` and `TestResults/`; `upstream/` contains no C# at all. `csharpier check .` reports 20 files, which is exactly the tracked source set. Add the file when something needs excluding, not before.
- 2026-08-29 CSharpier 1.3.0 also formats MSBuild XML, which was not expected. Accepted rather than ignored: the whole diff across seven `.csproj`/`.props` files is the removal of blank lines just inside `<Project>`, and an ignore list for it would be more configuration than the thing it buys.
- 2026-08-29 The tool manifest lives at `.config/dotnet-tools.json`, though the .NET 10 SDK's `dotnet new tool-manifest` now writes `dotnet-tools.json` at the repository root. `dotnet tool restore` resolves both; the `.config/` location keeps the root uncluttered. Verified restore from `.config/` after moving it.
- 2026-08-29 The Husky hooks are plain shell, not `task-runner.json` tasks. Both needed shell anyway: pre-push is a single `pwsh` line, and pre-commit needs `git add` after formatting, which the task runner has no concept of. `task-runner.json` is committed with an empty task list only because `dotnet husky install` recreates it on a fresh clone if it is absent.
- 2026-08-29 The pre-commit hook refuses a commit when a staged `.cs` file also has unstaged edits. It formats staged files and re-stages them with `git add`, and `git add` on a partially staged file sweeps the unstaged work into the commit as well - content nobody chose to commit. Refusing is a two-line guard; the stash-and-reapply dance that would handle it properly is not worth it for a case the slice driver never hits. Verified both paths: a fully staged mis-formatted file is fixed and re-staged, and adding an unstaged edit on top makes the hook exit 1.
- 2026-08-29 PowerShell 7 is documented as a hard prerequisite, and the `pre-push` hook gets no fallback to Windows PowerShell 5.1. Found by S01b's review: the hook calls `pwsh`, which is absent from an out-of-the-box Windows 11. A fallback would be worse than the failure, because 5.1 cannot run this tooling at all - `powershell.exe -Command "Import-Module ./tools/PortTools.psm1"` dies parsing `PortTools.psm1:387`, which uses null-conditional and ternary syntax that only PowerShell 7 has. The requirement predates the hook and was simply never written down; the hook is only what made it bite.
- 2026-08-29 Thread safety is asserted for `FuzzyRegex` (spec section 4, and the class doc) but was never asserted either way for `Match`. `Match` is **not** thread-safe, matching `System.Text.RegularExpressions.Match`, because `Groups` will be populated lazily. The gate for the pattern object is structural, not a concurrency test: a passing race test proves nothing, so the phase 2 slice that adds the first instance field to `FuzzyRegex` must add a reflection test asserting every instance field is `initonly`, which fires the moment a cache field appears - the actual failure mode, along with holding an `ArrayPool` rental on the instance instead of per-call. A multi-threaded stress test comparing against single-threaded results is a smoke test alongside it, never the gate. Not written in S02: every member throws `NotImplementedException`, so the field test would be vacuously green.
- 2026-08-29 Replacement templates speak **upstream's** language, not `Regex`'s: `\1`, `\g<name>`, `\n`, `\x41` and `\N{...}` are escapes and `$` is ordinary text. The two cannot both be honoured, because they disagree about `\` - measured against both engines, not reasoned: for the template `\n`, `regex.sub('.', r'\n', 'x')` gives a newline where `Regex.Replace("x", ".", @"\n")` gives a backslash followed by `n`. Choosing upstream's ports ~55 assertions in S02 verbatim (`test_sub_template_numeric_escape`, `test_symbolic_refs`, `test_basic_regex_sub`, `test_expand`) where choosing `Regex`'s would have made ~40 of them "not ported: different template language" - a permanent parity hole in the one area a fuzzy-regex user is most likely to have upstream code to move across. The cost accepted is that a .NET caller writing `$1` gets the literal text `$1`, which is exactly what upstream does. S01's `Match.Result` doc comment claimed `$1`/`${name}` and is corrected.
- 2026-08-29 `FuzzyRegex.Split` returns `string?[]`, not `string[]`, and puts `null` where a capturing group did not take part in a match. Upstream puts `None` there; the built-in `Regex.Split` instead omits the entry entirely, which both shortens the array and loses the difference between a group that matched empty and one that never ran. Verified against both engines: `regex.split('(x)|(1)', 'a1b')` is `['a', None, '1', 'b']` where `Regex.Split("a1b", "(x)|(1)")` is `["a", "1", "b"]`. Keeping upstream's shape ports all 8 affected assertions in `test_re_split` and `test_qualified_re_split` with identical positions and lengths.
- 2026-08-29 A `needs:<tag>` names the capability whose absence would make the test fail *and* that is least likely to land first; where a test needs two, the skip prose must name the combination, and **a slice must read the prose before un-skipping by tag alone**. Found in S02: five `Split` tests using `(?r)` and three using `\b`/`\m`/`\M` were tagged `needs:splitting`, so the slice delivering `Split` would have un-skipped tests that cannot pass without right-to-left search or word-boundary anchors. Retagged `right-to-left` and `anchors`. Two-capability tests were *not* retagged where both capabilities already have their own tests on the board (case-insensitive backreferences stay `ignore-case`; `\g<name>` in a template stays `substitution`), because nothing is hidden there - whereas `right-to-left` had no other test anywhere, so the mis-tag made the capability invisible to the status board and no slice would ever have been prompted to deliver it.
- 2026-08-29 A ported test's `#n` provenance counts **every** `self.assert` line in the upstream method, in source order, **including the branches of a `sys.version_info` guard this port does not use**. S02 set that convention implicitly (`test_qualified_re_split#4` is the `>= 3.7` branch, #5 the unused one); two of S03's three porting agents instead numbered only the taken branch, which silently shifted every later number in `test_zerowidth`, `test_unmatched_in_sub` and `test_bug_10328`. Renumbered, and the rule is now stated in each affected file's `<remarks>`. A provenance number that does not name the same upstream line every time is worse than none, because it is trusted.
- 2026-08-29 A `(?V0)`/`(?V1)`/`(?rV1)` pattern is ported as its **own** test and never folded into its unflagged sibling, even when upstream asserts the identical expected value, and it is tagged `needs:version-flags`. Upstream writes both because the parser has to accept the flag; folding them keeps the assertion and throws away the only thing being tested. Found in S03 review of the first agent's output, which had dropped all 13 version-flagged patterns in `test_search_reverse` and `test_zerowidth` as "incidental". This is what put `version-flags` (11 tests) on the status board - S02 had reserved the tag and left it unused, so before this no slice would ever have been prompted to deliver `(?V0)`/`(?V1)`.
- 2026-08-29 **Upstream test lines 1008-1740 contain no character above U+FFFF** - no `\U` escape, no astral literal, no surrogate escape; the only non-ASCII data is U+0300/U+0301 combining marks, U+00E0/U+00E1/U+00E9/U+00BD, U+0393/U+03B3, U+0410 and the Devanagari of `test_word_class`. Measured by scanning the range for code points > 0xFFFF and resolving every `\N{...}` name and `\uXXXX` escape, not assumed. So every Python codepoint index in S03 equals its UTF-16 index, the slice's headline risk did not arise, and its `Gaps/Surrogates` done-criterion ("one gap test per distinct surrogate situation encountered") is discharged as vacuous rather than skipped. The first real surrogate data is expected in S04/S05.
- 2026-08-29 Non-ASCII characters may be written as literal UTF-8 in a test source where a `const` is required (an `[Arguments]` value cannot be built from `(char)` casts, and C# `\uXXXX` escapes cannot be authored reliably through the agent tool path - they decode before reaching disk). Proven safe rather than assumed: no repo source carries a BOM, so the question was whether Roslyn reads BOM-less UTF-8 correctly on Windows. Read the `[Arguments]` values back out of the built `FuzzyRegex.Tests.dll` by reflection - all 18 rows of `PosixAndPropertyClassTableTests` round-trip as U+0393, U+03B3 and U+00E1, matching the Python oracle row for row. Elsewhere `(char)0xNNNN` casts or named constants stay preferred, because precomposed and decomposed forms are indistinguishable by eye.
- 2026-08-29 Oracle invocations set `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1` and `PYTHONIOENCODING=utf-8`. Without the first, running Python against `upstream/` leaves a `__pycache__` directory that makes the submodule show as dirty in the parent repo, which a slice could commit as a submodule pointer change; without the second, the Windows console codepage cannot print most of what these tests check (`UnicodeEncodeError: 'charmap' codec can't encode characters`). Both measured: with no env var `upstream/regex/__pycache__` appears on the first import and with it set it does not. Recorded in the `port-tests` skill.
- 2026-08-30 S04 widened the public stub with named lists, by owner approval: a `namedLists` parameter (`IReadOnlyDictionary<string, IReadOnlyCollection<string>>`) on the `FuzzyRegex` constructor and on the static `Match`, `MatchAtStart`, `FullMatch` and `Matches`, plus the `NamedLists` property S01 had deferred. Without it 41 assertions across `test_named_lists`, `test_fuzzy` and `test_partial` could not be *written*, let alone skipped: upstream passes named lists as Python keyword arguments (`regex.match(r"333\L<bar>444", s, bar=["one","two"])`) and no existing parameter could carry them, so the tests would not compile. This is the same argument that widened S01, and the same shape of evidence - count the affected assertions first. It is not sugar for an alternation: the oracle shows `\L<name>` composing with `(?fi)` full case-folding and with `{e<=1}` fuzzy budgets, so rewriting it as `(?:one|two)` would test a different code path. The widening added public API inside a test-porting slice, so the closing review covered `src/FuzzyRegex/FuzzyRegex.cs` explicitly.
- 2026-08-30 A `#n` provenance for `test_various` counts **table rows**, not `self.assert` lines. The method is a 524-row data table driven by a single loop holding one `assertEqual`, one `assertRaisesRegex` and one `fail`, so the normal convention would give every one of the 524 ported tests the same index and destroy the traceability it exists for. Rows are numbered from 1 in source order and the range is written compressed (`#21,24,30,35-37`). Every file generated for the method states the exception in its `<remarks>`, because a provenance number that means different things in different files is worse than none.
- 2026-08-30 A commented-out `#self.assert...` line **is** counted in provenance numbering (`test_recursive#29`, upstream line 2869). The numbering rule has to be mechanically reproducible by a future session, and "every line matching `self.assert`" is; "every line matching `self.assert` that is not commented out, and not inside a string, and ..." is a judgement call that two sessions will make differently. The index is counted and left unported, recorded in `docs/PORTMAP.md`.
- 2026-08-30 **Upstream test lines 1741-3083 contain no character above U+FFFF** - no astral literal, no `\U` escape. Measured by scanning the range for code points > 0xFFFF and for `\U000` escapes, as S03 did for its own range, before any index was ported. So every Python codepoint index in S04 equals its UTF-16 index and no span needed recomputing. The non-ASCII data that is there is BMP and confusable by eye - U+00DF (ß), U+0130 (İ), U+0131 (ı), U+FB06 (ﬆ) - so those are written as `(char)0xNNNN` casts rather than literal UTF-8, and the Turkic table's 16 rows were read back out of the compiled `FuzzyRegex.Tests.dll` by reflection and compared code point by code point against the oracle. **The prediction in S03's closing notes that S04 would bring the first real surrogate data was wrong**; the first non-BMP data, if any, is in S05.
- 2026-08-30 The fuzzy tests carry nine tags - `fuzzy-syntax`, `fuzzy-budget`, `fuzzy-bestmatch`, `fuzzy-enhancematch`, `fuzzy-insertion`, `fuzzy-substitution`, `fuzzy-deletion`, `fuzzy-counts`, `fuzzy-changes` - alongside the existing `fuzzy-matching`. The S04 slice file had suggested `fuzzy-charset`, which is unused because nothing in `test_fuzzy` puts an error budget on a character class, and had not anticipated `fuzzy-syntax`, `fuzzy-counts` or `fuzzy-changes`, which are three things that land separately from matching (constraint parsing, the reported edit distance, the reported edit positions). Where a `(?b)` or `(?e)` flag is present it decides the tag, so each flagged assertion sits beside a note naming its unflagged sibling's index and span. Which of insertion, substitution or deletion a subject needs was **measured** from `fuzzy_counts` against the oracle, not inferred from the subject's shape.
- 2026-08-30 The ported-test area for `test_partial` is `PartialMatching`, not `Partial`. CA1716 rejects a namespace segment that is a reserved language keyword (`partial`), and the analyzer is not disabled to get a build. The area name is what `docs/STATUS.md` prints, so the row reads `PartialMatching`.
- 2026-08-30 `test_named_lists` #15-16 are ported under the `CaseFolding` area, not `NamedLists`. They sit inside that upstream method but use no named list at all - they are `(?fi)\b(\w+) +\1\b` full-case backreference assertions - and the area name is the row heading on the generated status board, so filing them under `NamedLists` would misreport what has coverage. The provenance still names `test_named_lists`, and both files cross-reference each other.
- 2026-08-30 `test_various`'s 46 invalid-pattern rows are one test method, not one per upstream error constant. Twelve methods with identical bodies trip Sonar S4144, and the port does not assert error message text anyway (the convention set by `Ported/CharacterClasses/BadSetTests.cs` in S02), so the twelve constants and the rows belonging to each are recorded in the class `<remarks>` instead. All 46 were confirmed against the oracle to raise `regex.error`.
- 2026-08-30 **Upstream test lines 3084-4540 contain exactly two non-BMP sites**, the first in the whole port: `test_hg_bugs` #373 (the ZWJ-joined family emoji, U+1F468/U+1F469/U+1F467/U+1F466, upstream line 4161) and #433-434 (U+1F63A SMILING CAT FACE WITH OPEN MOUTH, line 4305). S03 predicted the first surrogate data would appear in S04 and S04 predicted S05; S04 was right. **The measurement method S03 and S04 used is not sufficient on its own and is corrected here**: scanning for literal astral characters and `\U` escapes finds #373 but misses #433-434 entirely, because upstream writes that one as the *name* escape `\N{SMILING CAT FACE WITH OPEN MOUTH}`. Every future range scan must resolve `\N{...}` names through `unicodedata.lookup` as well. The miss was caught by a porting agent reading its own assertion, not by the scan, which is luck and not a process.
- 2026-08-30 Where upstream passes `flags=regex.A` or `flags=regex.U`, the port writes a **leading inline `(?a)` / `(?u)` in the pattern** instead, because `FuzzyRegexOptions` has no ASCII or UNICODE member (PORTMAP records both as not surfaced). Proven equivalent against the local oracle on 2026-08-30 for every one of the 28 affected assertions rather than assumed, e.g. `regex.findall(r'(?u:\d)', '9\uFF19', flags=regex.A)` and `regex.findall(r'(?a)(?u:\d)', '9\uFF19')` both give `['9', '\uFF19']`, and all nine `\p{L}`-count rows of Git issue 575 match their inline form. This matters because Git issues 467, 572 and 575 exist *to test* how a scoped `(?a:...)` interacts with the global flag: rewriting the scoped part would have destroyed the thing under test, whereas moving only the global flag into a leading inline flag preserves it.
- 2026-08-30 `test_hg_bugs` #58 is ported as a plain `Replace` with no options, not as a WORD-flag test. Upstream writes `regex.sub(r'(\w+)', r'[\1]', subject, regex.WORD)`, and the fourth positional parameter of `regex.sub` is **`count`, not `flags`** - so `regex.WORD` (0x800) is used as a replacement count and the WORD flag is never applied. Measured on 2026-08-30: the call as written, the call with no fourth argument, and the call with `flags=regex.WORD` all return the identical string. Recorded because the assertion reads like a WORD test and a future session would otherwise 'fix' it by adding a flag the port does not have and upstream never exercised here.
- 2026-08-30 `test_hg_bugs` is filed under a new `Regressions` area, split across 29 files by the capability each assertion waits on (`RegressionsFuzzyTests`, `RegressionsPartialTests`, and so on), mirroring how S04 split `test_various`. One file per upstream method would have been a single 475-assertion class, and one file per Hg issue would have been 139 files; splitting by capability means the file a future slice opens is the file whose tests that slice un-skips. The upstream bug identifier ('Hg issue 38', 'Git issue 415') is kept in a comment above each test, so the grouping never costs traceability back to the original report.
- 2026-08-30 S05 coins five capability tags: `backtracking-verbs` ((*SKIP)/(*PRUNE)/(*FAIL), 34 tests), `define-groups` ((?(DEFINE)...), 16), `ascii-flag` (28), `posix-matching` ((?p) leftmost-longest, 8) and `keep-marker` (`\K`, 6). The 49-tag vocabulary was checked first and none of the five had an existing home: `posix` looked available but the three hits were the conventions test's own fixture data (`needs:posix0`), not a real tag, so grepping `docs/STATUS.md` alone would have been misleading. Each names a construct that lands as one piece of engine work, which is what makes it useful to a slice author.
- 2026-08-30 `Match.allcaptures`, `Match.allspans`, `Match.groupdict` and `Match.capturesdict` are **not** added to the public surface; the eleven assertions that use them are ported through `Groups` and `Group.Captures`, which already carry the same information. This follows S02's rule that a test-porting slice must not widen the public API, and unlike S04's named lists the tests compile and read fine without the addition - the S04 widening was justified by 41 assertions that could not otherwise be *written*, which is not the case here. Phase 2 can add them for their own sake if the shape is wanted.
- 2026-08-30 `test_hg_bugs` #74-81 are eight upstream assertions but one C# test body, written as a single `[Arguments]`-driven test carrying the upstream index as a row value. `Matches` is the one C# call behind both `finditer` and `findall`, and upstream's literal-versus-variable repeat is a Python object-identity concern with no C# counterpart, so all eight collapse to the same body - which trips Sonar S4144 (identical method implementations), and the analyzer is not disabled to get a build. Written as eight `[Arguments]` rows rather than folded into two tests so the status board still counts eight, matching upstream's assertion count. The index has to be a row value because eight identical rows would not be distinguishable from one another.
- 2026-08-30 U+0085 NEL, U+2028 LINE SEPARATOR and U+2029 PARAGRAPH SEPARATOR must be written as `\uXXXX` escapes in C# source, never as literal characters, even inside a string literal. C# treats all three as **source line terminators**, so a literal one ends the line mid-string: `error CS1010: Newline in constant`. Hit in `RegressionsUnicodePropertyTests` porting Git issue 477's `\p{VertSpace}` subject, which contains all three. This is a different failure from the confusable-character problem DECISIONS already records - those compile and read wrong, these do not compile at all.
- 2026-08-30 A `\uXXXX` escape written through the agent Edit/Write tool path is decoded to the character before it reaches disk, confirmed again in S05 (an edit writing `"\u200D"` landed as a literal ZWJ, checked with `cat -A`). The reliable route for escape text is a short Python script that writes the file, and the reliable check is `cat -A`, not the Read tool, which renders `\\X` and `\X` identically. S03 recorded the decoding hazard; what is new is that **`cat -A` is the only cheap way to see the difference**, and that `char.ConvertFromUtf32(0x1F63A)` sidesteps it entirely for astral characters.
- 2026-08-30 Porting agents running in parallel must be told **not to build the whole test project and not to touch a file they did not create**. Nine agents shared one working tree in S05; several ran `dotnet build`, saw compile errors from other agents' half-written files, and one renamed eight of its siblings' files to `*.bak` to get a clean build - removing finished work from the build. No work was lost (it was restored on instruction and verified against `git status`), but the shared tree is the hazard, not the agent. The brief now says the coordinator fixes the build centrally after everyone finishes, which is also what STATE.md already recommended for a different reason: a whole-project build in one place caught every analyzer failure that nine isolated builds did not.
- 2026-08-30 **A Python `span()` is `(start, end)`; our `Capture` exposes `(Index, Length)`. Converting one to the other by copying the pair is wrong for every capture that does not start at 0**, and it is invisible in review because the common cases (a match at index 0, a single-character match at index 0) coincide. S05's blind review caught two: `test_hg_bugs` #435-436 asserted captures of `(.)+` over `"abc"` as `(0,1),(1,2),(2,3)` where the lengths are all 1, and #60 asserted `(1,2),(0,2)` for `spans("x")` where the inner group captures one character at index 1. Both would have demanded wrong behaviour from a correct engine. The rest of the slice writes `(m.Index, m.Index + m.Length)` when it wants a span, which is the form to prefer, and the sweep for the `(Index, Length)` form found only these two genuine cases out of four sites. Phase 3 onward will meet this constantly - every upstream `span`, `spans`, `allspans` and `regs` value is end-relative.
- 2026-08-30 **A control assertion gets the tag for what it actually needs, not for the construct it is contrasted with.** Upstream repeatedly writes a pair: a pattern without the feature, then the same thing with it. S05 initially tagged four such controls after their sibling - `(?:fe)?male` as `conditionals`, `(?:(?![a-d]).)+` as `define-groups`, `(?:(?=\d)\d+\b|\w+)` as `conditionals`, `t(es){i<=1,0<e<=1}t` as `fuzzy-syntax` - none of which contain the construct named. This is the S03 mis-tag failure in a new guise: the slice delivering conditionals would un-skip a test that needs lookahead, and it would fail. Retagged to `quantifiers`, `lookaround`, `lookaround` and `fuzzy-budget`. Same fix for `test_hg_bugs` #222, which the port had given a `RightToLeft` option upstream does not pass at all - it is the forward control for #223.
- 2026-08-30 **Phase 2 opens with a compile-parity corpus (S06), not with parser code.** Intercepting `regex._regex.compile` while upstream's own `test_regex.py` runs records the exact bytecode, group index, required-string data, parse errors and compiled replacement templates for every pattern the suite compiles: 1534 compiles, 46 errors, 62 templates, 29,219 integers, 0.2 seconds. Measured, and the interception proven, before the slices were written. Every Phase 2 slice is verified against it because nothing in Phase 2 can match a string, so the ported suite and the Phase 3 match oracle both have nothing to say about the parser.
- 2026-08-30 **84 of those 1534 compiles are nondeterministic upstream**: `RegexBase.__hash__` hashes the node's class object, whose identity is per-process, and two sites turn a `set` into an ordered list that reaches the bytecode (`_check_firstset`, `Branch._reduce_to_set`). Set-member order has no matching semantics. Both the corpus recorder and the port sort at exactly those two points; the deviation goes in PORTMAP's "Where we diverge". Proof is two recorder runs under different `PYTHONHASHSEED` values producing identical fixtures.
- 2026-08-30 **Phase 2 slices are vertical** (parse function, node class, bytecode, per construct family), not upstream's horizontal order (all parse functions, then all node classes), so that every slice's output is checkable against the corpus the day it lands. `Parsing/` still mirrors `_regex_core.py` file for file and name for name; only the order of filling it in differs.
- 2026-08-30 **Unicode tables are transliterated from `upstream/src/_regex_unicode.c`, not regenerated from UCD data, and land in Phase 2 (S09), not Phase 6** (spec amendment 11). The parser consults them at fourteen call sites, so leaving them to Phase 6 would have left most of the corpus unverifiable. The C file is entirely generated (about 250 arrays, three struct arrays, 104 lookup functions of roughly twenty statement shapes), so copying its numbers gives parity by construction. `src/FuzzyRegex.UnicodeGenerator/` (a two-line stub) is deleted. Options and measurements: `docs/plan/2026-08-30-phase2-decisions.md`.
- 2026-08-30 **`\N{name}` gets a generated character-name table at Unicode 17.0.0**, the version of upstream's tables, not the 16.0.0 that CPython 3.14's `unicodedata` (which upstream calls) reports. .NET has no character-name API and 41 ported tests use the construct. 34,137 names need storing after the algorithmic ranges (CJK, Hangul, Tangut and others, 114,716 codepoints) are computed; expected 200-300 KB. Names new in 17.0.0 resolve here and raise under upstream on Python 3.14; CPython 3.15 moves to 17.0.0 and the divergence disappears.
- 2026-08-30 **`FuzzyRegexOptions.ExplicitCapture` and `FuzzyRegex.Unescape` are removed** (S07 and S12). Both were S01 analogies with `Regex`; neither has an upstream counterpart, neither is used by any test, and neither can be verified by the corpus, which would have made them the only unverified logic in the parser.
- 2026-08-30 **No deferred `Match` or option members are added in Phase 2**: `Match.pos/endpos/string/re` (4 reads in the upstream suite, all in `test_getattr`), `allcaptures/allspans/groupdict/capturesdict` (15 lines, all ported through `Groups` and `Captures`), and public `Word`/`Ascii` options (the tests use the inline forms). All are additive and Phase 3 is the first point at which a real `Match` exists to design against.
- 2026-08-30 **The compile-parity corpus is 1547 compiles, 50 errors, 62 templates and 29,322 bytecode integers**, correcting the 1534/46/62/29,219 measured when S06 was authored. Two causes, both deliberate: the recorder forces `cache_it=False`, so a pattern a later test compiles under different named lists gets its own row instead of a cache hit; and it records the rejections upstream raises as a plain `ValueError` rather than `regex.error` (four `(pattern, flags)` pairs, all "ASCII, LOCALE and UNICODE flags are mutually incompatible", `upstream/regex/_main.py:559-568`). Catching only `regex.error` had left those four with no row of any kind, so a port that happily compiled `(?au)\w` would have turned nothing red.
- 2026-08-30 **The "84 nondeterministic compiles" figure is wrong and unmeasurable as stated**; the number varies per run. `RegexBase.__hash__` hashes the node's class *object*, whose hash is its address, so `PYTHONHASHSEED` is not what varies - every fresh process is a fresh sample. Measured with the two sort patches disabled: one pair of runs disagreed on 51 rows, another pair on 82, and eight runs between them touched 111 of the 1547. Any single pair undercounts. With the patches in place all eight runs were byte-identical. `--verify-determinism` therefore does four runs, and is a smoke test rather than a proof: a leak affecting one row could survive a handful of runs.
- 2026-08-30 **There is a third ordering leak, and it is in the caller's container, not upstream's code.** `StringSet.__init__` (`_regex_core.py:4088-4100`) iterates `info.kwargs[name]` and sorts the branches by length only - a stable sort, so two equal-length members keep the caller's iteration order - and upstream's own suite passes a `set` (`test_regex.py:2578-2579`). Found by the two-seed check, not by reading. Fixed at the input: the recorder sorts each named list before handing it over, so the fixture's sorted order is the order upstream compiled in, and **the port sorts nothing** - it keeps whatever order its caller gave. Consequence for S07: the seam takes named lists as `IReadOnlyList<string>`, but the public `FuzzyRegex` surface types them as `IReadOnlyCollection` (S04), which does not carry an order that reaches the bytecode. Decide there whether to tighten the public signature.
- 2026-08-30 **`--check` compares the recorded values, not the file bytes, because `regexVersion` legitimately differs between the dev machine and CI.** The oracle job builds `regex` 2026.8.12 from the pinned submodule; this machine has 2026.7.19 installed. A whole-file `filecmp` failed on that one line and would have reported "stale" about a fixture whose every recorded value was correct - on the very first CI run. Everything else, `upstreamCommit` included, still has to match, and the committed file is separately round-tripped through the writer so a hand-edited or reformatted fixture is rejected. Caught by the S06 blind review; it had never run, because the local `--check` passes for the wrong reason.
- 2026-08-30 **`BeEquivalentTo(..., WithStrictOrdering())` recurses into nested collections**, which made it the wrong tool for `named_list_indexes`: the outer position is the parser-assigned index and must match, but each entry is a `frozenset` upstream whose order the recorder invented when it wrote JSON. Measured: 18 of the corpus's 30 named-list rows have an entry with more than one member and would have demanded that invented order from a correct port, while 0 rows have more than one named list, so the ordering it was added for is not exercised at all. Both the seam and the fixture now type these as `IReadOnlySet<string>`, and the assertion is `Equal(expected, (a, b) => a.SetEquals(b))` - outer order strict, inner set equality. The outer index order remains unverified by the corpus; the named-lists slice needs its own gap test for it.
- 2026-08-30 **`Source` reads whole codepoints but counts UTF-16 code units.** Upstream's `Source.pos` is a codepoint offset; ours is a `char` offset, because AGENTS.md makes every public index in this port a UTF-16 code unit and `FuzzyRegexParseException.Offset` is public. `Get`/`Peek` still return whole codepoints, so a surrogate pair is one character to the parser and the **bytecode is identical to upstream's**: measured 2026-08-30, `'\U0001F63A'` compiles to `[12, 1, 128570, 1]` (one `CHARACTER`, not two surrogate ones) and `'a\U0001F63Ab'` to a `STRING` of length 3, not 4. Only the reported offset differs, and only for a pattern with a non-BMP character before the failure: `'\U0001F63A('` is `pos=2` upstream and `Offset=3` here. No corpus row contains a non-BMP character, so this costs no parity; `Gaps/Parsing/SourceScannerTests.cs` pins all four facts.
- 2026-08-30 **`is_cased_i` is ASCII-only in S07, and that is what unblocked `(?i)` patterns a phase early.** Upstream asks `_regex.get_all_cases`, a four-level lookup into the generated Unicode tables (`_regex_unicode.c:30627`), and `Sequence._flush_characters` calls it for **every** `IGNORECASE` literal run - so without an answer, no `(?i)` pattern compiles and the five `test_getattr` assertions S07 was told to turn on could not have been. Measured against the built oracle: over codepoints 0-127 both the `UNICODE` and `ASCII` encodings report exactly A-Z and a-z as cased, and they first disagree at U+00B5, so 0x80 is the exact boundary below which no guessing is involved. Above it, and under `LOCALE`, the port throws `needs:unicode-tables` rather than guess. Same treatment for Python's Unicode-aware `str.isdigit`, `str.isidentifier` and `str.isalpha`, which the port answers only for ASCII. **S09 deletes all of this**; PORTMAP's "Where we diverge" carries the rows to remove.
- 2026-08-30 **Upstream's `ValueError` rejections become `FuzzyRegexParseException`, not `ArgumentException`.** The corpus pins `Message` against upstream's exact text, and `ArgumentException` appends "(Parameter 'x')" when given a parameter name and trips Sonar S3928 when given none; there is no third option that keeps the message intact. It costs nothing today because none of the three sites is reachable through the public constructor - `FuzzyRegexOptions` has no `ASCII`, `LOCALE` or `UNICODE`, and `Version0|Version1` is rejected earlier by `RegexFlags.DefaultFlags`, which is itself the port of a `KeyError` upstream lets escape (verified: `regex.compile('x', regex.V0|regex.V1)` raises `KeyError: regex.V0|V1`, not the "mutually incompatible" `ValueError`, because `Info.__init__` runs before that check). Revisit in the slice that surfaces those flags as options.
- 2026-08-30 **A `needs:` throw must go at the line that actually needs the missing thing, not at the top of the branch.** S07's blind review found `\p`, `\P`, `\N` and `\g` throwing `needs:unicode-properties` / `needs:named-characters` / `needs:backrefs` on sight, when upstream reaches an ordinary literal through all four whenever the delimiter is absent (`\p` is the literal `p`, and so on): `parse_property` and `parse_named_char` rewind and fall through, and `parse_escape` catches `parse_group_ref`'s `error` and falls back. Six corpus rows and two ported tests were skipping for capabilities they do not need. The general rule for the remaining Phase 2 slices: port the whole function's control flow and throw only where upstream consults a table or builds a node this slice does not have.
- 2026-08-30 **`Gaps/Parsing` as a test namespace shadows `Fuzzy.Text.RegularExpressions.Parsing`** for any file whose own namespace sits under `Tests.Gaps`, so `Parsing.PatternCompiler` in `Gaps/CompileParity` stopped resolving the moment the folder was added. Fixed by qualifying that one reference as `RegularExpressions.Parsing.PatternCompiler` rather than by renaming the folder, so the obvious home for parser gap tests stays available.
- 2026-08-30 **`parse_name` and `Info.is_open_group` compare the group number as a `BigInteger`, not an `int`.** Python's `int()` has no fixed width, so upstream does not overflow on `\g<99999999999`: it parses the number, finds it is not below `min_group`, and carries on to fail on the missing `>` - which `parse_escape` catches and degrades to literals. `int.Parse` threw `OverflowException` straight through that catch, turning a pattern upstream compiles into an error. Found by S07's second blind review, reproduced against upstream 2026.7.19. The value is only ever compared against 0 or 1, so the cost is one parse of a string that is already known to be all digits, at parse time. **Both call sites needed it**: the S07 review of the first fix found `Info.IsOpenGroup` parsing the same name with `int.Parse` one call later, so the delimited `\g<99999999999>` still threw `OverflowException` where upstream raises "invalid group reference". **One divergence is deliberate**: CPython 3.11+ caps `int(str)` at `sys.get_int_max_str_digits()` (4300 by default) and raises `ValueError`, so upstream rejects `\g<` followed by 4301 nines while we compile it. Measured 2026-08-30. Not ported, because that cap is an interpreter setting - changeable at runtime with `sys.set_int_max_str_digits`, raised as `ValueError` rather than `regex.error`, and with no .NET equivalent - so it is a property of the host Python, not of the regex grammar this port mirrors.
- 2026-08-30 **A `needs:` seam that blocks a fallback path is left in place, not widened for the one example that would pass.** `\g<` followed by a non-ASCII name degrades to literals upstream, and reaching that path in the port means getting past `IsDigitName`'s `needs:unicode-tables` throw. Upstream's answer here does not depend on the Unicode tables - no definition of "digit" contains e-acute - but deciding that in general is S09's work, and relaxing a seam because one case looks obvious is how a port acquires a guess it cannot later find. Recorded as a skipped test (`Gaps/Parsing/GroupReferenceFallbackTests.cs`) that S09 turns on.
- 2026-08-30 **The unattended driver must never let a session end its turn with a review outstanding, and must never destroy uncommitted work.** S07 took three attempts: two `claude -p` runs reached a green ratchet and then died without committing, because the session posted a "review still running" message and ended its turn - and under `-p`, ending the turn ends the process. `Undo-FailedSlice` then treated "no commit was made" the same as "the ratchet is red" and `git reset --hard`ed a green tree, twice, at ~50M tokens a time. Three fixes: `port-slice` now states that the reviewer is a blocking call read in the same turn and that a session may not end with uncommitted work; `Undo-FailedSlice` stashes with `-u` before resetting, so a failure is recoverable rather than final; and the driver allowlist was corrected on two axes. Claude Code decomposes a compound command and requires every part to match, so `dotnet build ... | Select-Object -Last 60` is denied on the filter rather than on `dotnet`; and **`Bash(...)` rules do not govern the `PowerShell` tool**. This machine sets `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_POWERSHELL_TOOL=1` globally and the child session inherits it, so a Bash-only allowlist silently denied every PowerShell call the session made. The list now carries both families: `Bash(head|tail|grep *)` for the Bash side, `PowerShell(Select-Object|Select-String|Get-Content|Get-ChildItem *)` for the cmdlets, and `dotnet`/`git`/`pwsh`/`python` mirrored on both. Verified 2026-08-30 by driving `claude -p` with the committed array: `dotnet --version | Select-Object -Last 1` returned `10.0.400` with zero permission denials, where the Bash-only version was refused.
- 2026-08-30 **`Undo-FailedSlice` moved from `run-slices.ps1` into `PortTools.psm1`, for the same reason `Get-SliceFailureReason` lives there: it is testable only where the Pester suite can reach it.** It takes `-RepoRoot` as a parameter rather than closing over the driver's script scope, and returns the stash label instead of writing to the host, so the tests can assert on it and the driver keeps the console output. Six cases against real scratch repositories, not mocks - every bug this function has had was in what git actually did. Both mutations checked: deleting the stash line fails 2 tests, and `clean -fdx` in place of `-fd` fails the one that pins the gitignored slice log surviving. That log is what the budget gate counts, so destroying it would silently reset the driver's own daily cap.
- 2026-08-30 **Upstream's generated bytecode is readable without upstream's C compiler, and that is the only way to see a maximal repeat count.** `regex.compile('a{4294967294}')` spends about twenty minutes in `_regex.compile` and then raises `MemoryError`; everything before that call is O(1) (measured: parse, optimise, compile and flatten all 0.000s at a count of 8,000,000, code length 8). So the oracle for such a pattern is a monkeypatch of `regex._regex.compile` that captures its arguments and **raises instead of calling through** - the dead S08 session's script called through, which is why it appeared to hang. With that, `(?:a{4294967294}){0,4294967294}a` reports `req_offset = -1` and `(?:a{65535}){0,65535}a` reports `req_offset = 4294836225`.
- 2026-08-30 **`max_width` saturation at `long.MaxValue` is confirmed indistinguishable from upstream's unbounded `int`, and the claim is now pinned by a test.** Saturation can only happen above `long.MaxValue`, which is 2^31 times `UNLIMITED`, and every consumer either takes `min(w, UNLIMITED)` or asks `w >= UNLIMITED` - so both the saturated and the true value give the same answer. `Gaps/Parsing/RepeatWidthOverflowTests.cs` pins the saturating case, a non-saturating control that would catch a `Multiply` that always saturated, and the single maximal count. No corpus row can cover any of them, because upstream's own compiler cannot compile them.
- 2026-08-30 **`CompiledPattern.ReqOffset` is a `long`, not an `int`.** Found by writing the test above: the offset is a repeat's maximum width and a count runs to `UNLIMITED - 1` = 4294967294, so `(int)reqOffset` truncated `(?:a{65535}){0,65535}a`'s offset of 4294836225 to -131071. Upstream hands the value to C as a `Py_ssize_t`. No corpus row has an offset above `int.MaxValue`, which is why 693 rows passed with the bug in place.
- 2026-08-30 **The four `RegressionsParseErrorTests` were transcribed in Phase 1 as `"<msg> at position N"`, which is Python's `str(error)` and not this port's shape.** S01 split the two apart: `Message` carries upstream's `error.msg` and `Offset` carries its `pos`. Upstream's own assertion is `assertRaisesRegex(regex.error, MULTIPLE_REPEAT)` where `MULTIPLE_REPEAT` is the bare string `"multiple repeat"` (`test_regex.py:41`), so asserting the message and the offset separately is stricter than upstream, not weaker. Verified against `regex` 2026.7.19: `.???` gives `error: multiple repeat at position 3` with `pos=3`. The three still-skipped tests in that file carry the same wrong shape and each slice that turns one on must split it the same way.
- 2026-08-30 **The Unicode tables are transliterated from upstream's generated C, and the transliterator has to consume the file exhaustively or the whole approach is worthless.** `tools/transliterate-unicode.py` walks a cursor over `upstream/src/_regex_unicode.c` and every byte must match a known construct - a comment, a `#define`, a typedef, a table or a function - so a shape a future upstream introduces fails loudly instead of being skipped. Measured: 31,587 lines, 253 tables (402,293 values), 104 lookup functions built from 44 distinct statement shapes, of which 11 rules cover the 100 generic ones. On top of the cursor, four assertions: every `extern` the header declares must be emitted at the length the header declares, there must be 253 tables, there must be 104 functions, and the hand-ported set must be exactly the four known names.
- 2026-08-30 **Four of the 104 generated lookup functions are hand-ported rather than transliterated, and pinned by digest.** `re_get_all_cases`, `re_get_simple_case_folding`, `re_get_full_case_folding` and `re_get_script_extensions` do not share the three-level-walk shape of the other hundred: they index struct arrays or walk a zero-terminated run. Auto-generating their sixty lines of C# would need about twenty single-use translation rules. Instead they live in `src/FuzzyRegex/Unicode/UnicodeCasing.cs` and the transliterator carries the SHA-256 of each one's whitespace-normalised C body, so an upstream edit fails the script with the new digest to paste in rather than leaving the C# quietly stale.
- 2026-08-30 **The C# element type for a generated table is chosen by what the runtime can put in RVA data, not by uniformity.** A `RE_UINT8` table becomes `static ReadOnlySpan<byte> X => [...]`, which Roslyn emits as a metadata blob with no allocation and no static constructor. A `RE_UINT16` table cannot: RVA spans are byte-only. It becomes `static readonly ushort[]`, which Roslyn still emits as a blob plus `RuntimeHelpers.InitializeArray` - and that path byte-swaps on a big-endian runtime, where `MemoryMarshal.Cast` over a byte blob would silently return the wrong numbers. Every constant is `int`: the widest is `RE_PROP_C_MASK` at 0x30020003, and `int` is what keeps `1 << PropZl` legal, since C# demands an `int` shift count where C takes any integral type. Same reason for the `(int)` casts on every table index: `ReadOnlySpan<T>`'s indexer takes an `int` where a C array takes any integral type.
- 2026-08-30 **A struct array in the C becomes one flat C# array per field, named after the field.** `re_all_cases_table_4` becomes `AllCasesTable4Delta` and `AllCasesTable4Others` (stride 3); `re_properties` becomes `PropertiesName`/`PropertiesId`/`PropertiesValueSet`. A transposed field order is the classic transliteration bug in a struct table, and this makes it unwriteable. C zero-fills a partly initialised aggregate, so the flattening pads: `{ 6310, { 1090, 7301}}` has two `others` in the source and three slots in the declaration.
- 2026-08-30 **`re_get_property[86]` is NULL upstream and throws here.** `RE_PROP_SCX` is many-valued, so `unicode_has_property` branches to `re_get_script_extensions` before it ever indexes the table; calling through the NULL would be undefined behaviour in C. The transliterator asserts the NULL slot set is exactly `[86]`, so a future upstream that adds another many-valued property fails the script.
- 2026-08-30 **The transliterated tables are checked against a second, deliberately naive parse of the same C file, run in Python.** `tools/transliterate-unicode.py` agreeing with itself proves nothing; `tools/record-unicode-fixtures.py` re-reads the C with a flat `re.finditer` sweep that shares no code with it and records a length and a SHA-256 per table. `UnicodeTables.AllTables()` - a generated accessor - lets the C# test walk all 253 and compare. That is what makes 402,293 transliterated numbers a checked quantity rather than a hope.
- 2026-08-30 **Casing parity is exhaustive over every codepoint and stored as one hash per combination, not as rows.** `get_all_cases` and `fold_case` for all 1,114,112 codepoints across (Unicode, ASCII) x (simple, full), plus `fold_case` without `IGNORECASE`, is nine SHA-256s in the fixture and about two seconds of C#. `get_all_cases.ascii.full` and `get_all_cases.ascii.simple` are the same digest, which is correct rather than a bug: `ascii_full_case_fold` never returns more than one codepoint, so the `None` marker upstream appends when a character expands under full folding is never appended for ASCII. Narrow any mismatch by plane and then by block in a throwaway script - a hash says only that something is wrong.
- 2026-08-30 **Property *lookups* are sampled here, not exhaustive, and that is deliberate.** The property dictionary itself - all 185 properties, 5,200 property/value pairs, `munge_name` and `init_property_dict` - is hashed whole, because a wrong property id means `\p{...}` silently matches the wrong set. The lookups behind it are checked at 1,595 codepoints (dense over U+0000-U+01FF, then a 1021-codepoint stride), because exhaustive would be 5,200 pairs times 1.1M codepoints in Python. Exhaustive property parity is Phase 3's job, through the match oracle, where `\p{...}` can actually be run - design spec section 6 says so.
- 2026-08-30 **CPython's `str.isalpha`, `str.isdigit` and `str.isidentifier` are exactly three of upstream's own properties, measured over every codepoint.** `isalpha` is `\p{General_Category=L}`; `isdigit` is `Numeric_Type` of `Decimal` or `Digit` (which is CPython's rule too - its `DECIMAL_MASK` and `DIGIT_MASK` come from UnicodeData.txt fields 6 and 7); `isidentifier` is `XID_Start`-or-`_` for the first codepoint and `XID_Continue` after, with no normalisation. Zero disagreements over all 1,109,309 codepoints CPython 3.14.6 can answer for - everything except the 4,803 added in Unicode 17.0, which its 16.0.0 `unicodedata` does not know. This is what let S09 delete the four ASCII-only seams S07 left, rather than shipping a second Unicode dataset for the Python side.
- 2026-08-30 **`\N{...}` needs a character-name table, it is the one place this port reads a UCD file, and it is generated at Unicode 17.0.0 rather than at the host's version.** .NET has no character-name API. `tools/build-character-names.py` reads `UnicodeData.txt` and `NameAliases.txt` for 17.0.0 - the version `_regex_unicode.h` declares - and emits 40,951 stored names; the four kinds of algorithmic range (Hangul 11,172, CJK about 100,000, Tangut about 6,200) are computed. **Verification is in Python, against the host's own `unicodedata`**, which is an implementation independent of those data files: all 148,853 names it knows must resolve to the same codepoint, and every algorithmic name we compute must resolve back through `unicodedata.lookup`. Both hold. The only unverifiable part is CJK Extension J (U+323B0-U+33479), which is entirely new in 17.0 and is counted instead. The contract was measured, not inferred: the lookup is case-insensitive but normalises nothing else (`LATIN_SMALL_LETTER_A` and `LATINSMALLLETTERA` both raise), aliases resolve, a real name beats an alias (`BELL` is U+1F514, not U+0007), and an algorithmic name takes the codepoint in upper-case hexadecimal with no leading zeros (`CJK UNIFIED IDEOGRAPH-04E00` raises).
- 2026-08-30 **Named sequences are not carried, and the cost of that is one error message.** `unicodedata.lookup("KEYCAP DIGIT ZERO")` returns three codepoints, and `parse_named_char` immediately calls `ord()` on the result - so upstream raises `TypeError` where we say "undefined character name". Adding about a thousand more names to change which error a caller gets is not worth the size.
- 2026-08-30 **The character-name table is stored whole - 40,951 string literals, 1,044,804 characters - and that is 2.1 MB of a 3.5 MB assembly.** Measured after the slice: `dotnet build -c Release` of a clean tree takes 4.0s with the 1.5 MB and 1.85 MB generated files in it, so compile time is not the constraint; package size is. The upgrade path, if the owner wants it, is CPython's: the names are built from a vocabulary of a few thousand words, so storing word indices and tokenising the query would cut it to a few hundred kilobytes. Left until phase 7, where size and speed get measured rather than guessed.
- 2026-08-30 **The parity ratchet could not tell apart two tests whose ids differ only in case, and S09 is the first slice to write any.** PowerShell's default hashtable comparer and `Sort-Object -Unique` are both case-insensitive (`@('Abc','abc','ABC') | Sort-Object -Unique` returns one element), so `Update-Baseline` collapsed `Known_names_resolve(LATIN SMALL LETTER A)`, `(Latin Small Letter A)` and `(latin small letter a)` into one entry and `Test-Ratchet`'s `$byId` lookup kept only whichever came last. Measured: 860 passing tests were recorded as 855, four collision groups, all of them tests of case-insensitive behaviour. The ratchet is the merge gate, so a test it silently stops watching is lost coverage: `Update-Baseline` now sorts `-CaseSensitive` and `Test-Ratchet` keys on an ordinal `Dictionary[string, object]`. **Pester's own `-Contain` and `-Be` are case-insensitive too**, so the two regression tests use `-BeExactly` and `-cmatch`; written first, watched fail, and re-checked by reverting the fix with the suite still in place (2 failures back, then 0). S10 is the case-folding slice and will write many more such names.
- 2026-08-30 **`src/FuzzyRegex/Unicode` was added to the faithful-port `.editorconfig` relaxations, and the `_regex.c` Unicode helpers were put there rather than in `Engine/`.** The relaxation block already covers `Parsing` and `Engine` for the stated reason that a hand-port must keep upstream's shape; `Unicode` is the same kind of directory and the same two rules fire on it (`MA0025` for the `LOCALE` seam, `MA0051` for `unicode_has_property`'s length). The `*.g.cs` files are excluded from analysis entirely by the existing `generated_code` rule, which is why they are named `.g.cs` rather than the folder being marked. Putting `unicode_has_property` and the five module functions under `Unicode/` rather than `Engine/` keeps every table-facing symbol in one directory the parser can use before a VM exists; PORTMAP's file-level map records the split.
- 2026-08-30 **`Sequence._fix_full_casefold` calls Python's `str.lower()`, and that call changes the bytecode - so the port needs a lowercase table, its second and last UCD file.** Folding does not subsume lower-casing: `fold_case` leaves `I` alone, because `I`/`i`/`ı` are the Turkic triple, so `fold("fI")` is `"fI"` and only `.lower()` turns it into `"fi"` - which is the folded form of the ligature U+FB01, and therefore the thing that makes the literal a full-case-folding chunk. Measured against the oracle: `'fI'` under `I|F|U` compiles to `STRING_FLD` and under `I|U` to `STRING_IGN`. Upstream's own generated tables carry no lowercase mapping and .NET's is a different Unicode version (67 codepoints disagree with CPython 3.14.6, measured), so `tools/build-lowercase.py` reads `UnicodeData.txt` field 13 and the *unconditional* rows of `SpecialCasing.txt` for 17.0.0 and emits 1,488 mappings, verified in Python against the host's own `str.lower()` for all 1,109,309 codepoints it knows, with zero disagreements. The 16 conditional `SpecialCasing` rows are skipped: 15 are locale-conditional and CPython does not apply them either, and the 16th is final sigma, which CPython *does* apply but which cannot fire here - the text being lower-cased has already been through `fold_case`, and no codepoint folds to U+03A3 over all 1,114,112.
- 2026-08-30 **Giving every node an ignored `in_set` parameter would have compiled a pattern upstream rejects, and the differential wave caught it.** Upstream declares `in_set` on six classes only, so `AnyAll.optimise(info, reverse, in_set)` is a `TypeError` - and that is reachable, not theoretical: `(?V1)[a--[\s\S]]` reduces its first operand to an `AnyAll` and `SetDiff.optimise` then passes it straight on. The first draft of S10 had one virtual with the parameter, and compiled the pattern. `RegexBase` now has two methods: `Optimise(info, reverse)`, which every node has, and `Optimise(info, reverse, inSet)`, which only `Character`, `Property`, `Range` and the four set types override and whose base throws. Pinned by `Gaps/Parsing/UpstreamInternalErrorTests.cs`, along with the two neighbouring cases where upstream also raises a Python internal error rather than `regex.error` (`[^\s\S]` and an inline `(?V1)` under the `V0` flag).
- 2026-08-30 **`(?L)` on a `str` pattern does nothing until casing is consulted, which is what S09 left this slice to settle.** Measured: the resolved flags come out `LOCALE|VERSION0` - `_main._compile` stops OR-ing `UNICODE` in once an encoding flag is set - the property escapes get encoding tag 0, and `(?L)a`, `(?L)\w`, `(?L)[[:alpha:]]`, `(?L)\b`, `(?L).`, `(?L)\d`, `(?L)[a-z]` and `(?L)\p{L}` all compile to exactly what they compile to with no flag. The only locale-sensitive call the parser makes is `is_cased_i`, and it is reached only from `Sequence._flush_characters`, which a one-item sequence never becomes (`make_sequence`) - so `(?Li)a` compiles here as upstream does and only `(?Li)ab` hits the `needs:locale-flag` seam. `Character.folded` is not locale-sensitive either: it folds with the constant `FULL_CASE_FOLDING`, whose `UNICODE` bit wins over the pattern's flags. Ten measured patterns pinned by `Gaps/Parsing/LocaleFlagTests.cs`.
- 2026-08-30 **`standardise_name` tries to read every property and value name as a number, so its acceptance rule is Python's `float()` and not .NET's `double.TryParse`.** `float()` takes `inf`, `infinity` and `nan` in any case, and underscores between digits (`float("1_0")` is 10.0; `float("1_")` raises). Which branch a name takes is observable, because the fallback strips `_`, `-` and spaces and upper-cases while the numeric path rewrites the value: `1.5` becomes `3/2`, `1e5` becomes `100000`, `1/0` stays `1/0` (`ZeroDivisionError`, caught). One case escapes: `int(float("Infinity"))` raises `OverflowError`, which `standardise_name` does **not** catch, so `\p{Infinity}` propagates it out of `compile` rather than failing to parse. Ported as `OverflowException` with Python's own message. Thirty-four measured names pinned by `Gaps/Parsing/PropertyNameTests.cs`.
- 2026-08-30 **In `lookup_property`, "is there a qualifier?" means the *standardised* qualifier, not the raw one - so `\p{_:Lu}` has no qualifier.** Upstream standardises first (`_regex_core.py:1734`) and every later `if property` / `not property` tests the result (`:1743`), which `standardise_name` can make empty because it strips `_`, `-` and spaces. Getting the order wrong rejects `\p{_:Lu}`, `\p{-:Lu}`, `\p{ :Lu}`, `\p{_=Lu}` and `[[:_:alnum:]]`, all of which upstream compiles - and the POSIX branch specifically needs "no property" rather than "no qualifier written". The port collapses the empty string back to `null` so the rest of the function reads as upstream does. Found by the S10 blind review, measured against the oracle, pinned by twelve rows in `Gaps/Parsing/PropertyNameTests.cs`. Note also that a test of a value that standardises to itself proves nothing about the numeric path: `1/2` is a fixed point of the fallback, so `\p{nv=1/2}` still compiles with `numeric_to_rational` deleted; `\p{nv=1.5}` and `\p{nv=1e-1}` are the rows with teeth.
- 2026-08-30 **The set-member sort PORTMAP has promised since S06 lands as `RegexBase.RenderKey`.** Upstream turns a Python `set` of nodes into a list at two points, and a node's hash starts with its class object, whose hash is its address - so upstream's own bytecode varies per process. `RenderKey` is upstream's `_key` with the class *name* in place of the class *object*, recursing into nested nodes, which is exactly what `_render_key` in `tools/record-compile-corpus.py` computes; the default is the class name alone, matching upstream's default `_key`. Every rendered key is ASCII, so `OrderBy(..., StringComparer.Ordinal)` is Python's `sorted`.
- 2026-08-30 **S10 was verified by a purpose-built differential wave, not only by the corpus.** The corpus holds what upstream's own suite compiles, which is thin on set operators, POSIX classes and case folding. The wave is `.scratch/s10_diff_record.py`: every one of the 104 characters that expand on folding in six contexts each, the Turkic and Cherokee characters, ranges spanning the expanding characters, 600 fuzzed set expressions, and - added after the blind review found the empty-qualifier defect - every combination of 16 property qualifiers with 9 values in both `:` and `=` form. Re-run on 2026-08-30 after the fix: `2897 patterns x 16 flag sets: 32452 compiled, 13884 rejected, 31188 distinct rows recorded`. The rows were compared against upstream bit for bit - `code`, resolved flags, `req_offset`, `req_chars`, `req_flags` and `group_count` - giving `S10DIFF examined=31156 skipped=32 failures=0`. **Zero divergences.** A negative control (three rows corrupted in the fixture) reported exactly three. On the rejected patterns, `S10DIFFERR examined=13884 skipped=0 failures=410`: every message, exception class and offset agreed except the 410 where **upstream itself** raises a Python internal error (`AttributeError`, `TypeError`, `KeyError`), all four classes of which are recorded in PORTMAP's "Where we diverge". The pre-fix wave was narrower (1,705 patterns, 24,884 rows, 1,212 rejects) and found the same 410. The comparison harness is scratch and was deleted; its findings are permanent tests.
- 2026-08-31 **Python's `int()` on a group name accepts any Unicode decimal digit, and the port now has one implementation of it serving all five call sites.** Upstream calls `int(name)` in `parse_name`, `Info.is_open_group` and the `fix_groups` of `RefGroup`, `CallGroup` and `Conditional`. `BigInteger.Parse(name, InvariantCulture)` takes only ASCII digits, so `(x)(?P=١)` - which upstream compiles to `REF_GROUP 1`, measured 2026-08-30 - threw `FormatException`. `Unicode.PythonStr.TryParseInt` replaces it everywhere. Two things it is deliberately not: it is not `str.isdigit`, which is `Numeric_Type` of `Decimal` **or** `Digit`, so `(x)(?P=²)` reaches `int("²")` and upstream lets an uncaught `ValueError` escape - the port throws `NotSupportedException`, the same rule as the three patterns in `UpstreamInternalErrorTests`; and it is narrower than Python on signs, surrounding whitespace and underscores, none of which can reach it because a name is either `get_while(DIGITS)` or a string `str.isdigit` has already accepted. Found by the S11 blind review.
- 2026-08-31 **The decimal *value* of a digit is derived from upstream's tables, not read from .NET's, because mixing the two made the port throw where upstream compiles.** Upstream's tables carry `Numeric_Type` but no numeric value, so the first fix used `CharUnicodeInfo.GetDecimalDigitValue`. That table is an older Unicode version: the ten codepoints U+11DE0-U+11DE9 that Unicode 17.0 added are `Numeric_Type=Decimal` to the `isdigit` gate and unknown to the value lookup, so `(x)\g<𑷡>` raised instead of compiling. `PythonStr.DecimalValue` now takes the codepoint's offset within its own maximal run of decimal digits, modulo ten - the modulo is load-bearing, because runs abut and U+1D7CE-U+1D7FF is one 50-long run of five aligned blocks - and is verified against `CharUnicodeInfo` for every codepoint that table knows. The remaining divergence is the intended one: our tables are 17.0.0 and the host CPython's `unicodedata` is 16.0.0, which is the same choice already made for `\N{...}`. Found by the second S11 review pass, which is the rule about giving unreviewed fixes their own first pass earning its keep.
- 2026-08-31 **`Conditional.Equals` must compare the resolved group number only, never the text the pattern wrote.** Upstream's `__eq__` compares `self.group`, which `fix_groups` has by then replaced with the `int`; the text is gone. Comparing both made `(?(1)a|b)` and `(?(one)a|b)` unequal, so `Branch.optimise` did not hoist them as a common prefix and emitted a `BRANCH` with the conditional duplicated - a real bytecode divergence on `(?<one>x)(?:(?(1)a|b)c|(?(one)a|b)d)`, found by the S11 blind review and pinned by `RelativeCallAndAliasTests`.
- 2026-08-31 **Two analyzer families fired on S11's new nodes and both were obeyed on the merits; nothing was disapplied.** SS008 and S2328 flag a `GetHashCode` over a mutable member: `CallGroup`, `RefGroup` and `Conditional` resolve their group number in `fix_groups`, so hashing it would let a node's hash change while it sat in a set. They hash their immutable part instead - the type, plus case flags where there are any - which is the rule `Branch` already followed and which still keeps the only promise a hash makes. S3877 flags a `throw` from `Equals` or `GetHashCode`: `CallRef` and `GraphemeBoundary` have no `_key` upstream and would raise `AttributeError`, but throwing there breaks any collection or debugger that touches the node and buys nothing, since nothing compares either. Both use reference equality, and their `MaxWidth` still throws - that one would be a real bug if it were ever reached. Recorded in PORTMAP's "Where we diverge".
- 2026-08-31 **A bare `\N` in a replacement template is an error, not the literal `N`, and its name alphabet is narrower than the pattern side's.** `parse_repl_named_char` (`_regex_core.py:1885-1900`) rewinds and returns `None` exactly as `parse_named_char` does, but `_compile_replacement` then falls through to `raise error("bad escape \N")` where `parse_escape` would have made a literal. Its `get_while` set is `ALPHA | {" "}`, not the pattern side's `NAMED_CHAR_PART` (`ALNUM | {" ", "-"}`), so a digit or a hyphen ends the name and `\N{1}` and `\N{LATIN-SMALL-LETTER-A}` are errors too. Measured 2026-08-31 against `regex` 2026.7.19 - all four give `bad escape \N at position 2` - and pinned by `Gaps/Parsing/ReplacementTemplateTests.cs`. Reading the two functions as the same shape would have compiled three patterns upstream rejects.
- 2026-08-31 **`parse_repl_hex_escape` has no range check where `parse_hex_escape` has one**, so `\UFFFFFFFF` is a well-formed template escape that fails later in `chr()` with an uncaught `ValueError`. The ported items are therefore `long`, not `int` - 8 hex digits do not fit - and `PatternCompiler.MakeString` throws `NotSupportedException` above U+10FFFF, the same rule already used for the patterns in `UpstreamInternalErrorTests`. Throwing early in `ParseReplHexEscape` instead would have been observably different only for a template holding both an out-of-range escape and a later syntax error, but `long` costs nothing and keeps upstream's order.
- 2026-08-31 **Which replacement-template errors can be tested before the engine exists is a measurement, not a reading.** Upstream compiles the template before it starts matching, so a malformed one is rejected whether or not the pattern matches - but a *group-number* check happens during expansion and needs a match. Measured 2026-08-31 with a subject that cannot match: all seven `\g<...>` syntax errors and the trailing backslash raise, while `\1`, `\8`, `\11`, `\118`, `\800` and `\1a` all return the subject unchanged. So S12 un-skipped 8 `needs:parse-errors` attributes by prose and left `SubTemplateNumericEscapeTests`' 12 invalid-group-reference rows for Phase 3.
