- 2026-08-31 **Upstream's `IndexError("unknown group")` for a `\g<name>` the pattern has not got becomes `ArgumentException`, not `FuzzyRegexParseException`.** Upstream deliberately declines to use its own error type there, because an unknown group name in a *template* is a caller's argument being wrong rather than pattern syntax, and `System.Text.RegularExpressions` raises `ArgumentException` for the same mistake - which is also what the Phase 1 `SymbolicRefsTests` port already asserted. CA2208, S3928 and MA0015 all require the `paramName` to name a parameter of the throwing method; `CompileReplGroup` is three frames below the public surface and has none, so the honest name is `"replacement"` and all three rules are disapplied **at that one line** with the reason in a comment. Not in `.editorconfig`: the per-directory block would have turned them off for the whole parser, and they are right everywhere else.
- 2026-08-31 **`Match.Result` is not wired to the template compiler, and that is S12's one undelivered scope item.** `Match` is a stub with no fields and nothing constructs it, so there is no pattern to compile a template against; wiring it would mean inventing `Match`'s internals, which is Phase 3's design work. `FuzzyRegex.Replace`, `Replace(..., out int)` and the static `Replace` do compile ahead of matching.
- 2026-08-31 **`FuzzyRegex.Escape` walks whole codepoints, written out rather than through `EnumerateRunes`.** Upstream iterates a Python `str`, so a character above U+FFFF takes one backslash and not one per surrogate - measured: `escape('\U0001F600', special_only=False)` is one backslash and one emoji. `EnumerateRunes` would be the one-line stdlib answer but replaces a lone surrogate with U+FFFD, and a lone surrogate is as legal in a .NET string as it is in a Python `str`: `escape('a\ud800b', special_only=False)` keeps it. Verified over **all 1,114,112 codepoints x 4 flag combinations**, both as a per-codepoint decision bitmap (0 differences) and as a SHA-256 of the concatenated output (four digests equal). The hash needed hand-rolled UTF-16-LE bytes: `Encoding.Unicode.GetBytes` replaces a lone surrogate with U+FFFD where Python's `surrogatepass` does not, which showed up first as four unexplained mismatches.
- 2026-08-31 **A `\uXXXX` escape is decoded before it reaches disk through the Bash tool's heredoc as well as through Write and Edit** (S03 and S05 recorded the Write/Edit path). A literal U+2028 landed in a C# comment that way and failed the build with `CS1010: Newline in constant`, which is DECISIONS 2026-08-30's line-terminator hazard arriving by a new route. The reliable form is to build the text with `chr(0x5c) + 'u2028'` inside the script, so no `\u` sequence appears in the agent's own output at all.
- 2026-08-31 **A fuzzy cost limit is the one number upstream range-checks nowhere, so the port's `uint` code word may only be clamped at the moment it is emitted.** `is_above_limit` (`_regex_core.py:623`) guards repeat counts; `parse_cost_limit` and `parse_cost_term` call a bare unbounded `int()` and check nothing, and the callers then do `max_cost -= 1`, `min_cost += 1` and `not 0 <= min_cost <= max_cost` on it. Two ceilings were tried and both were observable as patterns upstream compiles and this port rejected. At `UNLIMITED`: `{i<4294967296}` subtracted from the ceiling and capped at 4294967294, and `{4294967296<=i<=4294967295}` compiled. At `long.MaxValue`: `{9223372036854775807<i<=9223372036854775808}` overflowed `min_cost += 1` to a negative minimum, and `{9223372036854775807<=i<9223372036854775808}` collapsed two distinct values into one - all three accepted by `regex` 2026.7.19, measured 2026-08-31. The parse-time values are therefore `BigInteger`, and `Fuzzy.CodeWord` is the only clamp. The residual divergence is one code word, which upstream's own `RE_CODE` (an `RE_UINT32`, `_regex.c:58`) truncates identically. Both ceilings were found by S13's two blind review passes; the first was in the code the first pass reviewed, the second was in the fix, which is why the fix got its own pass.
- 2026-08-31 **`isinstance(parsed, Fuzzy)` is asked in two places, and wiring only one appends a spurious copy of the whole pattern.** `_main.py:577` feeds the whole-pattern `CALL_REF` wrapper; `_regex_core.py:4436`, inside `_check_group_features`, decides whether a call to group 0 needs an *additional* copy compiled with different features. With the second left at S08's `false` placeholder, `(?:a(?0)?){e<=1}` compiled to upstream's 25 words followed by a 26-word `CALL_REF` copy of itself. Note the two are deliberately asked of different trees - `_main` of the unoptimised parse, `_check_group_features` of the optimised one - and upstream lets the answers differ, so that is not a bug to fix. Found by the S13 blind review; pinned by `Gaps/Parsing/FuzzySectionTests.cs` with a non-fuzzy control.
- 2026-08-31 **A backreference inside a fuzzy test is rejected, because upstream rejects it and this port would otherwise have silently matched group 0.** `Fuzzy.fix_groups` (`_regex_core.py:2822`) descends into `subpattern` and not into `constraints["test"]`, so upstream's `RefGroup.group` stays the string `'1'`, goes into the code list as a string, and `_regex.compile` answers `RuntimeError: invalid RE code` - measured for both `(a)(?:abc){e<=1:\1}` and the `\g<1>` form. Our `RefGroup.GroupNumber` defaults to 0, a perfectly valid code word, so before the guard the pattern compiled and would have matched the whole subject once the engine landed. `RefGroup.CompileCore` now throws `NotSupportedException` when `GroupNumber` is still 0, which `FixGroups` guarantees means "never fixed". Same rule as `UpstreamInternalErrorTests`: where upstream lets an internal error escape, what matters is that the input is rejected rather than quietly compiled.
- 2026-08-31 **`StringSet._key` is dead upstream, so named-list equality is over the branches.** `StringSet.__init__` sets `_key = (class, name, case_flags)` and then inherits `Branch.__eq__` (`_regex_core.py:2520-2521`), which compares `self.branches` - so two differently-named lists holding the same strings compare equal. The port matches by not overriding `Equals` or `GetHashCode` on `StringSet` at all; `Branch.Equals` gained the `GetType()` test that is upstream's `type(self) is type(other)`, previously unobservable because `Branch` had no subclass. `RenderKey` still renders `_key`, because that stands for `_key` and not for equality.
- 2026-08-31 **A differential wave must sort each named list before handing it to upstream, or it invents divergences.** `tools/record-compile-corpus.py`'s `_canonical_kwargs` replaces every named list with a sorted one, because `StringSet.__init__` sorts its branches by length only - a *stable* sort - so the caller's container order leaks into the bytecode. The recorded rows are therefore upstream's answer for the *sorted* list, and a harness that passes the unsorted list is comparing two different questions: 58 of the S13 wave's first 62 divergences were exactly that. The remaining 4 were the one real finding.
- 2026-08-31 **Five patterns compile here and are rejected by upstream's C compiler, and that is deliberately left for Phase 3.** `{e<=1:\X}`, `{e<=1:\b}`, `{e<=1:\A}`, `{e<=1:\Z}` and `{e<=1:\L<a>}` produce this port's bytecode word for word identical to upstream's Python-level code list; `_regex.compile` then answers `RuntimeError: invalid RE code`. The validator is `re_compile` (`_regex.c:25863-26121`), which is Phase 3's to port, and adding a second one to `PatternCompiler` would put it in the wrong layer. Recorded in PORTMAP's "Where we diverge" with the instruction that the Phase 3 slice must close it; `.scratch/s13_record.py` is the ready-made check. Contrast the backreference case above, which could not be deferred because a `uint` code list cannot hold upstream's value at all.
- 2026-08-31 **`needs:fuzzy-syntax` was retired as a capability tag and its 50 remaining tests moved to `needs:fuzzy-matching`,** which the status board shows going from 48 to 98. The tag named a parser capability that S13 delivered, so leaving it on the status board would have kept a finished capability at the top of the "what to build next" table for the rest of the port. The tests it covered all call `FullMatch` or `MatchAtStart` and wait on the `FUZZY`/`FUZZY_EXT` opcodes, which is what `fuzzy-matching` means. `needs:named-lists` was *not* retired - there is no separate tag for named-list matching, so its prose was corrected instead. Both changes are visible in `docs/STATUS.md`; reverse them if the owner would rather the board tracked parser and engine capabilities separately.
- 2026-08-31 **The ratchet prints a raw passing count against a deduplicated baseline, and the two legitimately differ.** S13 ends `passing: 2046  baseline: 2040`, which looks like an un-updated baseline and is not: the baseline stores unique ids (`Sort-Object -Unique`) while the printed count is one per result. Measured on S13's TRX: 107 ids carry more than one result (101 all-skipped, 6 all-passed), and **zero have mixed outcomes**. The 6 passing pairs are `VariousParseErrorTests.Invalid_pattern_is_rejected` for `(abc`, `a\`, `()`, `abc)`, `a[]b` and `a[`, which upstream's suite genuinely lists twice. A set comparison of the TRX against the baseline is an exact match both ways, so nothing is unwatched today. The latent risk is real but small: the ratchet keys on the id, so if two rows sharing an id ever diverge it would watch only one. Left alone rather than fixed - deduplicating ported rows or widening the id is a change to the merge gate and belongs to a slice, not to a 01:00 tidy-up.
- 2026-08-31 **The local differential oracle may be PyPI regex 2026.7.19; CI's must be the pinned 2026.8.12 built from `upstream/`.** Upstream never published 2026.8.12 to PyPI, so `pip install regex` gives 2026.7.19, and the changelog delta between the two is the single line "Support Python 3.15." (`upstream/changelog.txt`) - behaviourally empty. The S14 recorder therefore accepts exactly those two versions, records which one produced each run, and fails on anything else; oracle.yml keeps asserting the exact pinned version because there it is free to build from source. Owner-approved 2026-08-31.
- 2026-08-31 **The span convention is enforced in exactly two places, and nowhere else.** Engine internals mirror upstream: positions and group spans are `(start, end)` in UTF-16 code units, iterated by codepoint. Conversion to the public `(Index, Length)` happens only in `Match`/`Group`'s accessors (S16/S18), and the S14 oracle recorder independently converts Python's codepoint `(start, end)` to expected UTF-16 `(Index, Length)` - so a slip at the accessor boundary shows as an oracle divergence rather than a silent agreement. A third conversion site anywhere in the engine is a defect by definition (DECISIONS 2026-08-30 has the convention itself).
- 2026-08-31 **The search prefilters are deferred to Phase 7: `locate_required_string` (`_regex.c:11082`), the `string_search`/`fast_string_search` family (`:5231-6918`) and the `try_match` test-node fast path (`:6919-7686`).** All are semantically transparent - they skip positions that cannot match - so deferring them changes speed, never results, and Phase 7 is the optimisation phase. The one risk is a ported test timing out without them; that contingency is named in S19 and checked at the S26 phase close, and the sanctioned response is to port the needed prefilter then, not to skip the test. `set_test_nodes` itself is still ported in S15 so the node graph keeps upstream's shape. Owner-approved 2026-08-31.
- 2026-08-31 **On a row upstream rejected, the oracle tells "this port rejected it too" from "this port crashed on it" by which call threw, not by the exception type or its message.** The types overlap - an `ArgumentOutOfRangeException` is both a deliberate rejection from `RegexFlags.DefaultFlags` and the shape an index slip in the engine takes - so `OracleComparer.Run` catches the `FuzzyRegex` constructor and the matching call separately and marks the latter `WhileMatching`, which `Compare` treats as a divergence. Comparing the *message* instead was tried and is wrong: .NET decorates `ArgumentException.Message` with the parameter name and the actual value, while upstream's `regex.compile('a', regex.V0|regex.V1)` raises `KeyError: regex.V0|V1` (both measured 2026-08-31), so a correct port scored a false divergence. The message is still compared for `regex.error`, whose text this port carries verbatim, exactly as `CompileParityTests` does. Found by S14's second and third blind passes - the first raised the swallowed crash, the second the false divergence the fix for it introduced.
- 2026-08-31 **"This port compiled the pattern and cannot match yet" is a different oracle verdict from "this port knows nothing", and conflating them would have blinded the harness for the whole of phase 3.** A `NotImplementedException` from the matcher used to report `unsupported`, but by then the port has already answered whether the input is acceptable: on a row upstream *rejected*, having compiled it is the divergence, and it is decided before any matcher is consulted. `OracleComparer.Run` therefore returns `CompiledButUnmatched` rather than `null` once construction succeeds, and `Compare` calls that a divergence against a recorded error and `unsupported` against a recorded match. This is what lets the oracle see the five patterns S13 left compiling here and rejected by upstream's `re_compile` - `(?:abc){e<=1:\b}` records `RuntimeError: invalid RE code`, measured 2026-08-31, and is pinned as `A_pattern_upstream_rejected_and_this_port_compiled_is_a_divergence`. Consequence for S15: any generator that emits fuzzy patterns before `re_compile` lands will legitimately turn the wave red.
- 2026-08-31 **`re_compile`'s rejection is `NotSupportedException("invalid RE code")`, not `FuzzyRegexParseException`.** Upstream's node compiler answers `RuntimeError: invalid RE code` (`set_error(RE_ERROR_ILLEGAL)`, `_regex.c:2117-2119`), not its own `error`, so a Python caller writing `except regex.error` does not catch it either. This port keeps one bright line - `FuzzyRegexParseException` means and only means upstream's `error`, which is what `CompileParityTests` asserts row by row - and follows the rule `Gaps/Parsing/UpstreamInternalErrorTests` already set for every upstream rejection with a non-`error` type. The alternative, mapping it to `FuzzyRegexParseException` so that one `catch` covers every bad pattern, was rejected: it would put a rejection upstream deliberately typed differently under the one type the corpus pins, and the wart it removes already exists for the three patterns in `UpstreamInternalErrorTests`. Message is upstream's, verbatim.
- 2026-08-31 **The engine-only operators (`RE_OP_BODY_END` .. `RE_OP_TAIL_START`, 81-97) live in `Parsing.Opcode`, not in a second enum.** `_regex.h:19-117` continues the parser's own numbering past `FUZZY_EXT` with seventeen operators the C compiler invents while building the graph; upstream keeps one numbering across both files and keys its `switch` on it. A separate `Engine` enum would duplicate all 81 shared names and add a cast at every node the builders create, for no check the single enum does not already give.
- 2026-08-31 **The node graph is built in the `FuzzyRegex` constructor and thrown away until S16.** Upstream's `_compile` hands its code list straight to `_regex.compile`, so the node compiler is the last thing that can reject a pattern; building at first match instead would move that rejection somewhere no caller expects it. Nothing reads the graph yet, so it is not stored - IDE0052 is right that a field nothing reads is dead. S16 is the slice that keeps it.
- 2026-08-31 **`csharp_indent_case_contents_when_block = false`.** The .NET formatter indents a braced block after a `case` label one level further than CSharpier does, and IDE0055 is an error here, which made a `case X: { ... }` unwritable - and the `Engine/` port needs one wherever upstream's C case declares a local. CSharpier is the repository's formatter of record (pre-commit hook and CI), so the .NET formatter is configured to agree with it rather than the other way round. Not an analyzer suppression: the rule still fires on every other formatting difference.
- 2026-08-31 **`basic_match` keeps upstream's shape: one dispatch switch, one backtrack switch, and `goto` at upstream's labels.** The alternative shapes - recursion, or a table of per-opcode strategy objects - were rejected before any code was written. `basic_match` (`_regex.c:11714-17403`) is where most upstream fixes land, and it is a backtracking machine whose two loops jump into each other and back to the search restart; expressing that some other way means re-deriving the translation at every sync instead of diffing it (AGENTS.md, design spec section 4). Consequence: Sonar's S907 ("remove this use of `goto`") is disapplied for `src/FuzzyRegex/Engine/**` and nowhere else, with the reason written at the `.editorconfig` entry. `Parsing/` and `Unicode/` contain no `goto` and must not start containing one.
- 2026-08-31 **`RE_State` is ported as a class with internal fields, so the CA1051/S1104/MA0008 note in `.editorconfig` is still untriggered after S16.** The note was written for a public-field struct on the assumption that the engine would need one. It does not: every helper in `basic_match` mutates the state, so a struct would put `ref` on each signature to say what a reference type says for free, and nothing in `Engine/` is public, which is what all three rules key on. Measured on this slice's build - none of the three fires. The note stays in place, still untested, for the slice that genuinely lands a public-field struct; forcing one now to discharge a plan item would be the tail wagging the dog. S16 also did not port `push_pointer`, which is where a struct-shaped question will actually arrive (S18).
- 2026-08-31 **A zero-width `CHARACTER` node steps by 0, and the port must honour `node->step` rather than assume 1.** `optimise_pattern` hoists the first character test of a pattern that begins with an anchor in *front* of that anchor, as a `CHARACTER` node carrying `RE_ZEROWIDTH_OP` and `step == 0`, so `'^a'` compiles to `CHARACTER(step 0) - START_OF_STRING - CHARACTER(step 1)`. S16's first draft advanced one codepoint for any `CHARACTER`, which left the anchor testing the wrong position, and **every pattern with a leading anchor failed to match** - 41 rows of a 2,400-row wave. Found by the differential oracle, not by the ported suite, which had no un-skipped test for a leading anchor. Pinned by `Gaps/Engine/MatchSpineTests`. **`RANGE` and the `SET_*` family carry the same flag** (`NodeCompiler.BuildRange`, `BuildSet`), so S17 must read `node.Step` too; `ANY` and `STRING` do not, and upstream increments them unconditionally.
- 2026-08-31 **`RE_ERROR_MEMORY` from the backtracking stack is an `InvalidOperationException`, not an out-of-memory exception.** Upstream's 1GB `RE_MEMORY_LIMIT` is kept - an unbounded backtracking stack is a denial-of-service vector and the guard is the defence - but the .NET runtime reserves both `OutOfMemoryException` and `InsufficientMemoryException` for real allocation failures (CA2201, S112 and MA0012 all say so), and a library that throws one makes a genuine failure indistinguishable from this limit. Unreachable in S16, where the stack never holds more than a few bytes; S19 is the first slice that can push enough to reach it.
- 2026-08-31 **A matching timeout surfaces as `System.Text.RegularExpressions.RegexMatchTimeoutException`, the built-in type.** Upstream raises `TimeoutError` (`_regex.c:2150`), which has no .NET counterpart, and this port's public surface is shaped after `Regex` throughout; the built-in exception means exactly this, already carries the input, the pattern and the timeout, and a caller who already handles `Regex` timeouts needs no new `catch`. Thrown from `FuzzyRegex.Run` rather than from the engine, because that is where the pattern text lives; `Matcher.DoMatch` returns `MatchStatus.Cancelled` and lets the caller raise. The plumbing is pinned by `MatchSpineTests`, but nothing can actually time out until S19 lands quantifiers - a pattern the S16 spine can run always terminates.
- 2026-08-31 **`csharp_indent_labels = no_change`**, the label twin of the `csharp_indent_case_contents_when_block` line above and settled the same way. The .NET formatter's default (`one_less_than_current`) outdents a `goto` label; CSharpier leaves it at the surrounding indent; IDE0055 is an error here, so a label formatted by one tool failed the other. S16 hit it the hard way: the pre-commit CSharpier hook reformatted `Engine/Matcher.cs`'s three labels *during* the commit, and the resulting tree - already committed, and green when it was verified a minute earlier - failed to build. CSharpier is the formatter of record (pre-commit hook and CI), so the .NET formatter is configured to agree with it rather than the other way round. The general lesson is worth more than the setting: **a formatter that runs inside the commit hook can turn a verified working tree into a broken commit, so re-run the ratchet after committing, not only before.**
- 2026-08-31 **Native AOT compatibility is a shipped requirement, enforced statically from now and dynamically in phase 6** (spec amendment 12). `<IsAotCompatible>true</IsAotCompatible>` goes on `src/FuzzyRegex` immediately, not at packaging time: on net8+ it enables the trim, AOT and single-file analyzers, and `TreatWarningsAsErrors` is already true (`Directory.Build.props:12`), so the slice that introduces a hazard fails its own build rather than handing phase 8 a retrofit across a finished engine. It costs nothing to set today - measured 2026-08-31, `src/` contains no reflection at all: every `System.Reflection` hit was in generated `obj/` assembly-info or a compiled binary, and the only `System.Type` use is `typeof(...)`/`GetType()` in `Equals` and `GetHashCode`, which is statically known and trim-safe. Static analysis is necessary and not sufficient, so phase 6 also publishes a small consumer app with `PublishAot=true` in CI and asserts real matches; that is the test that proves the claim. The reason this could not wait for phase 8 is phase 7: the classic .NET regex optimisation is `RegexOptions.Compiled`, which emits IL at run time via `Reflection.Emit`/`DynamicMethod` and has no JIT to emit into under native AOT, so phase 7 must plan around the constraint instead of discovering it after building the fast path.
- 2026-08-31 **Phase 6 closes against four named criteria, not a coverage percentage** (spec amendment 12). In descending order of value: (1) skips remaining in the ported suite - 1,880 of 5,492 as of today, about 34%, each with a machine-readable `needs:` reason that `tools/PortTools.psm1` aggregates into `docs/STATUS.md`, which makes it an enumerated per-capability list of unported upstream behaviour and a better instrument than any coverage tool for a port; (2) oracle waves across every generator with zero divergences, the only source of real ground truth and the only one that finds behaviour never tested at all - S16's leading-anchor bug was found by a wave and by no ported test; (3) mutation testing scoped to the public API layer and the parse-error paths, the two places the oracle cannot reach, and the only instrument that answers "would a regression actually fail a test?"; (4) line coverage last and as a backstop only, to find a file or branch with no test at all, never as a target. The gate exists because "sweep for coverage gaps" with no criteria produces a number nobody acts on. Phase 6 additionally pins what phase 7 regresses against, since optimisation is where silent behaviour change is likeliest: the benchmark baselines from `.claude/skills/benchmark/SKILL.md` (specified there, nothing committed under `bench/baselines/` yet) and the edge cases an optimiser is tempted to special-case - zero-width and empty matches, anchors, `MatchTimeout`, large inputs, pathological backtracking.
- 2026-08-31 **The browser demo is phase 9, after 1.0, and it runs the engine in a Web Worker - the worker is the safety design, not a performance choice** (spec amendment 12). A public demo invites strangers to type pathological patterns, regex matching is unbounded in the worst case, and this is a fuzzy engine, so approximate matching is combinatorially worse than the exact case. A `MatchTimeout` alone does not bound the freeze: it only fires if the engine polls the deadline in its inner loop, so one missed check point turns a bounded stutter into a frozen tab, and the guarantee then rests on the correctness of the very engine the demo exists to show off. In a worker it rests on the browser's scheduler instead - a runaway is killed with `worker.terminate()`, a warm spare is pre-spawned so the respawn is not felt, and `MatchTimeout` stays as the fast common-case exit and defence in depth rather than the sole net. No cross-origin isolation is needed: the worker boots a second, independent .NET WebAssembly runtime and talks to the page over `postMessage`, while COOP/COEP are required only by `WasmEnableThreads`, a different mechanism this design does not use - worth recording because GitHub Pages cannot set custom response headers at all, so the distinction is what keeps someone from later concluding the whole shape is impossible on Pages. Blazor was the first recommendation and was dropped once the UI no longer had to be .NET; it brings the Components assemblies and boot machinery for no benefit at this size. Vue 3 over SolidJS on owner familiarity: the expensive work is all behind `postMessage`, so fine-grained reactivity would optimise the part that was never the bottleneck, and the UI is three inputs, a result pane and an examples list. Verified 2026-08-31 against current Microsoft documentation and `dotnet/blazor-samples`.
- 2026-08-31 **`ENCODING_KIND` is live, and grepping the C alone says the opposite.** The macro (`_regex.c:167`) reads bits 16-17 of `node->status`, which are also `RE_STATUS_HAS_GROUPS` and `RE_STATUS_HAS_REPEATS`, and nothing anywhere in `_regex.c` writes them - the only two hits for `RE_ENCODING_SHIFT` are the `#define` and the macro itself. The tempting conclusion, that the switch is dead and always takes its `default` arm, is wrong: the bits come from the Python compiler, where `Property._compile` ORs `self.encoding << ENCODING_OP_SHIFT` (5) into the code word's flags and `create_node` then shifts the whole flags word by `RE_STATUS_SHIFT` (11), landing it on 16-17. Proved by intercepting `_regex.compile`: plain `\d` emits node flags `1`, `(?a:\d)` emits `33` (0x21) and `(?u:\d)` emits `65` (0x41). This is the whole of the scoped `(?a:...)`/`(?u:...)` flag at match time, and skipping it made `(?a:\d)` match U+FF19 FULLWIDTH DIGIT NINE. Ported as `NodeStatus.EncodingKind` and `Matcher.NodeEncoding`, pinned by `Gaps/Engine/ScopedEncodingTests`. It is safe only because no node carries both meanings - a future slice that sets `HAS_GROUPS` on a `PROPERTY` node would break the ASCII flag silently. **The general lesson: a claim about upstream's behaviour is a hypothesis until it is run.**
- 2026-08-31 **S4144 is disapplied for `tests/FuzzyRegex.Tests/Ported/**` and nowhere else.** Splitting a ported table test by the capability each row waits on necessarily leaves several methods with identical bodies: upstream drives its whole 524-row `test_various` table through one loop and one assertion, so the body is shared by construction and what distinguishes two methods here is their `[Arguments]` data and their `[Skip]` tag. Merging them back to satisfy the rule would delete the per-capability gap information `docs/STATUS.md` is generated from, and extracting the body into a helper does not satisfy it either - the callers are then identical one-liners. Scoped to `Ported/` rather than all of `tests/` because the reason is only true there. First needed at S17, splitting `test_various` by `groups` and `quantifiers`.
- 2026-08-31 **Un-skipping a slice's tags is triage, not a search-and-replace.** S17 removed 84 `[Skip]` attributes for its five tags; 300 tests then failed for the right reason, and after the engine landed **156 still failed** - none on an assertion, every one at another slice's seam. They were retagged `find-all` (132), `groups` (57), `quantifiers` (~40) and `ignore-case` (~9), and six fan-out methods had to be *split* because only some of their rows were blocked. Two consequences worth keeping: a slice should budget for the retagging, and it should diff every `[Arguments]` multiset against `git show HEAD:<path>` afterwards, because a split is exactly where test data gets altered without anyone noticing.
- 2026-08-31 **`Seam.Tag` is part of the deliverable, not decoration.** After S17 landed `PROPERTY`, `RANGE` and the `SET_*` family, the tag table still routed their `_IGN` variants to `needs:unicode-properties` and `needs:character-classes` - capabilities the status board now reports as delivered - so `(?i)\p{Cyrillic}` threw a tag that exists nowhere in the test suite and would have appeared on an oracle `unsupported` row. Found by the blind review. **Every slice that delivers a tag must re-home the opcodes that keep throwing it.**
- 2026-08-31 **Phase 6 gains a sweep of upstream's open issues - triage, reproduce as a test, fix here, report there - because the differential oracle is blind to an inherited bug by construction** (spec amendment 13). The oracle compares this port against upstream, so a bug we reproduce faithfully produces *agreement*, which is what it reports as success; a divergence shows up only where we accidentally failed to reproduce one. More waves cannot help, because the blindness is in what the comparison means, not in how much of it runs. It goes before phase 7 so that optimisation is not built on behaviour already known to be wrong. Sized from a real triage, not a guess: all 79 open issues on `mrabarnett/mrab-regex` were classified with `gh` on 2026-08-31 - 40 not bugs, 24 Python-specific and impossible in a C# port, 12 real engine bugs we would inherit, 3 undecidable without a reproduction attempt - and the 12 fall in four groups: memory-safety (611-614), fuzzy and BESTMATCH (470, 563, 564, 596), resource blowups (551, 554) and two singletons (367 partial matching, 425 branch reset). 3-5 sessions, an estimate whose basis is that count and nothing else, since the per-issue reproduce-fix-report cost is unmeasured; phase 6 therefore reads 6-10. The memory-safety four are heap-buffer-overflow reads and writes in C and cannot manifest as corruption here - they arrive as an `IndexOutOfRangeException` or a wrong answer - but the logic errors behind them are inherited, and several are compiler-side in code S15 and S16 have already ported, so one may surface early. Fixing a bug upstream still has creates a permanent oracle divergence, so the sweep brings an intentional-divergence allowlist: a version-controlled manifest carrying row identity, our result, upstream's result, a reason linking the upstream issue and this entry, and the upstream commit it was recorded against - the pattern web-platform-tests, test262 and pytest `xfail` all use, and stricter than PyPy's prose-only `cpython_differences`. Strictness is the anti-rot ingredient: a listed divergence that stops diverging must fail the run, as `xfail_strict` does and as Chromium's TestExpectations, documented as accumulating stale entries, does not. Same shape as `tests/parity-baseline.json` plus `tools/check-ratchet.ps1`, so it follows that convention rather than inventing one. Upstream engagement, verified the same day: the maintainer merges external PRs (7 of the last 20 closed, Feb-May 2026), no CLA, no CONTRIBUTING.md, and crisp reports land within hours (#607/#608 fixed the same evening) while design-trade-off arguments stall - so a report carries a minimal runnable repro with the version pinned, names the faulting function, distinguishes the bug from a similar one already fixed, and proposes a fix. Nothing is filed without the owner approving the text, and `gh` stays out of the driver allowlist in `tools/run-slices.ps1` so an unattended session cannot post. Licence checked, no action: `NOTICE` already declares the derivative work at the pinned commit with upstream's CNRI/Secret Labs statement and the UCD credit, satisfying Apache 2.0 4(b) and 4(c).
- 2026-08-31 **A node reference on the byte stack is an index into `PatternObject.NodeList`, not a reference.** Upstream's `push_pointer` (`_regex.c:2472`) pushes a raw `RE_Node*` into the untyped byte stack, and S18's `BRANCH` case is its first caller here. A managed reference cannot go in a `byte[]`, so `ByteStack.PushNode`/`PopNode` push `Node.Index` - a position in `PatternObject.NodeList`, which *is* upstream's `node_list`. That keeps the stack 8 bytes wide per pointer, so the byte layout still matches upstream's frame for frame, which the obvious alternative - a second, parallel `List<Node>` stack - would have broken, and with it the property that a future diff of `basic_match` maps mechanically onto this file. `Node.Index` is assigned in one loop at the **end** of `PatternObject.Compile`, because `Optimiser.DiscardUnusedNodes` removes unreachable nodes from the list and any index handed out earlier would be stale, and because the required-string node is appended after `CompileToNodes` returns.
- 2026-08-31 **The oracle wave now records `lastindex` and `lastgroup`, because nothing about the group spans implies them.** `regex.match('((a))', 'a').lastindex` is 1 although groups 1 and 2 both succeed with the same span - it is the group that *closed* last, from `group_info[g].end_index` - and `lastgroup` names the last *named* group even when an unnamed one succeeded later. So an engine that got only those two wrong would have agreed on every span of every row, and the port's `Match.LastGroupNumber`/`LastGroupName` were shipped by S18 with only three trivial ported tests behind them. Python's `None` maps to `-1` and to `null`, matching what the two members return. The negative control that justifies the change: ordering `lastindex` by group number instead of by which group closed last produced **195 divergences in 7,500 rows** and *zero* before the fields were compared. The gap was found by S18's blind review as a non-defect observation, which is the shape those observations should take - a hole in the instrument rather than a bug in the code.
- 2026-08-31 **`save_captures`/`restore_groups`/`discard_groups` have no caller until Phase 5, and `push_groups`/`push_captures` none until Phase 4 - so S18 ported none of them, against its own slice file.** The slice's stated reason for including them was that a repeated capture group exercises `save_captures`/`restore_groups`; it does not. Grepping `_regex.c` for call sites settles it in one step: `save_captures` is called only from `do_best_fuzzy_match` (`:17760`, `:17789`) and `do_enhanced_fuzzy_match` (`:17957`, `:18000`, `:18008`), both BESTMATCH/ENHANCEMATCH and therefore Phase 5; a repeat instead uses `push_captures`/`pop_captures`, whose only callers are `ATOMIC` (`:12040`), `CONDITIONAL` (`:12226`), `LOOKAROUND` (`:13774`), `GROUP_CALL` (`:13405`) and `GROUP_RETURN` (`:13500`) - all Phase 4. Porting them anyway would have shipped six pieces of engine code with no caller and therefore no test, which is the one thing a port of a backtracking matcher cannot afford. `PORTMAP.md` records every call site so the next slice does not re-derive this. **The general lesson is the same as `ENCODING_KIND`'s: a claim about upstream is a hypothesis until it is run - and for "who calls this?", the grep *is* running it.**
- 2026-08-31 **Do not rewrite a source file with Python's `Path.write_text` on Windows.** It opens in text mode with `newline=None`, which translates every `\n` to `\r\n`, and this repository's working tree is LF (`.gitattributes` sets `* text=auto eol=lf`). S18's un-skip pass rewrote 22 test files as CRLF, which changed every line of each of them and failed the build on IDE0055 in a file it had not otherwise touched. `write_bytes` on a UTF-8 encoded string is the fix. Worth recording because the symptom - a formatting error at a line the script never edited - points nowhere near the cause.
- 2026-08-31 **Upstream is exponential on `(a|a)*b` too, and the `try_match` test-node arm is not what saves it - `locate_required_string` is.** S19's guard gap test hung on `(a|a)*b`, and upstream had measured 0.00ms on the same pattern out to n=22, so the port looked to have a guard bug. It has not. The first measurement fed upstream `'a' * n + '!'`, a subject holding no `'b'`, and upstream's required-character prefilter (`locate_required_string`, `_regex.c:11082`) rejects such a subject before `basic_match` runs at all. Re-measured against `'a' * n + 'xb'`, which the prefilter cannot reject: upstream takes 0.14ms at n=8, 1.69ms at n=12, 27.8ms at n=16, 444ms at n=20 and 23.3 **seconds** at n=26. This port measures 0.81ms, 39.4ms and 534.6ms at n=8, 14 and 20 - the same curve, a small constant factor slower. Two consequences. First, **the Phase 7 prefilters are not only a speed matter**: they are the difference between an instant answer and an exponential search on a whole family of patterns, and `docs/PORTMAP.md` and `Matcher`'s class remarks now say so instead of calling them semantically transparent and leaving it there. Second, **`try_match`'s test-node arm (`:7671`) was ported in full and reverted in the same slice**: it was written to fix a blowup it turned out not to cause, and it measurably fixed nothing - `(a+)+b`, the one pattern that looked improved, was JIT warm-up noise on both sides. About a hundred lines of engine code and a behaviour change at eleven call sites, for no measured gain, is Phase 7's business behind a benchmark, not a slice's. **The general lesson: before diagnosing a performance divergence, make sure both sides are running the same code path - a prefilter that answers first makes the engines incomparable.**
- 2026-08-31 **A repeat count is a count of characters, and this port's positions are UTF-16 code units, so upstream's `text_pos += count * step` cannot be ported as arithmetic.** Upstream indexes the subject by codepoint, which makes a character count and a subject offset the same number; it multiplies freely (`_regex.c:13390`, `:13754`, `:15721`, `:15851-15852`, `:16485-16489`) and subtracts freely to get a count back (`:16297`, `:17058`). Here an astral character is one character and two code units, so every one of those sites needs a walk instead: `Matcher.CountOne` returns the position its walk reached as an extra out-parameter, and `Matcher.StepBy` and `Matcher.CountBetween` carry the two conversions that remain. Both are documented as **not upstream functions** and as existing for exactly this reason, so a future sync does not read them as an invention. The negative control that this is load-bearing: making `CountOne` step one code unit per character instead of one codepoint diverged on 1 of 600 quantifier rows - thin, because a lone low surrogate still satisfies `.` and the walk often ends in the same place, which is precisely why it needs the dedicated astral assertions in `Gaps/Engine/RepeatTests` rather than the wave alone.
- 2026-08-31 **`push_repeats`/`pop_repeats` and the guard-data pushes belong to Phase 4, not to S19, against S19's own slice file - the same mistake the S18 slice file made, found the same way.** The slice listed "the real bodies of `push_guard_data` / `pop_guard_data` and `push_repeat_data` / `pop_repeat_data` / `push_repeats` / `pop_repeats`" as its scope. One grep settles it: the ten call sites (`_regex.c:12228`, `:12431`, `:13407`, `:13502`, `:13514`, `:15423`, `:15475`, `:16369`, `:16400`, `:16416`) are all inside `CONDITIONAL`, `END_CONDITIONAL`, `GROUP_CALL` and `GROUP_RETURN`, every one Phase 4. The repeat opcodes park their own state through `RE_RepeatStateData`, `RE_BodyEndStateData`, `RE_MatchBodyTailStateData` and `RE_RepeatOneStateData` instead. **Two slice files in a row have now claimed a Phase 4/5 helper for a Phase 3 slice on the strength of what the helper's name suggests, so the next phase's slice files should be written with the grep in hand rather than from the header comments.**
- 2026-08-31 **A negative control that does not fire is evidence about the control, not about the code, and it has to be replaced rather than recorded.** Two of S19's three planned controls missed. Relaxing `if (count > maxCount) goto backtrack` in the `LAZY_REPEAT_ONE` backtrack case (`_regex.c:17090`) produced **zero** divergences in 600 rows, because `pos` never advances past `limit` and so `count <= maxCount` always holds - the branch is upstream's defensive guard and is dead in practice. Rewriting `CountBetween` to subtract positions instead of walking codepoints made the engine **hang** rather than diverge, which is a signal but not a countable one. The three controls that did fire, and the numbers worth keeping: refusing to retreat a greedy `*_REPEAT_ONE` down to exactly its minimum gave **148 divergences of 600**; counting a lazy `*_REPEAT_ONE` up to its maximum instead of its minimum gave **54 of 600**; stepping one code unit per character in `CountOne` gave **1 of 600**.
- 2026-08-31 **A negative control must be recorded with the four values needed to re-run it - the before/after snippet, the generator, the row count and the seed - not just the number it produced.** Verifying S19, reconstructing its strongest control from the English description in this file gave 3 divergences, then 8 on a second reading, against the 148 it reported; the reported figure was correct and only the session's own gitignored `.scratch/control.py` and `.scratch/ctrlA.txt` settled it, at 452 agree / 148 diverge, seed 7. Prose describing a mutation is not a mutation. Since the control figures are the only evidence that an oracle generator can detect a fault at all, a figure nobody can reproduce is not evidence - and it was living only in a gitignored directory, against this project's own rule. The `port-slice` skill now requires the four values in the closing notes, S19's are back-filled, and S26 re-runs each one at the phase close. S18's are already lost: its scratch files are gone and only the numbers survive, which is the demonstration rather than a hypothetical.
- 2026-08-31 **The Phase 6 mutation-testing pass widens from the API layer to the engine, because the constraint that scoped it narrowly was machine time and the owner has granted overnight runs.** Amendment 12 already made mutation testing item 3 of the Phase 6 exit gate and the roadmap already parked Stryker.NET for Phase 6, both confined to the public API layer and the parse-error paths on the stated grounds that "Stryker reruns the suite per mutant, so a 30k-line engine would take hours to days per run". That is a working-day objection, not a correctness one. With the pass and the Phase 7 optimisation slices allowed to run overnight with no contention, the engine comes into scope - and the engine is what Phase 7 rewrites, so it is the part whose test coverage most needs measuring before that starts. Sized at 1-2 sessions, Phase 6 6-10 to 7-12. Design spec amendment 14 has the evidence: mutant detection correlates with real-fault detection more strongly than statement coverage does, with a 73% coupling rate and a 17% blind spot (Just et al., FSE 2014), and Stryker.NET 4.16.0 reaches TUnit only through its still-preview MTP runner, which is why the deliverable is a written verdict rather than a CI gate.
- 2026-08-31 **S20 ported `ATOMIC` and `END_ATOMIC`, which its slice file did not scope, because `\X` compiles through them.** `_regex_core.Grapheme._compile` (`upstream/regex/_regex_core.py:2919`) turns `\X` into `Atomic(Sequence([LazyRepeat(AnyAll(), 1, None), GraphemeBoundary()]))`, so before the atomic opcodes existed every `\X` pattern threw `needs:atomic` without reaching a single grapheme rule. The two honest options were to port `GRAPHEME_BOUNDARY` as code no pattern could reach - unreachable, untestable, unverifiable, and the sort of thing that rots until Phase 4 - or to port `ATOMIC` as its dependency and deliver the `grapheme` tag the slice was written to deliver. The second cost about 90 lines: two forward cases, two backtrack cases and `push_captures`/`pop_captures`, which save only each group's `count` and `current` because a capture list is append-only within a match. `push_fuzzy_counts` is omitted from both ends, marked `NOT PORTED`, so the stack block is the same shape at both. The knock-on is that `atomic` is delivered too, `\R` works, and six more ported tests pass. **The general lesson, and the third scope surprise in three slices: a slice file written from upstream's C without checking what the Python compiler emits will mis-scope - S18 and S19 over-scoped from header comments, S20 under-scoped because the construct it named compiles through one it did not.**
- 2026-08-31 **Three of S20's four negative controls fired on nothing until the generator was widened, and that loop is the work, not a formality.** Seeding a fault into WB5 diverged on **0 of 600** rows: WB1/WB2 answer "boundary" for the two ends of the subject before any other rule runs, and an affix-shaped pattern searched forwards mostly matches at position 0, so 48 rows holding `(?w)` and `\b` never reached a rule past WB2 - fixed by an `infix` shape that puts the assertion between two characters the subject holds side by side. Seeding a fault into the regional-indicator count then also gave **0 of 600**, because two regional indicators have to be adjacent for GB12/GB13 to decide anything and a per-character draw rarely produces a pair - fixed by inserting whole clusters (a flag pair, an emoji ZWJ sequence, a base plus combining mark, a Devanagari conjunct, `can't`, `3.2`). Removing the `\K` backtrack restore gave **0 of 600** again, because none of the first five `\K` shapes can *fail* after the marker - fixed by three shapes that put the `\K` inside an alternative or an optional group. Final figures, all on `boundaries`, 600 rows, seed 7: 1, 2, 2 and 3 divergences. **All four are thin, so each of the four behaviours also has a dedicated assertion in `tests/FuzzyRegex.Tests/Gaps/Engine/BoundaryTests.cs`** - a control that fires on one row in six hundred proves detectability, not coverage.
- 2026-08-31 **`Py_UNICODE_TOLOWER` in `is_unicode_vowel` is `PythonStr.Lower`'s first codepoint, and U+0130 is why it matters.** WB5a does not break between an apostrophe and a vowel, and upstream decides "vowel" by simple-lowercasing first. Measured against `regex` 2026.7.19: `finditer(r"(?w)\b", "'İ")` starts at 0 and 2 - no break - while `"'ı"` (dotless i) gives 0, 1 and 2. So U+0130 LATIN CAPITAL LETTER I WITH DOT ABOVE counts as a vowel and U+0131 does not, which only a mapping that sends U+0130 to `i` gets right. `PythonStr.Lower` puts U+0130 in its SpecialCasing expansion (`i` plus a combining dot) and only its first codepoint can be one of the twenty vowels compared against, so taking `[0]` is upstream's answer. The apostrophe has to sit at the start of the subject for the rule to be observable at all: between two letters WB6 and WB7 answer first and WB5a never runs, which is why `"l'a"` and `"l'z"` give identical answers and look like evidence that the rule does nothing.
- 2026-08-31 **A quoted measurement has to be re-taken after the last change to the thing measured, and the recorder's seeding is not `random.Random(seed)`.** Nine of the eleven findings across S21's two blind reviews were the same mistake: a figure in a comment that no longer reproduced. Four of them shared one root cause - the measurements were taken with `random.Random(1)` while `_record_wave` seeds a generator with `random.Random(f"{seed}:{name}")` (`record-oracle.py:1219`), so `"1:backrefs"`, a different stream. A figure measured on the wrong stream describes a wave the recorder never produces, and it looks exactly like a real measurement. The rest were figures taken before a later change to the generator (a new shape, a longer subject) and never re-taken, plus one stale constant - "1,534 patterns" for the compile-parity corpus, a number this file had already corrected to 1,547. **Measure last, from the tool a reader would run, and name that command beside the figure.**
- 2026-08-31 **`REF_GROUP`'s codepoint stepping is not observable, and is kept anyway.** Replacing `NextPos` with `++` on both `stringPos` and `state.TextPos` in the `RefGroup` case agreed on all 600 rows of the S21 wave *and* passed the whole ported suite, including the three astral gap tests written to catch it. The reason is structural, not a test gap: both operands of a backreference index the same string, so a code-unit walk stays in lockstep with a codepoint walk - it tests each surrogate half separately and lands on the same `span.End`. Kept as `NextPos` because upstream's `++` means one character, every other opcode in this port spells that `NextPos`, and Phase 5's fuzzy retry advances `stringPos` on its own, where the lockstep argument stops holding. **The code carries this reasoning as a comment, because nothing we can run would catch a future reader who deletes it.** The mirror case, `GROUP_EXISTS` testing `Count > 0` instead of `Current >= 0`, is also unobservable and for a similar reason: `UnsaveCapture` decrements `Count` on the same backtrack that restores `Current`, so the two only part company for a branch-reset group.
- 2026-08-31 **A review subagent runs in the working tree and can dirty it, so the pre-commit `git status --porcelain` read is load-bearing.** S21's first reviewer left `upstream/regex/__pycache__` behind, which dirties the submodule, and regenerated `tests/parity-baseline.json` - a file it had no business writing. Neither is visible in a test run or in the ratchet's verdict, and both would have shipped in the commit. Brief a reviewer to clean up after itself and to leave the baseline alone. **The near-miss on top of that one is worth its own line: the regenerated baseline read `passingCount` 4309 against 4416 passing tests, which looked exactly like a stale figure and was written up as one before being checked. It is not stale - `Update-Baseline` stores the sorted set of *unique* ids (`PortTools.psm1:237`), so parameterised rows sharing a display id collapse and the number sits about a hundred below the raw pass count by design, as it did at S20 (4238 against 4343). Reading the function settled in one grep what the plausible story would have recorded wrongly for good.**
- 2026-08-31 **`(?(DEFINE)...)` delivered no ported tests, against S21's slice file, because all 15 of them call into the DEFINE.** The slice expected `define-groups` (15 tests) to be deliverable, on the sound reasoning that upstream compiles a DEFINE as a `GROUP_EXISTS` whose condition never holds, so its body never runs in place. That reasoning is right and the matcher now does exactly that - but every test in `RegressionsDefineTests` also spells `(?&name)`, which is `CALL_GROUP` and Phase 4's, so all 15 retag to `needs:recursion` and the delivered behaviour has no ported test at all. It is pinned by a gap test instead: `(?(DEFINE)(?<x>zzz))zzz` matches `zzz` with group `x` unset, which is only true if the body was stepped over rather than run. **Fourth scope surprise in four slices, and the first where the miss was in the *tests* rather than in the construct: reading upstream's compiler told the slice author what the engine had to do, and only reading our own ported tests would have told them what it would score.**
- 2026-08-31 **A negative control must also be re-run at a seed the slice has not used, and both numbers recorded.** S20's Control C - flipping WB5's `&&` to `||` - gave 2 divergences of 600 at its recorded seed 7, 2 at seed 12345 and **1** at seed 99. A control that catches a deliberately broken Unicode word-break rule on one row in six hundred is a thin margin, and a different seed could plausibly give none, at which point the control would appear to disprove a fault that is really there. The fresh run costs about three seconds - recording 600 boundary rows and replaying them was under two seconds measured - and it is the only way to tell a control that catches a fault from one that caught a coincidence. Deliberately NO numeric threshold: there is no evidence for where the line sits, and a rule such as WB5 genuinely applies to few generated subjects. The `port-slice` skill carries the rule; S26 re-runs every recorded control at a fresh seed at the phase close. Also learned re-running S21's Control B: quote the snippet as the file actually reads, because CSharpier breaks a ternary across three lines and the recorded one-liner appears nowhere in `Matcher.cs`.
- 2026-08-31 **Native AOT constrains a compiled fast path; it does not disqualify one, and the roadmap now says so.** The owner asked whether supporting AOT must deprive non-AOT users of `RegexOptions.Compiled`-style optimisation. Researched: no. A single assembly guards the emit path with `RuntimeFeature.IsDynamicCodeSupported` and marks it `[RequiresDynamicCode]`, which Microsoft's library guidance explicitly prefers over multi-targeting or separate packages. The real split is not AOT versus JIT but *when the pattern is known*: a source generator is AOT-compatible and gives "all the throughput performance benefits of RegexOptions.Compiled (more, in fact)" but only for compile-time literals, so runtime-constructed patterns - likely a large share for a fuzzy library - can be served only by runtime emit or by the AOT-safe middle tier of delegate threading. Trap recorded: `Expression.Compile()` is not a workaround, it silently falls back to the LINQ interpreter under AOT, exactly as `Compiled` silently becomes a no-op. **No scope change** - whether this port gets a compiled backend is still the owner's open decision, and either backend is phase-sized, since .NET maintains `RegexCompiler` and its source generator as near-1:1 twins.
- 2026-08-31 **Probe upstream with `regex.match`, never `regex.search`: its `search_start` prefilter screens start positions with predicates its own matcher does not use, so `search` and `match` can disagree.** `regex.search(r'(?ai)\p{Ll}', 'A')` is `None` where `regex.match` of the same pair spans `(0, 1)`, and `regex.search(r'(?ai)x?\p{Ll}', 'A')` - the same question with the screen defeated - spans `(0, 1)` again. S22 probed with `search` alone, concluded that `matches_PROPERTY_IGN`'s ASCII arm (`_regex.c:2972`) was not what upstream runs, rewrote the predicate to the encoding table's `has_property_ign`, and only a 1500-row wave of `match`/`fullmatch` rows showed the opposite. **The literal transliteration was right the whole time**; the detour cost most of the slice. `search_start` is the Phase 7 deferral, so until then this port's search answers what upstream's *match* answers - which is a known difference, not parity, and is pinned as such in `Gaps/Engine/CaseInsensitiveMatchingTests.cs`.
- 2026-08-31 **Upstream does not agree with itself about a cased property under the ASCII encoding, so the oracle's `case-folding` generator keeps the ASCII flag off property rows.** Three code paths reach three predicates and only one does the cased-category collapse: the dispatch switch's `matches_PROPERTY_IGN`, `count_one`'s `match_many_PROPERTY_IGN` (which calls `has_property_ign`, and does not collapse under ASCII) and `search_start`'s screen. `regex.match` against `'KsKK'`: `(?ai)\p{Ll}` spans `(0,1)`, `\p{Ll}{2}` `(0,2)`, `\p{Ll}{4}` `(0,4)`, `(\p{Ll})+` `(0,4)` - but `\p{Ll}+` spans `(0,2)` and `\p{Ll}*` spans `(0,0)`, where `(?ui)` gives `(0,4)` for all three. A greedy `*` consuming nothing where `+` consumes two is not a rule any predicate states. This port collapses in every path, which is the literal transliteration of `matches_PROPERTY_IGN` and self-consistent, so every ASCII property row would record as a divergence for as long as upstream is like this. **Candidate for an upstream report; not filed, as that needs the owner's approval.**
- 2026-08-31 **Two more Phase 7 deferrals are observable on full case folding, and the comment claiming the prefilters "answer the same" is now qualified.** Found by S22's blind review and re-verified: `regex.match('(?fi)fi', 'fı')` spans `(0, 2)` where this port finds nothing, because a whole pattern that is one `STRING_FLD` goes through `locate_required_string` into `string_search_fld`, which compares with `same_char_ign_turkic` (`_regex.c:6687`) - so the dotless i folds with `i` there and nowhere else in upstream; splitting the string (`(?fi)(f)i`) makes upstream answer `None` and agree with us. And `regex.match('(?fi).*ẖẛ', 'ẖṡ')` is `None` where this port spans `(0, 2)`, because upstream's `GREEDY_REPEAT_ONE` retreat fast path (`:16041`) clamps by a folded length it recomputes from already-folded pattern values, over-counts and gives up early; `(?fi)x?ẖẛ` and the bare `(?fi)ẖẛ` both span `(0, 2)`. Both are pinned as known differences. **So the deferrals are not uniformly transparent: they are transparent for the case-sensitive opcodes and not for the folding ones.**
- 2026-08-31 **A negative control that will not fire after three widenings is a boundary of the generator, and the honest move is to record it and name what does catch the fault.** S22's control D - `REF_GROUP_FLD` comparing the captured character raw instead of folded - gave 0, 4 and 1 divergences of 600 at three seeds, after doubled subjects, a `folded-backref` shape and subject-aware group selection were each added to try to make it bite. The reason is structural: only 1 of 19 FULLCASE backreference rows that match at all has a captured character that expands on folding, because the shape, the flag, the alphabet and the repetition all have to line up at once. What does catch it deterministically is the gap tests - with the control applied, three named tests fail every run. **Recorded in the closing notes as "do not trust the wave for this opcode", which is worth more than a fourth widening that might have bought one more row.** The three widenings that did work are kept, each with its own before/after numbers in a comment beside it.
- 2026-09-01 **A negative control's number is only valid for the code and generator it ran against, so every control gets one final re-run before the commit.** Verifying S22, its Control A - `SameCharIgn`, loop start 1 to 2 - was recorded at 15 divergences of 600 at seed 7 and 16 at seed 4242; the committed code gives **42 and 23**. B and C were out by 1 to 3. Only Control D reproduced exactly (0, 4, 1), and D is the one whose notes describe three later attempts to widen the generator - so it was the control run last, which is the entire explanation. The generator kept growing, and the blind review's own fixes land after everything else, so a control run mid-slice measures a generator that no longer exists by the end. The wave generator was ruled out as a cause: recording the same seed twice gives byte-identical output. No engine defect - every control fires at least as hard as claimed, the ratchet was green and the committed wave agreed at 2700 rows. S22's notes are corrected in place with the originals kept in brackets, and the `port-slice` skill now requires the final re-run. Third instance of the same lesson after S18 and S19: evidence recorded before the work finished is not evidence about the work.
- 2026-09-01 **`Sort-Object -Unique -CaseSensitive` is still culture-sensitive, and it had been quietly dropping 51 tests from the parity baseline.** `-CaseSensitive` was added at S09 to stop `Resolves(ABC)` and `Resolves(abc)` collapsing, and it fixes that - but the comparer underneath is still the culture one, which also folds compatibility characters, so `X.Folds(K)` and `X.Folds(K)` (U+212A KELVIN SIGN) collapse too. Reproduced in three lines of PowerShell: two ids in, one out, and the same under `-Culture InvariantCulture`. Found by S23's blind review *in S23's own regenerated baseline*, where the `(?i)[a-z]` rows of `CaseInsensitiveMatchingTests` swapped which of the pair was listed - so which test the ratchet watched was changing from run to run. Replaced with a `SortedSet[string]` over `StringComparer.Ordinal`, which is what `Test-Ratchet` already used at the reading end for the same reason; the baseline's unique count went **4594 to 4645**. Two Pester tests pin it, one for the collision and one for the `$null`-id regression the cast to `[string[]]` introduced (a `$null` becomes `""`, which the ratchet then reports as `Missing` for ever). **The lesson beyond this bug: a de-duplicating comparer is a silent coverage sink, and the only thing that made it visible was an unrelated slice regenerating the file and reading the diff.**
- 2026-09-01 **Reverse matching cost far less than its slice file scoped, because S16 and S19 had already ported the direction plumbing.** S23 listed `state->reverse`, the reversed search advance, reversed `get_limits` semantics, backward codepoint stepping, and the REV rows of both `*_REPEAT_ONE` backtrack sub-switches. All of it was already in place: S16 ported `state->reverse`, `PrevPos`, the reversed `next_match_2` advance, the reversed `at_end` and `do_exact_match`'s reversed width check when it ported the spine; S19 wrote the `*_REPEAT_ONE` backtrack cases against `node.Next2.Test!.Step`, which is `-1` for a reversed repeat, so they needed no change at all; `get_limits`'s boundary arithmetic is direction-neutral and was already `MatchState.ClampIndex`. What was actually left was the dispatch cases and two counting helpers. **A slice file written a phase ahead scopes what upstream contains, not what earlier slices have already taken from it - read the port before believing the estimate.** The reverse of the same coin, and the more expensive half: the slice claimed 49 `right-to-left` tests and delivered 11, because 34 of them call `FuzzyRegex.Matches`, `Split` or `Replace` and were tagged for the direction rather than for the API they need.
- 2026-09-01 **Under `(?r)` a backreference only runs if it is written before its group.** Reverse execution takes the sequence from the right, so `(?r)(.)\1` reaches `\1` while the group is still empty and never matches - `regex.match(r'(?r)(.)\1', 'abb')` is `None`, and so is every `(?r)(x)\1` shape probed. The working form is `(?r)\1(.)`, which is what upstream's own `test_recursive` reverse rows use. Worth recording because the failing shape is the one a reader writes first, and it looks exactly like a `REF_GROUP_REV` bug: S23's first six backreference probes all came back `None` and had to be re-derived before any of them could be pinned as a test.
- 2026-09-01 **A negative control aimed at a rare cell is evidence about the generator before it is evidence about the code.** S23's control D - `STRING_FLD_REV` reading the subject's folding from the front - fired on **1** row of 600 with the reverse wave drawing its fold rows from the ordinary `case-folding` generator. That cell is exactly the one upstream's 2026.5.9 fix was about ("Reverse matching with full unicode casefolding could lead to out-of-range string indexes"), and the slice had been told to probe it hardest. The reason it was unreachable is multiplicative: the fold share, the FULLCASE coin, the shape draw and the alphabet rotation each cut it. Adding `_generate_reverse_fold`, which forces all four, took it to 21 and 20 at two seeds. **The moment to widen a generator is while the mutant is still planted - the number before and after is the only proof the widening bought anything, and it cannot be recovered later.** Contrast S22's control D, which would not fire after three widenings and was correctly recorded as a boundary rather than chased further.
- 2026-09-01 **A count-style argument whose convention is inverted against upstream must be translated at *both* ends, and S24 translated one.** Upstream's `count=0` is "no limit" and its `count=-1` is "no replacements at all" (`regex.subn('a','b','aaaaa',count=-1)` is `('aaaaa', 0)`, measured); this surface spells those `-1` and `0`, as S01 decided. The oracle's consumer mapped only `0 => -1`, so a recorded row carrying a negative count asked the two engines opposite questions and reported a correct port as RED. Found by S24's blind review with a hand-written `-Rows` row. Fixed to a three-way switch, pinned in `OracleWaveTests`, and `SUB_COUNTS` gained a `-1` so the generator exercises the end that only a hand-written row could reach before. **S25's `maxsplit` has the same shape** - the lesson is that an inverted convention has two ends and the second one is invisible until something asks for it.
- 2026-09-01 **`min_width` is a codepoint count, so a comparison against it is only safe in code units when it errs towards *not* taking a shortcut.** `do_exact_match`'s width check may use code units because it only ever fails early, and units are never fewer than codepoints; `pattern_subx`'s too-short-subject shortcut may not, because it *returns* early and does so before the replacement template is compiled. On an astral subject that difference is observable: `regex.subn('..', r'\g<bad', '\U0001F600')` is `('\U0001f600', 0)` upstream, where comparing 2 against the two code units made the shortcut miss and the port compiled and rejected a template upstream never looks at. Found by S24's blind review. The general rule: **when porting an early-out, ask which way it errs before reusing an existing width comparison** - the same arithmetic is sound in one direction and a divergence in the other.
- 2026-09-01 **Upstream answers the same invalid template two different ways, and both are the port's answer too.** `regex.sub('x', r'\1', 'x')` raises `regex.error("invalid group reference")` from `get_sub_replacement`; `regex.match('x','x').expand(r'\1')` raises `IndexError("no such group")` from `get_match_replacement`. The two functions are otherwise the same check. Ported as `FuzzyRegexParseException` and `ArgumentException` respectively, which is also what the Phase 1 `SubTemplateNumericEscapeTests` and `SymbolicRefsTests` ports already asserted. Consequence for the oracle: a rejection raised *while substituting* is now a legitimate agreement, so the recorder records a `whileMatching` flag and the comparer requires the two sides to have thrown at the same point rather than assuming every recorded rejection came from `regex.compile`. Without that, every invalid-group-reference row was a false divergence.
- 2026-09-01 **A negative control that fires on zero rows is the generator's verdict, not the code's, and the widening that fixes it is only measurable while the mutant is planted.** S24's control F - the too-short shortcut comparing code units instead of codepoints - fired on **0** of 600, because seeing that cell needs an astral subject, a pattern whose codepoint width falls strictly between the subject's codepoint and code-unit counts, *and* a template the compiler rejects, all at once. A dedicated `_narrow_row` arm forcing all three took it to 21 at two seeds. Control E, the negative-subscript rule, was thin for a related reason: no pattern shape made a group capture more than once, so `{n[-1]}` and `{n[0]}` agreed by accident; adding a `(atom)+` shape and weighting the negative subscripts took it from 11 to 16 and 14. **Both numbers exist only because the control was re-run before and after the widening** - the same lesson S23 recorded, now with a case where the before-figure was zero.
- 2026-09-01 **The port was doing exactly `3n^2` character-walk steps where upstream does four machine instructions, because a UTF-16 port has to convert between a character count and a position and this one converted by walking.** Found while verifying S24 by asking why the suite had gone from 1m28s to minutes. Upstream's `LAZY_REPEAT_ONE` backtrack arm (`_regex.c:16485-16489`) computes `pos`, `available`, `max_count` and `limit` with one multiplication and two subtractions, because a CPython `str` is a fixed-width codepoint array (PEP 393, `PyUnicode_KIND` at `:18232`) so the k-th character *is* index k. A .NET `string` is UTF-16 and is not fixed width, so S19 introduced `StepBy`/`CountBetween` to convert - correctly, but as loops. The arm is re-entered once per repeat position, so four linear conversions inside an `n`-entry loop is quadratic. **Measured by counting the walk steps, which unlike a clock does not depend on machine load: 107,999,997 steps at 6,002 characters, 431,999,997 at 12,002, 1,727,999,997 at 24,002 and 6,911,999,997 at 48,002 - `3n^2` to four significant figures at every size.** The same shape is in the `GREEDY_REPEAT_ONE` arm, so `a+b` over a long run of `a`s had it too. Fixed, not deferred: it is a porting defect rather than a missing optimisation, nothing was traded for anything, and the honest place to fix one is where it was introduced. Correction to this entry's first version, which quoted 372 seconds for the worst test and 6m24 for the suite: both were measured on a loaded machine. Re-measured in isolation the test takes 69 seconds and the suite 53, and the suite's own range across runs was 53s to 3m16 - **that variance is itself the symptom, because quadratic work is what makes a suite sensitive to load in the first place.**
- 2026-09-01 **Two paths, because there are two kinds of subject: upstream's arithmetic restored for subjects with no surrogate pair, and a sampled position table for the rest.** `MatchState.OneUnitPerCharacter` is one vectorised scan for any high surrogate; when it holds, one character is one code unit and `pos + count * step` is exactly upstream's line, so BMP subjects drop to **zero** walk steps and the fast path can be reviewed against `_regex.c:16485` side by side. When it does not hold, `CharacterIndex` records the position of every 32nd character boundary, so a conversion is one array read or a binary search plus at most 31 steps of the ordinary walk. Astral subjects go from 6,911,999,997 steps and 6.25s at 48,002 characters to **2,328,005 steps and 0.034s**, and from `3n^2` to a flat 48.5 steps per character. The table is one `int` per 32 characters, about 3% of an all-astral subject, built lazily on the first conversion and only as far as `TextEnd`, so a pattern with no single-character repeat pays nothing and `Match(hugeSubject, 0, 10)` does not index the whole subject. Suite 53s to 4.4s; against upstream we are now ~68x on BMP and ~227x on astral, where astral was ~41,000x and growing with the square. The stride is a measured tuning knob and not a correctness property - halving it halves the residual walk exactly, but below 32 the clock stops improving, and setting it to 33 leaves all 5,703 tests passing. Rank-and-select is the documented upgrade path if a measurement ever asks. **The background, all six options with their pros and cons, and the measurements behind the choice are in `docs/plan/2026-09-01-quadratic-conversion-decisions.md`**, kept as the record of why these two files exist. **What this does not buy: upstream costs the same for astral as for BMP, and nothing short of changing how .NET stores strings reaches that.**
- 2026-09-01 **The reason a sampled table beat carrying the position around, which is what Java does, was testability rather than size.** Java's `Curly` (`Pattern.java:4522-4622`) carries the UTF-16 index and the character count side by side and never converts one into the other, which is the structurally cleaner answer and would have fixed astral subjects too. It lost on two grounds. It is not smaller - `available` still needs the arm's upper bound cached per repeat, with a rule for when that cache goes stale, because one of the arm's own branches rewrites the repeat's start. And **it cannot be checked**: its state exists only part-way through a backtracking search, so there is no reference to compare it against, and a stale cached position gives a wrong answer rather than a crash. `CharacterIndex` is a pure function of the subject and the walk it replaces is a complete reference implementation of that same function, so it is compared against that walk over every string of up to four symbols from an eight-symbol alphabet - 4,681 subjects, every position, every count, and for shorter ones every pair of slice bounds. **That test found two real defects in the first draft within minutes of existing**, one of them a `CountBetween(p, p)` that answered 1 instead of 0 inside a surrogate pair. Java can afford its shape because its loop was positional from the start; ours is a line-for-line port of C that converts because C could do it for free. The general rule: **when two designs both work, prefer the one whose correctness has an executable oracle, even if the other is structurally tidier.**
- 2026-09-01 **`NextPos` and `PrevPos` disagree about where a character starts at positions above `TextEnd`, and no upstream answer exists because the question cannot arise there.** `NextPos` refuses to pair a high and low surrogate when `pos + 1 >= TextEnd`; `PrevPos` pairs regardless of any bound. So above `TextEnd` a forward walk sees two characters where a backward walk sees one. Found by the exhaustive `CharacterIndex` test, which swept positions the engine cannot reach and failed until the sweep was narrowed. Provably unreachable from the engine: `NextPos` is only called at `pos < TextEnd`, and for the two to disagree at `pos` you need `pos >= TextEnd - 1` while `PrevPos(pos + 2)` also needs `pos <= TextEnd - 2`. So it is a latent trap rather than a live bug, and it is left alone deliberately - it is a semantic decision about what `beginning` and `length` mean when they split a surrogate pair, upstream indexes by codepoint so it has no opinion to port, and bundling a semantic change into a performance fix makes both harder to review and to revert. **Carried to S26's symbol accounting.** Recorded because the only thing that surfaced it was a test deliberately asking questions outside the domain, which is worth doing again.
- 2026-09-01 **Three ported tests asserted the opposite of what upstream asserts, and only a working engine could reveal it.** All three were written in Phase 1 against a suite that could not run them, and all three failed against a correct S25 engine. (1) `WordBoundaryTests` #6 dropped the trailing `""` from a `(?V1w)\b` split; `pattern_split` appends the segment after the last match "even if empty" (`_regex.c:22330`) and upstream's own assertion carries it (`test_regex.py:1566`). (2) and (3) `RegressionsKeepMarkerTests` #165 and #168 asserted on `m.Value` where upstream's `findall` yields **the one group's** text when the pattern has exactly one (`:22440`) - which inverted the point of the upstream assertion, that `\K` shrinks the match *without* shrinking the group: `regex.findall(r'(\w\w\K\w\w)', 'abcdefgh')` is `['abcd', 'efgh']` where the matches are `'cd'` and `'gh'`. Each fixed with the measurement quoted at the assertion. The general rule: **a skipped test is unverified, not merely unrun** - a Phase 1 port of an assertion is a hypothesis about upstream until something executes it, and the slice that un-skips it is where that hypothesis gets tested. Expect a handful per slice for the rest of the phase, and prove each against the real module before touching it.
- 2026-09-01 **`state->version_0` and `state->visible_captures` are both written and never read in this release, so there is nothing to port for either.** S25's slice file expected a V0/V1 difference in how a split treats a zero-width match, and S24's expected one in `pattern_subx`. Neither exists: `state_init` sets `version_0` at `:18482` and no other line of `_regex.c` reads it; `visible_captures` is set at `:18307` and likewise unread. Measured for the split case over four V0/V1 pattern pairs, every one identical. Both pinned by a gap test naming the mechanism, so the next slice that reads an upstream comment about "the new behaviour" does not go hunting for a code path that was removed. **The general rule: a field being set is not evidence that it is read**, and `grep` for the reads is cheaper than any amount of reasoning about the comment beside the write.
- 2026-09-01 **Upstream writes the same scan loop twice - once as `findall`, once as the scanner - and this port has one, because the two were shown to agree rather than assumed to.** Only `pattern_findall` (`:22415`) carries the `slice_start <= text_pos <= slice_end` guard; `scanner_search_or_match` (`:20874`) has none and relies on `do_match` failing. Folding them is exactly the kind of tidying that costs a future sync, so it needed evidence: `regex.findall` and `[m[0] for m in regex.finditer(...)]` are element for element identical on nine subject/pattern pairs, overlapped and not, including the reverse and zero-width cases where the guard is the only thing that ends the scan. Kept as `Iteration.Scan` with the guard, since the guarded version is the one whose termination is visible in the code. **The rule this instance illustrates: two upstream functions may be merged only when a measurement says they answer alike, and the measurement goes in the comment.**
- 2026-09-01 **A wall-clock ceiling is not a sound complexity assertion inside a parallel test suite, and a ratio between two input sizes is.** S25's linearity guard for the iteration surface first asserted "under 5 seconds" and failed the ratchet on its first run at 7.5s - not because the scan was slow but because the suite's other 5,700 tests were running alongside it; the same three scans took 2.1s alone. Rewritten to `[NotInParallel]` plus a ratio: 468ms at 480,000 code units and 970ms at 960,000, a ratio of 2.07, against 10.8 for a scan that builds a `MatchState` per match. A ratio does not move with machine speed, so the threshold of 5 has the same margin on a slow CI box as here. Two corollaries worth carrying. **The engine's own `MatchTimeout` cannot enforce a per-scan complexity bound**, because a state carries its own start time and a per-match-state scan resets the budget on every match - which is why this needed timing at all rather than the `RepeatTests` trick of leaning on the timeout. And **the failure this guards is invisible to correctness tests**: a per-match-state `Matches` passes every one of the 169 tests S25 un-skipped and takes 209 seconds where the shared-state scan takes 0.14.
- 2026-09-01 **An oracle row whose answer is a sequence has to be compared as a sequence, or the harness agrees with a scan that is wrong in the only way a scan can be wrong.** S25 added `finditer`, `finditer-overlapped` and `split` operations to the wave, recording the whole list of matches (or split pieces, `null` slots included) rather than one. A row comparing only the first match, or comparing a count, agrees with a scan that finds the right matches in the wrong order, stops one match early, or repeats a zero-width match at one position - and those are precisely the defects an iteration port introduces. Confirmed by mutation: emitting matches in reverse order diverges on 243 of 600 rows, and dropping the trailing empty split part on 103. **The general shape for the rest of the port: the recorded outcome must be the whole answer at the granularity the caller sees it**, which for `Match` was groups-and-captures, for `sub` was the `(text, count)` pair, and for a scan is the ordered list.
