The fix is one conjunct, upstream's own second term dropped.
- 2026-09-14 **A fuzzy-matching divergence is judged on upstream's own definition plus self-refutation, because there is no second engine to run - and the ABSENCE was measured, not asserted.** Amendment 16 asks for a real run of a second engine, so `tools/probes/pcre2-has-no-fuzzy-matching.py` runs one: pcre2 0.7.1 over libpcre2 10.47 does not merely lack fuzzy matching, it reads the suffix as LITERAL TEXT - `compile(r'(?:x){e<=3}').match('xyz')` is None and `.match('x{e<=3}')` is (0, 7) - so a comparison built on it would answer confidently and wrongly, and only `(?b)` fails loudly. Perl and .NET have no approximate matching; TRE and agrep do and implement neither upstream's syntax nor its ranking. **An unavailable limb of amendment 16 has to be evidenced like any other**, because a maintainer who reads "no second engine" as "nobody checked" dismisses the report. What replaces it: `BESTMATCH` is documented as a RANKING flag (`upstream/README.rst:592`), so it chooses among the flagless engine's candidates and cannot destroy them all - and the same engine answers the match the moment the flag is deleted. Any future fuzzy-only entry should be judged the same way rather than parked for want of a second engine.

Both alternatives were tried and rejected with evidence. Row-keying, which `bestmatch-loses-a-partial` uses, cannot work here because this family's rows renumber with the ROW COUNT as well as the seed - the seed-7 gate draws different questions at 6000 and at 6300. - 2026-09-14 **`bestmatch-loses-a-candidate` is deliberately wider than one mechanism, and the width was measured rather than argued.** It asserts "this port answered a candidate upstream's own flagless engine answers", which covers ledger entry 12 (mechanism established to the line) and entry 13 (mechanism not established) alike - and it retired seed 20260914 row 76345, an entry-13 instance that had been diverging unclassified. What it cannot discriminate is a port that IGNORES `(?b)`, since that also answers the flagless answer. Control S46-C measures the cost: a stops-ranking engine leaves the wave at 0/0/1 divergences against 0/0/0 and fails 5 of 5,947 tests, so the suite is the instrument for the flag's ranking and the wave is the instrument for upstream losing a candidate.
- 2026-09-14 **The full default oracle wave has been RED at HEAD since before S46, and the row COUNT reshuffles the wave as much as the seed does.** Re-consuming the three identical recorded waves with HEAD's engine in a git worktree gives 3 + 2 + 10 = 15 divergences at 6000 rows; S45's closing notes record the default wave green with `expected` 4/1/2 at 6300 rows, where the full default list gives 63/67/42 at 6000 and 66 at 6300 on seed 7, so that figure cannot be the full list. **VERIFICATION rule 7a needs a second half: a wave counts at three seeds AND at the row count the gate is stated in.** A wave "green at 6300" says nothing about the same seed at 6000.
- 2026-09-14 **A worktree cannot record an oracle wave, only consume one.** `git worktree add` does not populate submodules, so `tools/record-oracle.py` dies on `upstream/pyproject.toml` before it reaches a row. The HEAD-baseline technique is therefore: record in the main checkout, copy `wave-<seed>.jsonl` into the worktree's `TestResults/oracle/wave.jsonl`, and run `tools/run-oracle.ps1 -SkipRecord` there. Worth knowing before the next slice spends a turn on the traceback.
- 2026-09-14 **An upstream crash that blocks a whole oracle cell is worked around by not asking the faulting QUESTION, not by refusing the row.** Reading `Match.fuzzy_changes` on a POSIX fuzzy match that spent an error kills the recorder, and from S43 the `interactions` generator refused to draw POSIX on any fuzzy row rather than lose whole waves to it. S46 lifted that: the recorder records `fuzzyCounts` and omits `fuzzyChanges` on a POSIX pattern, and both sides render `fuzzy=(s,i,d)[changes unavailable upstream]`, so the span, the groups and the error COUNTS are compared where before nothing was. The cost is the change positions, which upstream has no answer for anyway. **The guard belongs at the one function every recorded match passes through** - `_describe_match`, which the single-match door, `finditer`, a `(*SKIP)` substitution's `subMatches` and `_anchored_scan` all call - because a missed door kills a wave rather than failing a test. Keyed off `compiled.flags & POSIX`, never off a prediction that the match will spend an error: `(?p)(?:abc){e<=1}` over `'abcd'` looks exact and faults, so no predicate over the pattern or the subject is safe. The same shape applies to any future upstream fault that is confined to one attribute.

Deleting the two stale files gave 40 / 29 / 37. A control compared against a baseline recorded by different generator code measures nothing, and nothing in the runner's output says which wave it used.
- 2026-09-14 **Never rewrite a `.cs` file whole from Python on Windows: text mode turns LF into CRLF and CSharpier then refuses the file, which reads as an unrelated IDE0055 storm.** `pathlib.Path.write_text` translates `\n` to `os.linesep`, so a bulk `read_text`/`replace`/`write_text` over a CSharpier-formatted file reverses its line endings; `dotnet csharpier check` says "The file contained different line endings than formatting it would result in" but the BUILD says `error IDE0055: Fix formatting` at a dozen lines the edit never touched, which sends you hunting in the wrong place. It cost two failed builds and a wasted control run in S46. Use the `Edit` tool, or pass `newline=''`, and run `dotnet csharpier format` before any control run or commit. Markdown, JSON and Python files are unaffected in practice, because `.gitattributes` sets `* text=auto eol=lf` and git normalises them on commit.
- 2026-09-14 **Ledger entry 11 is not one bug with two doors but one defect class with at least FOUR mechanisms, and the reason is structural: upstream saves and restores the fuzzy COUNTS as a block and unwinds the CHANGES one item at a time, so anything that abandons a sub-attempt without backtracking through it desynchronises them.** S47 fixed A (a search restart clears the counts and leaves the list) and B (a partial match returns from inside a nested section, so the counter holds the innermost section's errors alone). C (POSIX/BESTMATCH candidates leave the list polluted or empty) and D (a lookaround under `(?e)`) are unfixed and in the ledger with their reproductions, flags included. **Both engines agree on C and D**, so no wave can see them - which is the whole argument for the new property test.
- 2026-09-14 **An invariant that holds of THIS PORT'S OWN answer is an instrument the oracle cannot be: a wave compares against upstream, so a bug both engines share reports as agreement.** `OracleWaveTests.Our_own_answers_never_contradict_themselves` asserts, over every fuzzy match of every row of a whole wave and every match of every scan, that each change list holds exactly as many positions as its own count. It found ledger entry 11's mechanism D on its first run, at seed 4242, on a row where this port and upstream answer identically. **Reach for a self-consistency property wherever the ported behaviour has an internal invariant**; it costs one pass over a wave already recorded.
- 2026-09-14 **Fixing an inherited bug upstream reaches on ordinary rows creates a divergence family too large to key on rows and too uniform to key on a predicate, and the answer is a SECOND RECORDED QUESTION rather than a wider predicate.** S47's fix for ledger entry 11 mechanisms A and B leaves the default wave diverging on 39 rows (B) plus 19 rows (A) per 18,000, measured at 6000 rows and three seeds. Upstream's leaked change positions are structurally indistinguishable from a port that computed a position wrongly, so a predicate reading "same counts, different positions" would swallow exactly the defect the oracle exists to catch. The design that works, and the shape `bestmatchFreeOutcome` and `searchOnlyPartial` already have: ask upstream the same row again ANCHORED at the span it reported, where no earlier attempt exists to leak from, and account for a divergence when this port equals upstream's own leak-free answer.
- 2026-09-14 **A second recorded question is not a universal answer, and the previous entry's design covers ONE of the two mechanisms it was written for.** S47 sitting 2 measured it: re-asking upstream a PARTIAL row anchored at the span it reported is the IDENTICAL question and returns the identical wrong counts, so mechanism B gets nothing from it. B is judgeable without a second question anyway - upstream's counts are componentwise no larger than this port's and its positions are a per-kind PREFIX of this port's, which is what a change stack truncated to a wrong total looks like and is not what an engine computing different positions looks like (35 of 35 rows carrying positions, 0 failing). **Before building a recorded discriminator, check which rows it can actually discriminate.**
- 2026-09-14 **An anchored re-ask cannot be asked at all of a fuzzy section inside a LOOKAHEAD, of a `\K`, or of a scan's second match at one position, and the honest response is a documented weak arm rather than a wider predicate.** `match(pos=start, endpos=end)` cuts the lookahead off, reports a start the attempt did not begin at, and cannot reach the second match - eight of the 23 diverging matches of a three-seed 6000-row gate, in four rows. Four asking strategies were scored: `match(pos,endpos)` answers 20 of 38 matches, `search(pos)`/reversed `search(endpos)` 25, and the union of all four still leaves 13, so trying several buys ONE row of 19. One strategy, and the entry says out loud that its second arm accepts this port's positions with nothing to hold them to, about 1.3 rows per 126,000-row seed.
- 2026-09-14 **`Throw<Exception>` on a test that also passes a `MatchTimeout` cannot tell the bound from the clock, and three of `FuzzyRecursionTests`' assertions had that shape.** A `RegexMatchTimeoutException` from the tests' own 30-second budget satisfies it just as well as the engine's stack limit, so a regression that merely made the engine slow would have passed. They now name `InvalidOperationException` and match its message; mutating the message to a sentinel fails exactly those three. **Assert the exception TYPE and message wherever the test also supplies a deadline.**
- 2026-09-14 **PCRE2 answers ledger entry 14's mechanism with a NAMED match-time guard rather than a memory bound, which settles "correctness fix or resource bound" as BOTH, in that order.** `PCRE2_ERROR_RECURSELOOP` - "nested recursion at the same subject position" - fires in microseconds on a self-recursive call whose body need not consume, while the recursion that does progress still answers (0, 4) (`tools/probes/pcre2-bounds-an-unbounded-recursion.py`, libpcre2 10.47). PCRE2 has no fuzzy matching, so it was shown the same mechanism with the fuzzy section replaced by an optional atom. Porting the guard is not a transcription: it is positional rather than a progress proof, so a shape re-entering one position where a different branch would still have succeeded changes answer, and it must be measured against a wave before it lands.
- 2026-09-14 **Independent verifier added to the slice procedure; ledger entry 13 ruled outcome (d); two corrective slices authored.** An Opus audit of S44-S46 (`.scratch/audit-s44-s46.md`, findings copied into S47b) found the four-limb evidence standard of amendment 16 had no mechanism for limb (d): the skill ran a blind review and nothing else, so no slice since S44 had a verifier. Owner approved a verifier step (skill, step 3 after the review) - a fresh subagent that re-runs every quoted probe and number from the committed files and reports CONFIRMED / DIFFERENT / COULD NOT RUN; unconfirmed claims go, unreproducible pins are not kept. On entry 13 the owner accepted the recommendation: strong evidence, mechanism not established to the line, so the pin is kept but narrowed to the measured rows and the minimised shape (S47b), and the mechanism is traced in upstream's C with a debug build (S47c; the owner authorised installing the compiler). The audit's other findings - a Turkic pin with a recorded false positive, S45's evidence only in gitignored scratch, three notes claiming unmeasured numbers - are S47b's scope. S47b and S47c run before S48.

- 2026-09-14 (S47): ledger entry 14 is fixed here rather than reproduced - PCRE2's positional recursion guard, refusing a call that re-enters a group where a call of it is already open. **It fails the PATH, not the MATCH as PCRE2 does**, because refusing one infinite path cannot cost an answer another path reaches and it keeps answers PCRE2 throws away (`(?P<g1>(?:ab)?(?&g1)?)` over `'abab'` is an error there and (0, 4) here). The 1GB backtracking bound stays as the backstop for the branching the guard cannot see.
- 2026-09-14 (S47): the guard's state rides a stack with the SSTACK DEPTH of each open call, not just a set keyed on the call. A `(*PRUNE)`/`(*SKIP)` truncates the backtracking stack without touching the saved stack, so an enclosing atomic group, lookaround or conditional can discard an open call's frame with neither backtrack arm running; found by S47's blind review, which reproduced a match upstream finds and this port lost.
- 2026-09-14 (S47): `INTERACTION_FUZZY_WRAPPERS` draws a self-recursive call round a fuzzy section again. The cost objection went with the guard (microseconds, not a second and a gigabyte) and the rows upstream CAN answer are the only instrument that shows the guard does not touch them.

Applied: `bestmatch-loses-a-candidate` is keyed on its recorded questions as well as on upstream's flagless answer - four when the narrowing landed, nine after the five wave rows it turned red were each judged with the probe and added, so it no longer classifies entry 13's family (seed 20260914 row 76345 among them) or a port that ignored `(?b)` altogether; `bestmatch-loses-a-partial` gains the minimised shape as an example row, so the staleness alarm runs it. The cost is stated rather than hidden: a new draw of entry 12's own mechanism now reds the wave until someone judges it.
- 2026-09-14 (S47b): **the `turkic-default-folding` false positive S45 recorded as unclosable IS closable - by asking about the PATTERN's route to the letter rather than about the two answers.** Spelling a letter out is the one route that consults no case data, so a pattern that spells the letter its answer covers cannot be diverging over a `T` row. S45 looked in the answers, where there is nothing: a fabricated total failure and a real one are the same two answers. The refusal also needs the pattern to hold no class, property, named list or `\N{...}`, measured on seed-31337 `interactions` row 6150 and minimised to `match('ıı', 'ı[A-Z]', I)` - a real family row whose pattern spells `ı` as well. A dot is deliberately not such a construct: it matches any character whatever the case data says.
- 2026-09-14 (S47b): **an oracle classification predicate gets the same instruments as engine code, because five reasoned drafts of one were wrong and every instrument caught a different one.** `turkic-default-folding`'s discriminator went through five versions in one slice: "the pattern does not spell the covered letter" (killed by gate row 50168), "a `T`-row pairing" alone (killed by `interactions` row 6150), "a pairing or a LIST of folding constructs" (killed by gate row 52004, where the construct is a backreference), "a pairing or `[` or `\`, over the pattern text" (killed by the blind review: `(?i)` puts an `i` in the text), and "blank the flag groups first" (killed by the second blind pass: a group name or a comment does it too). What holds is a pairing test over the pattern with every `(?...` HEADER blanked by ONE rule, plus `[`, `\` and the four named spellings `(?P=`, `(?P>`, `(?&`, `(?R`. The rows that killed each draft are pinned in two constants named for what they assert, iterated whole, because the last finding was a doc comment off by one from counting positions in a single list.
- 2026-09-14 (S47c): **ledger entry 13's mechanism is established to the line, and it is a LEAK ACROSS TWO ATTEMPTS rather than anything about ranking.** `RE_OP_SKIP` narrows `slice_start` (`_regex.c:14555`, or `slice_end` at `:14553` when reversed) during the NORMAL attempt; `do_match:18170` restores `text_pos` alone before the partial retry; `do_best_fuzzy_match:17625`'s loop guard `state->slice_start <= start_pos && start_pos <= state->slice_end` is then false for the caller's own start position, the body never runs, and `status` keeps the `RE_ERROR_FAILURE` from `:17599` - the partial is never attempted. `do_simple_fuzzy_match` is handed the same leaked slice and answers anyway because it has no such guard, which is the whole of why `(?b)` looks like a filter. Traced in a `/Od /Zi` MSVC build of the pinned source, `fprintf`-instrumented; entry 13 is no longer amendment 16 outcome (d). The old hypothesis named the right guard for the wrong reason - it blamed the retry's `start_pos = state->match_pos`, and the loop is refused on its first iteration.
- 2026-09-14 (S47c): **this port has carried the fix since S40b without knowing what it was fixing, and that is now evidence rather than luck.** `Matcher.cs:10098-10100` restores `SliceStart`/`SliceEnd` beside `TextPos`, chosen on self-refutation grounds; it IS candidate fix A, and a build of upstream carrying it answers, under `(?b)`, this port's answer IN FULL on all five judged rows - 77937 included, which retires that row's "only the span agrees" caveat. The flagless answer was only ever a stand-in for "what upstream would say without the defect"; a repaired build is the better yardstick and the port agrees with it.
- 2026-09-14 (S47c): **a pin widens to a row whose recorded QUESTION can be reproduced, not to every row the mechanism explains.** Seed 20260914 row 76345 is explained - measured on stock and both fixed builds - and was still not added, because its wave file has been overwritten and a single-generator re-run at its seed does not redraw it (the generators share one RNG stream), so the only honest key would have been a guessed `flags`. What was added instead is the minimised REVERSED shape, the `slice_end` arm's only small witness. Recovering 76345 needs the full 21-generator 6000-row gate at seed 20260914.
- 2026-09-14 (S47c): **an investigation slice gives a blind pass far more purchase than an engine slice, and should be budgeted accordingly.** Five findings raised, FIVE reproduced, against the standing rate of about one in five. The output of such a slice is claims about measurements, and a reviewer holding the same builds can check each one directly: two false scope claims ("rows 1-6 are forward-only", "None at every door"), a probe that did not measure what three documents cited it for, a wrong "unconditionally", an off-by-one line reference. The third is the one the verifier step exists for - both rows had been measured only in scratch scripts about to be deleted.
- 2026-09-14 **Thread safety gets its own slice, S52b, and spec amendment 23 (owner request).** The spec promised immutable, shareable patterns and listed thread-safety smoke tests; nothing tested it and no slice carried it. The owner noted the .NET considerations differ from Python's: upstream's engine is GIL-free in the match loop (`release_GIL`, `concurrent=True`) so pattern immutability is upstream's own rule, but its Python-level object is GIL-protected and the port's is not, and .NET callers are concurrent by default. Order of weight in the slice: structural immutability by reflection with a proven allowlist, static-state audit, debug pool wrapper against double returns, deterministic parallel stress test, `Match` immutability, documented contract. Tests are permanent and constrain Phase 7. Runs after S52.
- 2026-09-14 **Version 1 is the default (owner decision; S50b, spec amendment 24).** Grounded by running regex 2026.9.10, CPython 3.14.6 and .NET 10 on the README's four listed differences: two no longer exist (V0 tracks `re` 3.7+ on zero-width `split`/`sub` and on inline-flag scoping), leaving nested sets with set operations and full case-folding. No compatibility debt here, both are the better behaviour, and .NET users already expect set arithmetic from `Regex`'s `[a-z-[aeiou]]`. Costs: one constant, an explicit per-row default on the oracle's port side (wave headers already carry `defaultVersion`), a `Version0` pin for the 129 ported constructions that assume upstream's default, a helpful error for `[` inside a set, docs. Placed before S51 so timeouts, thread safety, oracle hardening and benchmarks measure the shipped default.
- 2026-09-14 **`docs/DIVERGENCES.md` is the running record of deliberate differences from upstream, and Phase 8 writes the user documentation from it (owner request).** Seeded from DECISIONS and the spec: Version 1 default (24), no `concurrent` parameter, per-call timeouts and cancellation (22), the `Regex`-shaped API with UTF-16 indices and `(Index, Length)` captures, `maxSplits = -1`, upstream-style replacement templates, the exception mapping, the `Match` members not surfaced, cost ranking for `(?e)`/`(?b)`, every fixed inherited bug, PORTMAP's not-ported table, and the oddities kept on purpose so nobody "fixes" them. The skill now requires a slice that diverges to append in the same commit; the ROADMAP's Phase 8 row points at the file.
- 2026-09-14 **No `net11.0` target for now (owner, on the orchestrator's recommendation).** Researched on Microsoft's pages the day of asking: .NET 11 RC1 shipped 2026-09-08 with go-live, GA expected November, support type not yet published; .NET 10 is LTS to 2028-11-14. RC1's runtime notes are JIT and runtime work (bounds-check and redundant-branch elimination, switch folding, generic virtual devirtualisation, SIMD cost modelling, Arm64 `IndexOfAny`, NativeAOT dispatch, Runtime Async) which a `net10.0` assembly receives when hosted on .NET 11; the libraries notes add `RegexOptions.AnyNewLine` and `Rune` overloads, nothing this engine calls. Multi-targeting now would cost an RC SDK in CI, a second test matrix and tooling churn mid-Phase 6 for no API. S54 records baselines under both runtimes from the one build; Phase 8 revisits.

- 2026-09-14 (S48): **ledger entry 5 has a SIXTH door, this port shared it, and it is fixed here.** `do_best_fuzzy_match`'s walk guard (`upstream/src/_regex.c:17625`) holds `start_pos` between the LIVE `slice_start`/`slice_end`, and `init_match` - this report's own subject - does not reset them; `start_pos` is set to `state->match_pos`, so a `(*SKIP)` that consumed anything leaves `slice_start` above it and the `(?b)` walk ends on its first successful candidate. `(?b)(?:a(*SKIP)b){e<=1}` over `'axab'` answers a one-error match where the same compiled pattern's own `match(2)` finds a PERFECT one. Judged with no second engine, because PCRE2 has no fuzzy matching: the verb's own definition forbids only attempts BELOW the skip point and the lost candidate is above it; `(*PRUNE)` and the deleted verb both give the perfect match. `Matcher.DoBestFuzzyMatch` now restores the caller's slice before each candidate in both passes.
- 2026-09-14 (S48): **the entry-5 fix does NOT go in `InitMatch`, although the ledger's own proposed fix says `init_match`.** `DoEnhancedFuzzyMatch` and `DoBestFuzzyMatch`'s widened-slice fallback narrow the slice deliberately and then call it, so a reset there would throw their narrowing away and change `(?e)`. Save and restore around each candidate instead. The ledger entry now carries the correction, because a report that proposes a fix which breaks a neighbouring function is worse than one that proposes none.
- 2026-09-14 (S48): **a negative control that fires zero can be a finding about the generator rather than about the fix, and this one is.** S48-A restores the truncated `(?b)` walk and moves NOTHING across 24,000 `interactions` rows at seeds 7, 4242, 31337 and 99991 - identical agree, expected and diverge counts, mutated against unmutated - while the same fault changes upstream's answer on 1,861 of 11,340 shapes in a hand-built alphabet. The generators compose `(?b)` with `(*SKIP)` and never where the truncation decides anything. Recorded as S48-A with that number, handed to S52, and what covers the fix meanwhile is the permanent test plus five rows in `ExpectedDivergences`.
- 2026-09-14 (S48): **S31-A, S31-B and S31-C are repaired rather than left in the owed-maintenance list.** All three failed to resolve because S40b inserted the slice restore and its note into the very lines they mutate, and S31-C's argument gained a trailing comma. Re-anchored on the text as it now reads, they fire 10-15, 23-26 and 64-68 divergences of 600. A control that will not resolve measures nothing, and these three are the partial-fallback family this slice is about, so re-running them was the verification the slice asked for.
- 2026-09-14 (S48): **the inherited-bug group needs S48b, and the inventory is what says so (spec amendment 25).** Thirteen of the fifteen ledger entries are closed; the three items left - ledger 11 mechanisms C and D, and ledger 9's remaining POSIX-plus-`(?e)` count bug, which is this port's own and still reproduces on the committed code - are one mechanism seen three ways, the counts saved as a block against a change list unwound item by item. Entry 11 had already written "That is a slice of its own".
- 2026-09-14 (S48): **read the WHOLE output of a control run, not a `tail` of it.** The S29-A row recorded in these notes said it fires at seed 7 and it does not - the seed-7 line had scrolled off a `tail -30` and the number was transcribed from the four that remained. The independent verifier caught it by re-running. S29-A fires at three seeds of five, which is a thinner control than the notes claimed and is now recorded as such.
- 2026-09-14 (S48): **a claim whose evidence lived in `.scratch/` is stated as unreproducible rather than quietly kept.** The seed-99991 gate's four rows were proven identical to HEAD's by replaying the saved wave through a worktree at `bb6213a`; the wave was scratch and is gone, so the notes now say the replay cannot be re-run from the commit and rest the claim on the structural argument instead - the edit is inside `DoBestFuzzyMatch` and no diverging row carries `BESTMATCH`. Same rule for "checked by an assertion per line", which was a scratch script and is now described as one.
- 2026-09-14 **S48b is S48's own authoring, not the orchestrator's.** S48 wrote `S48b-fuzzy-counts-and-changes-restore.md` in `c4c1cd0` for the three open items its inventory left - ledger 11's mechanisms C and D and ledger 9's port-side POSIX count bug - and the DIVERGENCES audit correctly marked them PLANNED (S48b). The orchestrator first mis-marked that row SHIPPED, then authored a duplicate S48b before noticing S48's file; both errors were reverted within the hour and one line was added to S48's slice (do not widen the ledger 9 fix into the cost-ranking rule; update DIVERGENCES in the same commit). Lesson recorded: check `slices/` for a file before authoring one, even when an audit names a slice id that looks invented.
- 2026-09-14 (S48b): **two of the slice's three items were ONE bug, and the slice's own premise that they were "one mechanism seen three ways" was right in a way it did not predict.** Ledger 11's mechanism C came in two halves with opposite shapes: the change list too LONG for the counts (the worst case, and mechanism D) and too SHORT (the twin). Only the first is the save/restore desynchronisation the slice was scoped around; the second was ledger 9's stale running totals, because the twin carries `(?b)` as well as `(?p)` and the `BESTMATCH` walk was ranking a restored candidate's counts against a discarded candidate's `TotalErrors`. **Truncating the change list cannot fix a list that is too short**, and noticing that - rather than widening the truncation until the test passed - is what kept the two fixes separate and each minimal.
- 2026-09-14 (S48b): **the nineteen save/restore sites reduce to one edit plus one judgement per site, and the judgement is "restore or merge".** `PushFuzzyCounts` pushes the change list's LENGTH beside the counts block and `PopFuzzyCounts` truncates back to it; eight pops are restoring (`ATOMIC`/`END_ATOMIC`, `CONDITIONAL`/`END_CONDITIONAL`, `LOOKAROUND`/`END_LOOKAROUND`) and three are merging and keep a separate `PopFuzzyCountsMerging` (both `END_FUZZY` arms, where the inner section's changes ARE the answer, and the `FUZZY` backtrack arm, where every item in the section has already unwound its own change). Ledger entry 11 predicted "eight push sites and eleven pop sites, each needing a decision". **A first draft of this note claimed the decision is readable off which buffer the pop writes into - `state.FuzzyCounts` a restore, a scratch span a merge - and the blind review killed it with the counter-example**: the `FUZZY` backtrack arm merges into `state.FuzzyCounts`. The buffer is a useful smell and not a rule; the question is whether the site is abandoning a sub-attempt (restore) or closing one whose changes are part of the answer (merge).
- 2026-09-14 (S48b): **the truncation never grows the list, deliberately.** A restore whose sub-attempt unwound BELOW the push point has nothing to put back, and inventing entries would turn a visible contradiction into an invisible wrong answer. That is why the twin needed the other fix rather than a cleverer truncation.
- 2026-09-14 (S48b): **the fix exposed a new upstream defect, ledger entry 16: a `POSIX` overlapped scan of a `BESTMATCH` fuzzy pattern drops its LONGEST match.** Upstream contradicts itself four ways on one subject - the scan with `BESTMATCH` and no `POSIX`, the scan with `POSIX` and no `BESTMATCH`, the scan with neither, and `fullmatch` at the very flags the failing scan uses, all answer `(0, 9)`; only the two flags together drop it and start at `(0, 8)`. `POSIX` is leftmost-LONGEST, so the one row the flag exists to guarantee is the one it removes. Drawn independently a second time by the 6000-row gate at seed 20260914 (row 76101), which is what makes it a family rather than a curiosity. Mechanism NOT established in upstream's C, and the entry says so.
- 2026-09-14 (S48b): **upstream leaks a fuzzy change through an ATOMIC GROUP, and S47's anchored question cannot see it.** `_leak_free_fuzzy` re-asks upstream the row anchored at the span it reported, which removes a leak from an EARLIER attempt; an atomic group cuts the backtracking WITHIN one attempt, so the anchored answer is identical to the leaked one. The door that settles it is upstream's own control - replace `(?>` with `(?:` so the abandoned attempt unwinds the ordinary way - and on the gate's row 74033 upstream then moves its deletion from codepoint 2 to codepoint 3, which is UTF-16 4 and is exactly this port's answer. **Consequence: the `fuzzy-changes-leaked-from-an-abandoned-attempt` entry's discriminator does not cover this door**, and classifying it needs a recorded control the recorder does not yet write.
- 2026-09-14 (S48b): **a before/after row diff against a worktree at HEAD is cheap and settles "is this row mine", where a count comparison does not.** The three-seed 6000-row gate went 19 -> 24, and naming which five were new took two replays of saved waves through `.claude/worktrees/s48b-baseline` with `-SkipRecord` - seed 20260914 whole (baseline 9, mine 11) and seed 7 narrowed to the three generators that carry any divergence (baseline 6, mine 9). S48's notes recorded only the per-seed totals, so the row identities had to be re-measured rather than read; **record the row NUMBERS, not just the count.**
- 2026-09-14 (S48b, sitting 2): **the recorder's second questions are a TABLE now, not a chain of wrappers.** `_record_row_and_its_bestmatch_free_answer` became `_record_row_and_its_control_answers` over a `_CONTROLS` tuple of `(recorded key, how to take the construct away)`, and two entries joined `bestmatchFreeOutcome`: `posixFreeOutcome` and `atomicFreeOutcome`. Each keeps the original's rules exactly - an outcome upstream errors, times out or exhausts itself on leaves the key OFF, which makes the consuming entry not apply and reports the row rather than hiding it. `(?p)` is stripped only from the LEADING RUN of inline-flag groups (`_INLINE_FLAG_PREFIX`), because a `(?p)` written mid-pattern scopes to the group it sits in and deleting it there asks a different question, not the same one without POSIX.
- 2026-09-14 (S48b, sitting 2): **all five of the gate's new rows classify, and the fifth was judged to be THIS PORT'S answer being right rather than parked.** Sitting 1 named five rows and two families; the measurement found three families and one row that neither control reached. Four went to `posix-fuzzy-contradicts-its-own-flagless-answer` (three rows) and `atomic-group-leaks-a-change-position` (one). The fifth, seed-7 row 73463, needed the pattern bisected to a three-character subject before anything could be said about it.
- 2026-09-14 (S48b, sitting 2): **S48b's own truncation moved row 73463, and the honest thing was to bisect rather than to classify it as sitting 1 had assumed.** Sitting 1's notes claimed "on every one of the five this port answers what upstream's own control answers"; on this row that was FALSE - the port answered neither upstream's drawn answer nor its anchored one. Bisecting against a worktree at `c8165b5` showed the pre-S48b port reproduced upstream EXACTLY, leak included, and flipping `TruncateFuzzyChanges`'s comparison restores that at every prefix length. **So the row is a change this slice made, and it had to be justified rather than pinned on upstream's authority.**
- 2026-09-14 (S48b, sitting 2): **what justified it is upstream contradicting ITSELF between its two directions**, on `A(?=[^A]{e<=1})A+\D` over `'AAA'` - four constructs, three characters, no flags. Forward, upstream puts the substitution where the lookahead tested; add `(?r)`, which picks the same candidate at the same span and count, and upstream moves it to the MATCH START while this port stays put. Ledger entry 11 mechanism F, oracle entry `reversed-lookahead-change-at-the-match-start`.
- 2026-09-14 (S48b, sitting 2): **the first draft of that entry's mechanism was wrong and a fourth probe row killed it.** It said the shift was the lookahead's OFFSET from the match start; `AA(?=[^A]{e<=1})A+\D` over `'AAAA'` has offset 2 and upstream reversed still answers 0. The real condition is a GENERAL REPEAT after the lookahead - make it a fixed count and both directions agree. **A mechanism inferred from two rows that agree with it is a hypothesis; the row that could refute it is the measurement.** `tools/probes/upstream-reversed-lookahead-change-position.py` now carries the refuting row and exits non-zero if upstream's behaviour moves.
- 2026-09-14 (S48b, sitting 2): **a pin keyed on rows needs an over-classification control, and the realistic wrong answer is upstream's own.** `OracleWaveTests.A_row_this_port_answers_upstreams_way_is_not_accounted_for_by_the_three_judged_entries` feeds every example row of the three new entries two answers - upstream's drawn answer, which is what this port really gave before S48b, and no match at all - and asserts neither is classified. That is a measured control rather than the assertion that "keyed on the answer" implies it.
- 2026-09-14 (S48b, sitting 2): **`tools/run-controls.py` CANNOT measure a control that mutates the recorder, and two controls were withdrawn rather than kept on numbers that do not reproduce.** `wave_for` records each wave BEFORE the mutation is applied and caches it, on the stated assumption that "the rows do not depend on our own code" - true of an engine mutation and false of a recorder one. S48b-C and S48b-D reported identical figures at every seed, which was the tell: the numbers came from cached waves recorded without the new fields, not from either mutation. Applied by hand they behave correctly and differently, so the mutations are sound and the harness is not. The two are recorded in the slice's closing notes with their by-hand reproduction instead of in `controls.json`. **S42-2A mutates the same file and is in the same position; it is owed maintenance, not this slice's to fix.**
- 2026-09-14 (S48b, sitting 2): **the `.scratch/control-waves` cache is invalidated by any change to the recorder's OUTPUT FORMAT, and 76 stale waves were deleted.** Waves recorded before this slice carry no `posixFreeOutcome`, so the new entries cannot fire on them and every control run against them reports two extra divergences at seed 7 that have nothing to do with the control. The cache has no version key; deleting it is the whole fix, and it re-records on demand.
- 2026-09-14 (S48b, sitting 3): **TUnit's assembly-wide `[Timeout]` is not a backstop for anything in this suite, and a test may not be written as though it were.** A synchronous CPU-bound test body has no `CancellationToken` for TUnit to signal - the analyser says so directly, `TUnit0015` - so the engine's match loop never observes one and the attribute has no effect: a 20-second body **passes** under `[assembly: Timeout(3_000)]`, measured. Made `ByteStack.Grow`'s limit unreachable and the real stack-bound test ran 200 seconds without failing or reporting. `tools/check-ratchet.ps1:33-36` already recorded the same fact from a host that ran 44 minutes past `--timeout 20m` on 2026-09-13; this is the second independent measurement of it. The consequence is a rule: **`FuzzyRegex.InfiniteMatchTimeout` in a test that could loop turns a red test into a hung suite**, because a `MatchTimeout` is the only thing that can stop the engine. Budget generously instead of not at all. Found by the blind review, on a first draft of the fix below, with a reproduction; the draft was withdrawn.
- 2026-09-14 (S48b, sitting 3): **a test that has to ALLOCATE its way to an assertion is timing the machine, not the engine, and must be budgeted for the machine.** `FuzzyRecursionTests.The_stack_bound_is_still_what_catches_a_blowup_the_guard_cannot_see` reaches `ByteStack.Grow`'s 1GB limit only by committing a gigabyte, so the time it takes tracks FREE MEMORY: on one 32GB machine on one day, 63.4s and 17.6s at 3.4GB free and 15.1-18.8s over five runs at 9.3GB free. Under the file's shared 30s `_budget` the engine raised `RegexMatchTimeoutException` instead and red-ratcheted a tree whose engine had not changed - **proven environmental before anything was edited**, because the identical failure reproduces in a worktree at `c8165b5`, three commits earlier. It now has a five-minute `_blowupBudget` of its own, about five times the slowest throw measured. The general form: when a ratchet goes red, read the failure mode before reading the diff - a machine-paced test and a real regression look identical in the summary line.
- 2026-09-14 (S49): **a live re-triage of the upstream tracker retires four of the twelve "real engine bugs" the 2026-08-31 snapshot listed, and running the case is what retired every one of them.** 334, 551 and 596 no longer reproduce on 2026.9.10 - the 2019 crash is gone, the maintainer's 2025 partial fix for 551 has landed, and `(?:X){e<=0}` now costs what `(?:X)` costs against a reported 210x - and **367 was never a bug**: PCRE2 10.47 answers PARTIAL on every one of its rows, so what is wrong is upstream's documented promise that a partial means "a complete match could be possible", which no engine keeps and which 367's own example (a primality test) shows to be undecidable. The tracker itself had barely moved - 74 open, five closed, **none opened, and not one still-open issue's text changed since 2026-08-31** - so none of this was new information; it was a stale reading, and the instrument that corrected it was a probe, not a re-read. Triage in `docs/plan/upstream-issues/2026-09-14-triage.md`; the classification was redone blind by a subagent and reconciled, agreeing on 60 of 74.
- 2026-09-14 (S49): **issue 589 is a real inherited bug and issue 367 is not, although they are the same machinery failing in opposite directions, and a report that merges them would be rejected.** Both concern an assertion evaluated at the truncation point of a partial match. 367 is the false positive - a partial no continuation can complete - and every engine produces it. 589 is the false negative: `fullmatch('True', partial=True)` against `(?!(True|False)\b)(.*)` is None although 'Truest' matches, which contradicts upstream's own documented definition, and **PCRE2 answers PARTIAL** there under both SOFT and HARD. The mechanism is one row: `True\b` over 'True' is `match` under SOFT and `PARTIAL` under HARD, so PCRE2 treats a boundary at the end of the available text as unresolved instead of deciding it against text the caller has declared truncated. The false-negative direction is the dangerous one for the incremental-input use case upstream's own documentation advertises. Ledger entry 21; `tools/probes/pcre2-partial-truncation-assertions.py`.
- 2026-09-14 (S49): **a test written for an issue upstream has not settled asserts only what the maintainer's own candidate rules agree on.** Issue 425's maintainer offers three numbering rules and has chosen none, so the test asserts that `bug` reaches 'BUG' and '!' lives elsewhere - which options 2 and 3 share - rather than a group numbering. It does rule out option 1, today's behaviour, and the ground is stated: under it a capture group's text is unreachable through any API and `groupindex` maps the name onto a different group's text. The same discipline gives 564 a monotonicity assertion rather than a span list and 554 upstream's own ceiling rather than a byte figure. A sweep slice has no authority to choose upstream's semantics, and a first draft of ledger 17 that claimed the status quo "matches none of" the three options was wrong - option 1 is labelled "(current behaviour)" - and was caught by the blind review quoting the text.
- 2026-09-14 (S49): **a measurement that varies with the ORDER of calls, not just between runs, must have its cause found before any figure from it is quoted.** The independent verifier could not reproduce this port's bytes-per-repetition on issue 554 at all (537 B/rep flat against a recorded 574 then 343). The cause is that the engine rents its backtracking buffer from a **process-wide** pool, so a later call may reuse an earlier one's and appear to allocate half as much; a fresh `FuzzyRegex` per row does not help, because the pool is not per-instance. Measured both ways: n=2,000,000 costs 1166.2 MB (611 B/rep) cold and 654.2 MB (343 B/rep) warm. `tools/probes/port-issue-sweep.ps1` now uses `GC.GetTotalAllocatedBytes` rather than a live-heap delta and **only the first call in a fresh process is quoted**. Two sibling corrections in the same slice make the general rule: a `tracemalloc` peak was described as including the subject string when the probe builds the subject before `start()` (it reads `(0, 0)` there), and the port's issue-596 timing was pinned at a ratio of 0.83-0.87 that the next run falsified at 0.90. **Three numbers in one slice were quoted more precisely than the instrument justified, and only re-running found them.**
- 2026-09-14 (S49): **evidence for a ledger entry lives in `tools/probes/`, never in `.scratch/`, and the blind review now checks that.** Ledger entry 18's `re` and `regex` bytes-per-repetition figures were first measured by a gitignored scratch script while the entry claimed every reproduction was re-runnable from the committed tree - the same failure an independent audit found across S44-S46. The fix was to move the `tracemalloc` measurement into `tools/probes/upstream-issue-sweep.py` itself, so the probe prints the number the ledger quotes.
- 2026-09-14 (S50): **upstream issues 563 and 564 are ONE bug, and its mechanism is `_regex.c:10214` with upstream's own comment beside it.** `permit_insertion = !search || text_pos != search_anchor`, under "Permit insertion except initially when searching (it's better just to start searching one character later)" - and `search_anchor` is set once per matching operation (`init_match`, `:3410`), never per candidate start position, so **the rule fires at exactly ONE of the positions a scan visits.** The isolating probe is the same subject and the same winning span answered two ways purely by where the search was told to begin: `compile(r'\m(?:Y){i}\M').search(' XY', 0)` is `(1, 3)` and `.search(' XY', 1)` is `None`. 564 reaches the same rule through BESTMATCH's re-anchoring, which is why the LOOSER budget finds fewer matches, and the 563 change turned 564 green with no code of its own - confirming the reporter's suspicion that nobody had shown. S49's ledger had this as "not established to a line" and suspected the word-boundary check having no preceding character; both were wrong. `tools/probes/issue-563-anchor-rule.py`.
- 2026-09-14 (S50): **a fix that is justified as "a generalisation of upstream's own behaviour" still needs its blast radius measured against upstream's own tests.** The 563 fix's case is strong - `^` and `\A` already escape the anchor rule, because `basic_match` turns a start-anchored pattern into an anchored match, so upstream KEEPS a match beginning with an inserted character for `^(?:abc){i<=1}` over 'xabc' and loses it for the identical `(?m)^` pattern. A first version lifted the rule whenever ANY assertion had held at the anchor and reddened upstream's own ported `test_fuzzy` rows 51, 52, 54 and 56. The rule that survives is narrower: lift it only when the assertion **fails one character on**, because only then is the later start a different match rather than this one minus an insertion. The narrowing was found by breaking those tests, not by argument.
- 2026-09-14 (S50): **bare mutable state cannot express a fact about the current PATH in a backtracking engine, and two independent blind reviews each found a different half of that.** The 563 fix held "an assertion has pinned the match here" in a `MatchState` field, which the engine never saves or restores. It **under-clears**: an assertion that held only on a path later abandoned still pinned the anchor, so `(?:\bq|)(?:abc){i<=1}` and `(?!\bz)(?:abc){i<=1}` over 'xabc' answered `'xabc'` where upstream says `'abc'`. Clearing it in the `Branch` and failed-lookaround backtrack arms fixed those two shapes and left every repeat - `(?:\bq)*`, `(?:\bq)?`, `(?:\bq){0,3}`, `(?:\bq)*+`, `(?>(?:\bq)*)` - still wrong, ten of twelve probed shapes. And it **over-clears**: the same unconditional clears discard a pin set before and outside the construct, so an inert `(?:z|)` or `(?!q)` after the `\m` threw the fix away. The fix was reverted rather than patched a third time. It must be backtracking state, or a compile-time "every path to this fuzzy item passes a position assertion" analysis combined with the same dynamic test.
- 2026-09-14 (S50): **returning a PARTIAL status from a predicate ends the match, so it cannot be used to mean "unresolved" while backtracking still has work to do.** The 589 fix made the seven word and grapheme boundary predicates answer `PARTIAL` at the right-hand truncation point. It turned the issue's row green and broke partials this port previously got exactly right: `search(r'(\.+?)\1\b', '..', partial=True)` reported group 1 as `(0, 1)` where upstream and HEAD give `(0, 2)`, because the lazy repeat's FIRST try reached the boundary, escalated, and returned before the repeat could grow. **PCRE2's model is the sound one and is different in kind**: a `hitend` flag meaning "the end of the subject was reached while deciding", which lets matching and backtracking run to completion and turns only a FINAL failure into a partial. Reverted; ledger 21 carries the design.
- 2026-09-14 (S50): **an `ExpectedDivergences` entry written in the same slice as the fix it describes can hide that fix's own regressions, and only a control run against the committed tree finds it.** The entry added for 589 compared overall spans and never looked inside a capture group, so it classified the truncated-group regression above as EXPECTED - and the negative control for the fix went **GREEN with the fault present** (`expected 13, 7, 7, 10` against the unmutated 6, 2, 2, 2) instead of reddening. The entry also swallowed a live wave row at seed 20260915 where the port had agreed with upstream at HEAD. Both went with the 589 revert. The general rule: a control's job is to red, and a control that goes green is a finding about the instrument, not a tick.
- 2026-09-14 (S50): **a negative control that does not fire is the most valuable result it can give, because it measures the tests rather than the code.** Three of S50's suite controls - the too-loose form of the 563 fix, and each of the two backtrack-arm clears - reported **0 failures** on their first run, which proved that no test covered the too-loose form or either path-sensitivity defect the blind review had just found by hand. The rows were then pinned and all three fired. Run the controls before believing a green suite has covered a fix.
- 2026-09-14 (S50): **upstream issue 425 is fixed by the maintainer's own option 2, and the two orderings option 2 cannot reach are parked rather than improvised.** `ParseCommon` rolls `GroupCount` back per branch and `OpenGroup` returns a known name's number without advancing the counter, so the next group in the same branch is handed it again and the later write wins. `Info.OpenGroup` now SKIPS a number a reused name has already claimed in the branch - "skip group numbers that have been used up to that point in the branch". An over-strong first attempt ADVANCED the counter to that number instead and reddened upstream's own `test_branch_reset#16-17` plus compile-parity corpus row 613; skipping leaves both green and changes no corpus row. Where the UNNAMED group comes first the name's number is already fixed, so only option 3's lookahead over the whole branch would help, and upstream has chosen neither option.
- 2026-09-14 (S50): **upstream issue 554 is a performance gap rather than a wrong answer, and both cheap answers were ruled out by measurement before it was parked.** Capturing is where the cost is: `(?:ab)*` reaches n=4,000,000 and fails at 6,000,000 where `(ab)*` fails at 4,000,000, and atomic `(?>(ab)*)` and possessive `(ab)*+` **both still fail at 4,000,000**, so no existing construct avoids the per-repetition block. Raising the bound would be a patch on the symptom: the 1GB limit is tested against the DOUBLED capacity, so the largest usable stack is just under 512MB - but that is upstream's own check ported faithfully (`_regex.c:2357`), and upstream reaches 6,000,000 under the same cap because its cost is lower. The failure mode here is already better than the one the issue reports, so what is left belongs behind Phase 7's benchmarks.
- 2026-09-14 (S50b): **Version 1 is this port's compile-time default** (`PatternCompiler.DefaultVersion`), per owner decision and spec amendment 24. Measured first, not read from the README: on regex 2026.9.10 and .NET 10 the only live V0/V1 differences are nested sets with set operations, full case-folding, and - **not in upstream's README** - a backreference to a group that is still open, which V0 rejects as "cannot refer to an open group" and V1 accepts. The zero-width and inline-flag-scoping differences the README still lists no longer exist, because V0 tracks `re` 3.7+. .NET agrees with V0 on both live behaviours it has (subtraction-only sets, simple folding) and with V1 on the open-group backreference. Probes: `tools/probes/upstream-version-defaults.py` and `dotnet-version-defaults.ps1`.
- 2026-09-14 (S50b): **the ported upstream suite compiles through one helper, `Ported.Upstream`, which pins `DEFAULT_VERSION = VERSION0`** - the version `upstream/regex/tests/test_regex.py` is written against and never changes. 784 call sites across 145 files were rewritten mechanically; the ratchet's passing set did not move, which is what proves the rewrite was behaviour-neutral before the default flipped. The version is passed as a **default, not a flag**: `Version0` in the flags beside an inline `(?V1)` leaves both version bits set and is rejected as "VERSION0 and VERSION1 flags are mutually incompatible". `Conventions.PortedTestConventionsTests` scans the `Ported/` sources and fails on any bypass, proved by injecting one; prose would not have held, because a new ported test arrives with every upstream sync.
- 2026-09-14 (S50b): **flipping the default made a latent upstream bug live, and it is fixed here rather than pinned** (ledger 22). Under `DEFAULT_VERSION = VERSION1` upstream contradicts itself: `compile('a', regex.V0).flags` is `U|V0` and `compile('(?V0)a').flags` is `F|U|V0`, so the same version folds two ways. A leading global flag makes `_compile` parse twice, and `Info.__init__` assigns `global_flags` AFTER OR-ing `DEFAULT_FLAGS` (`_regex_core.py:4359-4361`), so the first attempt's guess at the version leaks its implied `FULLCASE` into the attempt that knows better. Unreachable upstream, where `DEFAULT_FLAGS[VERSION0]` is 0. `Parsing.Info` now assigns `GlobalFlags` before the `DEFAULT_FLAGS` line; under `Version0` that is bit-for-bit upstream's behaviour and all 1,659 compile-parity rows are unmoved.
- 2026-09-14 (S50b): **the oracle compares under the version the RECORDER ran, not this port's default.** `OracleWave.Load` stamps the header's `defaultVersion` onto every row and `OracleComparer.Run` passes it as the compile's default version; `Describe` prints `version=V0`/`version=V1` on every block. Threading it through the row rather than through each call site is deliberate: `OracleComparer.Run(row)` has a dozen callers and an optional parameter defaulting to the port's own version would be a footgun that fails silently, one row at a time.
- 2026-09-15 (S50b): **the "unterminated character set" error names the way out only for a pattern version 0 actually accepts, and the check is a second compile rather than a heuristic.** Three cheaper discriminators were tried and each was killed by a blind review reproducing a pattern where it gave advice that does not work: a `try`/`catch` around the nested parse missed `[a[b]` (the nested set closes; the OUTER one runs off the end), a parse-wide "a nested set was opened" flag advised `Version0` on `[[a-z]--[aeiou]]x[`, and scoping that flag to one top-level set still advised it on `[[a]--[b`. Version 0 rejects all three. `PatternCompiler.Compile` now catches its own bare message and recompiles under `Version0`, re-wording only if that succeeds; it costs one extra parse of an already-failed pattern, cannot recurse, and let `Info.SawNestedSet` be deleted again. **The general lesson: an error message that tells the user what to do is an assertion about their input, and it wants the same evidence as any other assertion.**
- 2026-09-15 (S50b): **the ported-suite guard checks two tokens on one line - the `FuzzyRegex` type and a `new` - after cutting the comment tail, and both halves were forced by a blind review.** Enumerating call shapes (`new FuzzyRegex(`, `FuzzyRegex.X(`, `FuzzyRegex x = new(`) leaked, because `private static readonly FuzzyRegex[] _p = [new("a")];` matches none of them and the target-typed `new` names no type at all. Replacing that with "names the type and does not say `Upstream.`" leaked too, because `[Upstream.Compile("a"), new("b")]` says `Upstream` and still compiles one pattern directly, and so does a direct construction with `Upstream.Compile` in its trailing comment. Requiring the `new` also removed two false positives the same pass found - the type named inside a string literal, and inside a `/* */` tail.
- 2026-09-15 (S50b): **the V0 pin on the ported suite is load-bearing for exactly two of 5,916 baselined tests today, measured, and that is worth knowing rather than assuming.** Control A - `Upstream.Compile` pinning `Version1` instead - reddens only `Api.GetAttrTests.Options_reports_the_inline_flag_and_the_default_version` and `Various.VariousParseErrorTests.Invalid_pattern_is_rejected(((·)\1+))`, the second being the open-group backreference V0 rejects and V1 accepts. The blind review confirmed the number against upstream itself: running `test_regex.py` with `regex.DEFAULT_VERSION = regex.VERSION1` fails `test_getattr` and that one `test_various` row and nothing else. Upstream's suite writes `(?V0)`/`(?V1)` explicitly wherever the version matters, so the pin's value is almost entirely prospective - it stops a future sync's tests from silently changing what they measure - and the conventions guard, not the current failure count, is what makes it hold.
- 2026-09-15 (S51): **the per-call surface takes BOTH a timeout and a token, as optional trailing parameters rather than overloads, and the research that settled it is `tools/probes/bcl-sync-cancellationtoken-apis.ps1`.** Measured on the running framework that day rather than recalled: **28 synchronous (non-`Task`-returning) BCL methods take a `CancellationToken` and 28 of 28 put it last**; only one of the 28 makes it optional. `Regex` itself takes a `matchTimeout` on **11 static methods, always last, never optional, and on 0 instance methods** - so .NET's own regex has no per-call budget for a compiled pattern at all, and upstream's `timeout=` keyword (`_main.py:253-298`) is the closer precedent. Both are offered because the exception types carry different meanings a caller acts on differently (`RegexMatchTimeoutException` versus `OperationCanceledException`) and because a `CancellationTokenSource(timeout)` allocates a timer per call where the engine's `Stopwatch` poll allocates nothing. Optional parameters rather than overloads: the surface has 27 input-dependent methods and doubling it buys nothing, CA1068 does not fire when the token is last and optional, and the library has no shipped binary to break.
- 2026-09-15 (S51): **cancellation is polled at the engine's existing check site and nowhere else, and it is upstream's `PyErr_CheckSignals` half rather than a new mechanism.** `safe_check_cancel` (`upstream/src/_regex.c:2266`) has two halves - `check_timed_out` and `PyErr_CheckSignals` - and the port's comment had recorded the second as having "no counterpart here". The token is that counterpart: it is the only way a .NET caller can interrupt a running match, exactly as Ctrl-C is for a Python one. All three call sites reach it through upstream's own `state.Iterations == 0` gate - a `ushort` stepped by `0x100`, so it wraps every 256 turns - so the token costs one field read per 256 iterations of the matching or backtracking loop. Guidance followed rather than guessed: "For long-running computations that loop or recurse, you can listen for a cancellation request by periodically polling ... Polling itself does not significantly impact performance" and "the correct way to terminate the operation is for the delegate to call `ThrowIfCancellationRequested`" (Cancellation in Managed Threads, learn.microsoft.com, read 2026-09-15).
- 2026-09-15 (S51): **a cancelled token beats the clock when both fired, and the token is read at the door as well as in the engine.** `MatchLimits.Cancelled` asks the token first, so a caller who asked to stop gets `OperationCanceledException` carrying their own token rather than "the library ran out of time" - which is what the cancellation framework asks a listener to throw so the source of the cancellation can be verified. The door check exists because `Substitution.Subx` takes a min-width shortcut before it ever builds a state (`regex.sub('abcdefghij', ..., 'z')` never reaches the engine), and "a cancelled token throws unless the work happened to be trivial" is not a contract a caller can use. It costs one predictable read per public call, off the matching path.
- 2026-09-15 (S51): **`Match.NextMatch` deliberately takes neither**, because it takes no arguments at all; it runs under the pattern's `MatchTimeout`, which is exactly where the built-in `Regex.Match.NextMatch` sits. A caller who needs to bound or cancel a walk uses `Matches`, which takes both and which builds its whole list inside the call. Recorded because the asymmetry looks like an oversight and is not.
- 2026-09-15 (S51): **the oracle's row deadline moved from the constructor to the per-call argument, and the slice's own "compare `timeout` rows" bullet was NOT done, on the recorder's evidence.** Every one of the seven call sites in `OracleComparer.Run` now passes the deadline per call, so all ~126,000 rows of a default three-seed wave exercise the new plumbing; verdicts were unchanged and the wave stayed GREEN at all three seeds. Comparing rows where upstream timed out was rejected because `record-oracle.py`'s own `timed_out` docstring already argues against it with a reason that holds: upstream "did not reject the pattern, it simply never finished", so scoring a port that *does* answer as diverging - and a port that also hangs as agreeing - gets both halves backwards. A port that answers where upstream hangs is better, not wrong.
- 2026-09-15 (S51): **a BenchmarkDotNet run in the main tree silently measures the WORKTREE's binary when a git worktree of this repo exists, because benchmark project names must be unique.** S51's before/after used a `49a0467` worktree and its first AFTER pair reported allocation byte-identical to BEFORE - impossible, since the change adds an 8-byte field to `MatchState`, and the real figures are 2,096/856/784 B before and 2,104/864/792 B after. The independent verifier hit the honest version of the same fault, `System.NotSupportedException: Found more than one matching project file for FuzzyRegex.Benchmarks`. **Remove the worktree before benchmarking the main tree**, and read the Allocated column first as the tell for which binary ran. S54 inherits this: it owns the committed baselines, and a baseline measured against the wrong binary is worse than none. The general lesson is the one S48's control taught in the other direction - a number that matches the null hypothesis *exactly* is a reason to check the apparatus, not to believe it.
- 2026-09-15 (S52): **the oracle could not classify ANY divergence family on an answer that carries no match position, and that - not a new engine defect - was the whole of the pre-existing red at 2000 rows.** `sub`, `subf` and `split` answer with a string, a count and a list of parts; a row upstream fails WHILE MATCHING answers with an exception. Every span-keyed entry in `ExpectedDivergences.cs` therefore read nothing on four of the nine operations, so a row belonging to a judged family as plainly as any other reddened the run. The three-seed 2000-row wave of `58977bb` was red at all three seeds on three rows and two were exactly this (seed 7 row 29165 `subf`, seed 4242 row 24416 `split`), both proved to be `CaseFolding.txt`'s `T` rows by an isolating control rather than by resemblance: only a range SPANNING `I` reaches U+0131 (`[A-Z]` and `[A-Y]` match it, `[A-H]` and `[J-Z]` do not), and swapping U+0130 for `h` or U+00C5 moves upstream onto this port's span. The fix is a third recorder second-fact, `scanMatches` - upstream's own `finditer` over the row - beside `subMatches` and `anchoredScan`. `subMatches` does not serve: it is truncated to the row's replacement count, so a row that replaced nothing carries an empty list exactly where the divergence is a match upstream made and this port did not.

This follows the owner's 2026-09-14 ruling for this file: a pin is narrowed to the rows where its contradiction was measured, and it widens only by judging another row with the probe. The refused row is pinned in `OracleWaveTests._turkicRowsToRefuse`.
- 2026-09-15 (S52): **astral SUBJECTS were already everywhere and three other things were not, and the widening was scoped from that measurement rather than from the slice text.** `tools/probes/generator-plane-coverage.py` over 1200 rows a generator at three seeds: every one of the 21 generators already drew 118-484 astral subjects, but SMP digits reached only `classes` (125) and `reverse` (13); emoji modifiers, ZWJ and `\X` reached only `boundaries` and `reverse`; and **seven generators never put a character above U+FFFF in a PATTERN at all** - `classes`, `groups`, `quantifiers`, `backrefs`, `substitution`, `iteration` and `fuzzy` - because each builds its pattern from a fixed atom list rather than from a slice of its subject. So a fuzzy edit over a surrogate pair, an astral backreference and an astral repeat were unreachable. One `ASTRAL_*` block now holds every such character with its measured properties (U+200D ZERO WIDTH JOINER is `Cf` and `\w` is TRUE of it, which is not obvious), and `fuzzy` went from 118 astral subjects and 0 astral patterns to 512 and 477.

Recorded so sitting 2 does not build the machinery the slice file describes.
- 2026-09-15 (S52): **the seed sweep distinguishes a HARNESS failure from a divergence, and keys resumption on the whole question rather than on the seed.** A non-zero exit from `run-oracle.ps1` means either; `Our_own_answers_never_contradict_themselves` refuses a wave holding no fuzzy match at all, so `-Generator case-folding` reds every seed with `diverge 0`. Both blind passes found the other two: resumption keyed on the seed alone silently skipped every seed when the same log was re-run at a higher row count or with a named seed, and a seed that died before the consumer ran read the PREVIOUS seed's `report.txt` and was logged with another seed's tally. The summary is filtered to the current question for the same reason one log can now hold one seed twice.
- 2026-09-15 (S52, sitting 2): **the `(*SKIP)` -> `(*PRUNE)` control is now RECORDED per row, as `pruneOutcome`.** Four entries in `ExpectedDivergences.cs` already rest their judgement on it and each quoted a probe run by hand: `(*PRUNE)` prunes exactly the backtracking `(*SKIP)` prunes and moves neither slice bound (`upstream/src/_regex.c:14545` forwards, `:14551` under `(?r)`), so an answer that changes between the two changed because of the bound. - 2026-09-15 (S52, sitting 2): **a Turkic divergence can live in the PATTERN, and isolating it needs a control that holds the fold LENGTH fixed.** Rows 25482 and 34508 carry no Turkic letter in the subject - one has U+0130 as the first letter of a `\L<w1>` word, the other as the pattern's leading literal on an empty slice - so both older Turkic entries, which ask which Turkic letters the divergence's SPANS cover, correctly decline them. The obvious control is wrong: swapping U+0130 for `h` also shortens the fold from two characters to one, so a divergence about length would survive it. Swapping it for U+00DF (`ss`), U+FB00 (`ff`) and U+01F0 (`j` + U+030C) instead varies only whether a `T` row is consulted, and the two engines then agree cell for cell on both rows - this port treats U+0130 exactly as it treats those three, upstream treats it exactly as it treats `h`. New entry `turkic-default-folding-from-the-pattern-side`, keyed on the two rows and on this port's answer to each.

Sitting 1 read the row as that family and as not-Turkic, having swapped the U+0130 out of the SUBJECT when it is in the PATTERN; seed 4242 row 24256 was read as POSIX when its flags `0x4002` carry no POSIX bit at all. Both corrected by re-triaging every one of the eleven with an ablation run through `run-oracle.ps1 -Rows` rather than by description.
- 2026-09-15 (S52, sitting 2): **POSIX has now been seen to break its own contract a third way - it ADDS a match.** Ledger entry 23: `subn` with `count=2` replaces twice under POSIX and once without it, on a template that expands to nothing either way, so both answers carry the same text and only the count says what happened. A differential oracle keyed on the rendered answer would see nothing; the row was caught because the recorder writes the replacement count down too. `POSIX` chooses leftmost-longest among the matches the ordinary engine can make, so producing one it cannot is outside what the flag is defined to do - the same contract entry 9 (the cost) and entry 16 (the longest match) break in the other two directions.
- 2026-09-15 (S52, sitting 3): **upstream's `$` reads a SLICE bound where every one of its seven sibling edge predicates reads a TEXT bound - including its own Unicode twin - and that is a second defect behind the carried-slice one, not a consequence of it.** `try_match_START_OF_LINE` (`upstream/src/_regex.c:7360`), `START_OF_LINE_U` (`:7367`, through `at_line_start` at `:902`/`:1945`), `START_OF_STRING` (`:7373`), `END_OF_STRING` (`:7123`), `END_OF_STRING_LINE` (`:7129`), `END_OF_STRING_LINE_U` (`:7136`) and `END_OF_LINE_U` (`:7117`, through `at_line_end` at `:922`/`:1966`) all compare against `text_start`/`text_end`; `try_match_END_OF_LINE` (`:7110`) alone compares against `slice_end`, which is the field `RE_OP_SKIP` writes under `(?r)` (`:14553`). So a reversed `(*SKIP)` makes `$` true wherever the verb last fired. The 2026-09-11 entry above recorded the same `slice_end`-against-`text_end` split between upstream's fast and slow paths without noticing that every sibling predicate agrees with `text_end` and only `$` does not; S35 had already made every assertion here read the text bound. **`(?w)` is therefore a control as well as a fact**, because it compiles `$` to `END_OF_LINE_U` (`regex/_regex_core.py:506-510`) - but it is not a clean swap of one bound for another, and the reason it works on one row and not the other is narrower than it looks. `(?w)` moves the line ends on BOTH rows and in both directions: on 24224 `$` is true at 3 and 10 where `(?w)$` is true at 2, 8 and 10; on 38101 `$` is true at 2 and 10 where `(?w)$` is true at 1, 7 and 10. What decides it is whether it moves THE PHANTOM POSITION - 8 becomes a `(?w)` line end on 24224, so a `(?w)` run there could not tell a bound that stopped being read from a line end that started existing, while on 38101 the phantom end 5 is a line end under neither spelling and `(?w)` removes the match outright. New entry `end-of-line-reads-a-skip-moved-slice` for seed 20260915 rows 24224 (a reversed `split`) and 38101 (a reversed `subf`); ledger entry 5 gains it as the line to put at the top of that report.
- 2026-09-15 (S52, sitting 3): **a stepwise walk is not a control for a row whose pattern reads the end of the subject, because moving `endpos` sets the very bound the defect leaves stale.** On both rows above, `search(subject, 0, <the phantom end>)` reproduces the phantom span on the VERB-FREE pattern too - so a walk would have said upstream was right. What tests it instead is spelling `$` out as `(?:(?=\n)|(?!\n|.))`, which no slice bound can answer, and upstream then gives this port's answer on both. The recorder's `_reads_the_end_of_the_subject` refusal already encodes this and is why neither row carries an `anchoredScan`; the sitting-2 notes read that refusal as a gap and it is not one.
- 2026-09-15 (S52, sitting 3): **upstream's partial call can return a match that is not partial, where its own non-partial call returns `None`** - seed 7 rows 24018 and 24737, both added to `partial-retry-carried-slice-forward`. `partial=True` is documented to ALSO allow a partial match, so it cannot answer a complete one the same engine denies without it, and the tell needs no model of the two-pass restore at all. Row 24018 is a `match`, which is ONE attempt: there is no next attempt for `(*SKIP)` to move the start of, so within it `(*SKIP)` must prune exactly what `(*PRUNE)` prunes, which closes the "but a `(*SKIP)` is allowed to differ on a scan" objection for the whole family. The new tell is NOT safe as a predicate - it is also what `bestmatch-loses-a-partial` looks like from the other side - so the entry stays keyed on rows.

The walk is sound on these two for reasons that do not generalise - `must_advance` is set only after a ZERO-WIDTH match (`:20932`; every `_regex.c` line number in these four entries is against the 2026.9.10 pin and was re-read out of the file, where older text in this repo cites `:14545`/`:14551` for the two `RE_OP_SKIP` writes and `:20903` for the scanner's overlapped step - today a `TRACE` call, a blank line and a comment terminator, the correct ones being `:14553`, `:14555` and `:20927-20928`. **A repo-wide reconciliation is owed**: both spellings are in tree and the stale one reaches source comments, probes, done slice notes and this file. A sweep cannot follow the count - the correct spelling is the majority for the two `RE_OP_SKIP` writes (`:14553` 45 uses against `:14545`'s, `:14555` 33 against `:14551`'s 19) and the MINORITY for the scanner step (`:20903` 22 against `:20927`'s 7). `do_match`'s two-pass block is cited as `:18160` in twelve places and the `text_pos` save is at `:18159`) and row 25854 draws none, and row 38151's lookahead sits where both questions read the subject identically - so the walk is computed in the probe and the entry (`skip-carried-slice-on-a-scan-with-no-walk`) is keyed on the two rows. Widening the recorder instead would write a walk that is wrong the moment a row draws a zero-width match, which is the row S34's blind review built.
- 2026-09-15 (S52, sitting 4): **the long-subject generators are a WRAPPER over the base generators, not four new grammars, and the filler pads the side the pattern does not run off.** `literals-long`, `quantifiers-long`, `partial-long` and `fuzzy-long` take the base generator's pattern unchanged and pad its subject to 1,000-20,000 characters, so a long row and a short row differ in the one variable under test; four bespoke long grammars would have made every divergence a question about which grammar found it. Forward patterns pad LEFT and `(?r)` ones pad RIGHT, because a reversed pattern runs out of text at the left end - pad the wrong side and a `partial-long` row is `literals` with a long prefix rather than a partial match over a long text. The operation is forced to `search`: every path the variant exists to reach is a scan, `match`/`fullmatch` are anchored and reach nothing new on a padded subject, and it bounds the cost of a row whose base generator cycles ALL_OPERATIONS over 20,000 characters against the 10s row timeout.
- 2026-09-15 (S52, sitting 4): **`quantifiers-long` is judged on match LENGTH where the other three are judged on match DISTANCE, and its filler comes from the base alphabet for that reason.** Measured first, by `tools/probes/generator-long-subject-reach.py`: with unmatchable filler it reached nothing - 9 of 148 matches began 100+ characters into the scan, median distance 0, median length 1 - and no spelling of the filler alphabet would have fixed it, because a nullable quantifier (`a*`, `.{0,2}`) answers a zero-width match at offset 0 without reading the text at all. The path that generator exists for is the repeat guard, which is reached by ITERATIONS rather than by a distant start, and a repeat cannot iterate over text it cannot consume. Drawing its filler from `ALPHABETS[0]` takes it from 0 to 32 of 155 matches running 100+ characters. The probe reports both columns so each generator is read on the one its path lives in.

Adding a red generator to the default wave would turn the gate every other slice depends on red for a reason unrelated to that slice. Sitting 3's `end-of-line-reads-a-skip-moved-slice` is the obvious hypothesis and no more than that: neither pattern holds a `(*SKIP)`, so whatever moves the bound here is something else, and sitting 3 also proved a stepwise walk is not a control for a row that reads the end of its subject.

Measured by `tools/probes/port-long-subject-cost.ps1` and `tools/probes/upstream-long-subject-cost.py`, which time each engine at truncated subject lengths: the two are in the SAME complexity class - both quadratic on row 307, upstream 4/15/53/217/861 ms at n=800..12800 against the port's 32/120/369/1,304/5,214 in Release - and the port is a constant factor slower, 5-8x in Release and up to 75x in Debug. The control is `pwsh -File tools/run-oracle.ps1 -SkipRecord -Configuration Release`, which consumes the IDENTICAL recorded rows and gives `diverge 0 of 600` against Debug's `diverge 2 of 600`, so the build is the only variable. No `ExpectedDivergences` entry: keying one on a row the port answers correctly would be wrong, and keying one on a wall-clock measurement would put a flaky test in the file whose whole value is that `Every_expected_divergence_still_diverges` is strict.

- 2026-09-15 **Correctness beyond agreement (owner questions; spec amendment 25; S52c authored).** Asked how the port knows its pinned behaviour is right, and how an upstream bug would be spotted when the oracle's reference is upstream itself. Answer recorded: four legs - upstream's own suite verbatim, the differential oracle, the four-limb standard for overruling upstream, and controls/mutation - with two gaps closed here. (1) Gap tests could pin the port's own output as expectation: the skill now requires provenance on every gap-test expected value, the verifier samples them, and S57 audits the pre-rule gap tests against upstream. (2) The oracle is blind to inherited bugs: S52c adds metamorphic invariants evaluated on upstream's own answers and on the port's for every wave row, making the self-contradiction checks that found entries 5, 11 and 13 automatic. The `tre` PyPI binding does not build on Windows (measured), so the fuzzy second engine stays an open item inside S52c. Also moved the driver's deadline and message files from `.scratch/` to `.claude/driver/` (gitignored) after S52's session cleared `.scratch/` mid-sitting and the heartbeat lost its countdown.

They do nothing of the kind: the row's own `(?<!(?:a|\p{ASCII})+)` reads ASCII-ness, and `h` and `i` are ASCII where `ı` is not, so the swap changed the question. Four non-ASCII, single-fold letters with no `T` row - U+00F1, U+01E7, U+0125, U+00E5 - all AGREE between the engines, and only the dotless i diverges. - 2026-09-15 (S52, sitting 9): **upstream's case expansion of a character SET turns on the set having a SECOND MEMBER, which is what makes gate row 88716 a Turkic row rather than a set-folding bug.** Under IGNORECASE|FULLCASE `\p{ASCII}` and `[\p{ASCII}]` reach none of U+0131, U+0130, U+212A or U+017F, while `[\p{ASCII}\p{ASCII}]` - the same member twice, so exactly the same characters - reaches all four, as do `[\p{ASCII}z]` and `[a\p{ASCII}]`; `[ab]` reaches none, so it is the PROPERTY's members expanding rather than sets in general. This port shares the expansion and the one-member exception and refuses only the two `T` rows: over the 42-cell grid of six characters against seven spellings, 36 cells agree and the 6 that diverge are U+0131 and U+0130 against the three multi-member spellings. A first draft said "a set is expanded by its members' case partners"; `[\p{ASCII}]` refutes that, and the blind review's delta pass is what caught it.
